    MODELLING THE MASS AND ENERGY

 BALANCE OF ALUMINIUM REDUCTION CELLS




            Vanderlei Gusberti





A thesis submitted in fulfilment of the requirements for the degree of
            Doctor of Philosophy

         School of Chemical Engineering

           University of New South Wales

               Sydney, Australia


             May 2014

## PDF page 2

PLEASE TYPE
                                 THE UNIVERSITY OF NEW SOUTH WALES
                                                      Thesis/Dissertation Sheet
Surname or Family name: Gusberti

                                                                   Other namels:First name: Vanderlei
Abbreviation for degree as given in the University calendar: PhD
School: School of Chemical Engineering                       Faculty: Faculty of Engineering
Title: Modelling the mass and energy balance of aluminium reduction cells


                                         Abstract 350 words maximum: (PLEASE TYPE)

The primary aluminium industry is continuously aiming to reduce the energy consumption of the smelting process
and the harmful smelter emission rates. Both aspects can be optimised with a comprehensive understanding of the
aluminium electrolysis cell mass and energy balance. Traditionally, the existing cell heat balance models present
very limited material balance. They rely on curve fitted voltage data, obtained decades ago when the cell technology
and operation was totally different from current cells.
The electrolysis cell mass and energy balance model proposed in this thesis is entirely based on the first law of
thermodynamics. This approach is valid for any type of cell, for both steady state and transient calculations. A new
extended cell control volume is adopted, allowing for a more complete material balance. The model is composed of
the main algorithm that uses input information obtained from subsidiary 3D sub-models. A new software tool was
developed, which could be used on site by process engineers to improve the smelting process.
Important differences between the classical models results and the new mass and energy balance model were
observed. The impact of each mass stream on the cell energy balance was studied and quantified.
 In transient simulations, a moving grid Finite Volume algorithm was specially developed to track the ledge movement.
The dynamic model was applied to study the impact of mass and energy imbalances on the Haii-Heroult cell
behaviour such as: intermittent aluminium fluoride additions and several power modulation functions. In the model,
the voltage compensation approach to mitigate the effects of power modulation perturbations was tested, presenting
excellent effectiveness. In addition, the shell forced air cooling was also simulated, showing negligible contribution to
the improvement of the power modulation window.
The dynamic model results were compared with measurements obtained from large scale industrial cells. In general,
the modelling results presented good correlation with measurements. The effect of irregular aluminium fluoride mass
 addition on the cell behaviour was studied, as well as the electrical energy input fluctuations. The dynamic model was
also applied to investigate the cell lining failure related to transient energy imbalances.

## PDF page 6

ABSTRACT

      The primary aluminium industry is continuously aiming to reduce the energy

consumption of the smelting process and the harmful smelter emission rates. Both

aspects can be optimised with a comprehensive understanding of the aluminium

electrolysis cell mass and energy balance. Traditionally, the existing cell heat balance

models present very limited material balance. They rely on curve fitted voltage data,

obtained decades ago when the cell technology and operation were totally different from

current cells.

      The electrolysis cell mass and energy balance model proposed in this thesis is

entirely based on the first law of thermodynamics. This approach is valid for any type of

cell, for both steady state and transient calculations. A new extended cell control

volume  is adopted, allowing for a more complete material balance. The model  is

composed of the main algorithm that uses input information obtained from subsidiary

3D sub-models. A new software tool was developed, which could be used on site by

process engineers to improve the smelting process.

       Important differences between the classical models results and the new mass and

energy balance model were observed. The impact of each mass stream on the cell

energy balance was studied and quantified.

       In transient simulations, a moving grid Finite Volume algorithm was specially

developed to track the ledge movement. The dynamic model was applied to study the

impact of mass and energy imbalances on the Hall-Héroult cell behaviour such as:

intermittent aluminium fluoride additions and several power modulation functions. In

the model,  the voltage compensation approach  to  mitigate  the  effects of power

modulation perturbations was tested, presenting excellent effectiveness. In addition, the

shell forced air cooling was also simulated, showing negligible contribution to the

improvement of the power modulation window.

      The dynamic model results were compared with measurements obtained from

large scale industrial cells. In general, the modelling results presented good correlation

with measurements. The effect of irregular aluminium fluoride mass addition on the cell

behaviour was studied, as well as the electrical energy input fluctuations. The dynamic

model was also applied to investigate the cell lining failure related to transient energy

imbalances.

AKNOWLEDGEMENTS


         I would like to thank the Caete Engenharia directors: Dagoberto Schubert Severo

and Lenira Klein Guimarães, for the technical and financial support for this PhD

project. I also wish to thank all my engineering staff colleagues, who have contributed

with many productive discussions during the course of this research.

       Special thanks to Emeritus Professor Maria Skyllas-Kazacos and Emeritus

Professor Barry Welch. It was a great privilege, to be supervised by these experienced

researchers. Their wise guidance was fundamental for proceeding with this work with

confidence.

         I am also deeply grateful to my wife Tomoe for the support, encouragement and

understanding shown during all steps of this journey.

        Finally, I dedicate this work to my daughter Kazumi Aurora, born during the

thesis writing period.

                                              XXV

## PDF page 31

                                                          1 - Introduction



     1  INTRODUCTION


      The Hall-Héroult electrolysis process has been used since the beginning of

industrial-scale aluminium production. An immense amount of electrical energy and

raw materials are consumed by aluminium smelters around the globe to produce the

aluminium via this process. The electrolysis takes place inside a device called the

―electrolysis cell‖ or ―reduction cell‖. In the smelters, many cells are connected in series

to form one or more cell lines called potlines.

      The electrolysis cell is a complex multiphysics device, and many physical and

chemical phenomena are relevant to the process. The electrowinning of aluminium

occurs inside the electrolyte layer, also called the ―bath‖. The electrolyte is a solution of

molten  cryolite (Na3AlF6) and other  additives. Aluminium deposits  at bath-metal

interface because the aluminium density is higher than the bath density. The electrolyte

is also responsible for the largest share of the electrolysis cell energy consumption.

      The cell cavity occupied by the liquids is surrounded by a frozen bath layer

(called ―ledge‖) which forms a biphasic solidification system. The ledge is a desirable

layer between the liquids and the cell refractory materials, providing protection to the

lining materials. Its thickness is variable depending on cell thermal conditions and on

the bath chemical composition. Thermal conditions are determined by the insulation

layers of the cell and the fluids movement produced by Lorentz forces and gas induced

forces. The bath composition determines the freezing temperature and thus the ledge

thickness, influencing also the insulation of the cell.

      These facts evidence the interdependence between bath chemistry, heat transfer,

magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) and gas induced flow of  electrolysis  cells. These

phenomena are also linked to the electrical field distribution. In addition, because the

alumina feeding is not continuous and the anodes must be changed in a certain time

interval, working in a real steady state condition is impossible. Many cell studies are

currently done in time-averaged basis while some cell processes can only be studied in

transient models.

      Modern  cells’ behaviour  is a  result of a wide range of parameters. Some

parameters are defined by the cell project (cell thermal insulation, maximum electrical

current  capacity,  magneto-hydrodynamic  condition),  while  other  parameters  are

                                                                      1

## PDF page 32

                                                          1 - Introduction


controlled automatically by a computerized system (cell voltage drop, alumina feeding)

and there are also parameters dependent on human interaction (anode changing, anode

covering, bath corrections, aluminium tapping). Optimum values have to be pursued by

plant operation. In the large scale production, any process small improvement can lead

to a considerable amount of energy saving.

     As electrolysis  cell behaviour  is essentially dynamic, the understanding of

transient effects of heat and mass imbalances  is a key factor to improve the cell

performance by reducing the variability of the cell states to a narrow band.

      1.1  Aluminium Reduction Cell Modelling


       In the past, mathematical models were developed attempting to describe the cell

thermal balance. Those models have helped the cell design to evolve in size, production

capacity and efficiency. Many simplifications and assumptions have been done in order

to produce results in a reasonable timeframe due to:

         -   Difficulties to have good measurements, many relevant phenomena are not

            fully understood and therefore, modelling sometimes is based on unproved

          hypotheses;

         -  The cell multiphysics is very complex, comprehensive mathematical models

          need many non-linear equations to be described and solved simultaneously.

          Numerical methods have to be robust. If the model is 3D, solutions may

           require unaffordable computational time. Even for steady state calculations,

          convergence might be difficult.

       Usually, specific aspects of cell behaviour have been separately studied using

mathematical modelling, such as: magnetic field calculation models (Blanc, 1980;

Ziegler and Kozarek, 1991; Schneider 2006), MHD models (Urata et al, 1976; Davidson

and Lindsay 1998; Droste et al., 1998; Antille et al, 1999; Gusberti, 2007), thermal-

electrical models (Bruggeman 1990; Dupuis 1998; Dupuis 2011;), bath-CO2 flow

models/bubbles induced flow (Severo et al. 2007; Severo and Gusberti, 2009), alumina

transport (Feng et al 2011). However, all these phenomena are interdependent and the

conclusions obtained by such models must be carefully interpreted.

      Nowadays, thermal equilibrium of modern cells is becoming very constrained

and difficult to be maintained because the electrolytic cells are being operated at their

                                                                      2

## PDF page 33

                                                          1 - Introduction


limits. Therefore, fine tuning of cell heat balance is very important and requires more

accurate cell modelling. There are gaps in the knowledge regarding the cell mass and

energy balance worthy of investigation. Heat losses associated with ―pacmaning‖

(cathode top  cleaning procedure) has been ignored by  existing  literature. Many

processes occurring above the crust have been also neglected.

      The  dynamics  of  the  cell  mass  and  energy  imbalances  require  more

understanding by the smelter operation and control system developers. The cell  is

always in a thermal transitory state. It is very difficult to predict by measurements in

which direction the cell energy imbalances are going and what will be the consequences

to the cell thermal equilibrium.  Therefore, it is a great challenge to determine which

action must be taken in order to maintain the cell within a minimum control band. In

view of these difficulties, the modelling of transient reduction cell behaviour would be

very helpful in increasing the cell energy efficiency by predicting and quantifying the

outcome of cell mass and energy disturbances.

      Another important aspect to be studied is the influence of the gas induced flow

inside the bath. In recent years (Cooksey and Yang, 2006; Severo and Gusberti, 2009),

bath flow  is being more studied in physical scale models and numerical models.

However, the resulting influence of different gas induced flows on the cell dynamic

behaviour has not yet been well understood or quantified.

      1.2  Research Objectives and Milestones


      The main objective of this research is to provide a tool for engineers to study cell

operation parameters, quickly performing a variety of virtual tests, obtaining relevant

answers regarding the cell operation. This modelling system aims to help the process

engineers to find the cell's optimum operation window, which is a combination of bath

chemistry, ACD (anode-cathode distance), bath superheat, voltage drop and cell current,

increasing the process efficiency.

       In order to obtain such a valuable tool, it is necessary to build up a modelling

system capable of using all the power of 3D sub-models in order to supply detailed

information to a global cell energy balance model.

       Figure 1-1 presents a flowchart of the proposed modelling structure. The 3D

electromagnetic model provides MHD forces for fluid flow calculation at the metal

                                                                      3

## PDF page 34

                                                          1 - Introduction


layer. In parallel, the gas induced flow is evaluated in the bath. Once the fluid flow is

obtained, wall heat transfer coefficients at the ledge, anode and cathode surfaces are

calculated. These coefficients are supplied to the thermal 3D sub-model and also to the

global cell balance model. The cell top sub-model determines how the cell duct flow

relates to the energy balance, thus influencing the 3D cell thermal and electrical sub-

model.

      The 3D sub-models supply the balance model with the wall thermal resistances

for the analysed cell design. Finally, the global cell balance model processes all the

inputs and determines the resulting cell state: bath composition, mass and heat balance

and ledge configuration. The resulting cell balance model calculates the cell averaged

steady states and, as well the dynamic behaviour of the cell function of either energy

imbalances or mass imbalances, or both simultaneously.

    Electromagnetic Model                          Cell Top CFD Model





 3D MHD Flow and Gas Induced       3D Electrical - Thermal Model

 Flow Models




                Global Cell Mass and Energy Balance Model
                    Substances concentrations;
                    Calculation of bath temperature;
                   Ledge movement;
                    Cell thermal dynamics.


                                       Figure 1-1: Models flowchart

      The main research objective can be divided in the following milestones:

            1.  Establish the equations of heat and mass balance as complete as possible

              using the extended control volume suggested by Welch and Keniry

              (2000). The traditional cell models found in the literature usually neglect

               the extended control volume.


                                                                      4

## PDF page 35

                                                          1 - Introduction


            2. Perform a 3D thermal modelling of the cell temperature and heat flux at

               sidewalls, endwalls and cell bottom in order to determine the equivalent

              thermal resistances of these regions.

            3.  Obtain the heat transfer coefficients ―h‖ at metal-ledge interface, bath-

              ledge  interface  and  cathode-metal  interface  using  the  modelling

             procedure developed by Severo and Gusberti (2009).

            4.  Construct the global cell mass and energy balance model in a Visual

             Basic code. The program will solve all heat and mass balance equations

              using the coefficients provided by the sub-models. At this stage, the

            model is steady state averaged.

            5. Determine the influence on the cell heat and mass balance of many of the

              usually neglected mass streams: the ―pacman‖ material collected from

               the cell during the cavity cleaning procedure; the energy carried by the

              cleaning device (due to temperature increase); carbon consumption not

             used in reduction reaction, alumina impurities and others.

            6. Study the influence of cell duct flow on the cell energy balance.  It is

              believed that optimising the cell duct flow is important to achieve better

                 cell energy efficiency.

            7. Expand the model to transient mass and energy balance simulation of the

                electrolysis cell. Using such a dynamic model, many aspects of the cell

              operation can be better understood, as they introduce energy imbalances.

            8. Apply all the previously listed steps into real industrial cells and compare

               the predicted results with measurements.

      Once the global balance model has been set up with information from 3D sub-

models and measurements, the user could easily modify operation parameters, such as:

line current, bath composition, cathode resistance, bath temperature, metal and bath

depth, ACD,  fluoride  additions,  voltage  setup and  a new  cell  state would be

recalculated.

      The global balance model could re-run many operational setups in order to

obtain an operational window chart for a given cell technology. The dynamic model

could also be used to identify how the cell deviates from the optimal window region

                                                                      5

## PDF page 36

                                                          1 - Introduction


when a thermal disturbance occurs. The Figure 1-2 shows a generic representation of

the cell’s operating window and its limits are defined and interpreted as follows:

         -  The red  lines of the graph (Figure 1-2) are the thermal limits of the

           operational window and  lines represent constant bath superheat values.

        Above the limit, the cell would lose the ledge protective layer at sidewalls

         and endwalls and the  cell lining would be destroyed. Below the limit,

           excessive bath freeze is formed and the cell operation would then become

           impossible. These thermal limits are predictable by good cell energy balance

            calculations. Usually good thermal behaviour is observed when the superheat

             is maintained between 5°C and 15°C.

         -  The ACD is represented by the blue line. Usually, ACD minimisation is

           desirable as the cell energy consumption is highly dependent on the ACD

         ohmic heat dissipation. However, there is a limit when the cell is prone to

            short-circuit (metal layer, which is the cathode, in contact the carbon anode)

            either by metal pad waves or by the occurrence of carbon spikes under the

          anode. The ACD limit is very difficult to predict and it is determined in real

             cell operation practice.





                               Figure 1-2: Voltage operational window diagram



                                                                      6

## PDF page 37

                                                          1 - Introduction


      Higher cell currents combined with low cell voltages are always desirable. It is

the situation where the cell energy efficiency is maximised because the aluminium

production rate is nearly proportional to the cell current, following the Faraday’s law

and, therefore, more aluminium is produced using less energy.

## PDF page 38

                                                      2 - Literature Review



     2  LITERATURE REVIEW


      2.1  Aluminium Smelting: Historical Background


      The chemical element aluminium is the most abundant metallic element in the

earth’s crust. Ironically, it was discovered in the nineteenth century, thousands of years

after other metallic elements  like copper, gold,  silver and  iron. The reason why

aluminium remained hidden from human society so long is that it forms highly stable

oxides, commonly combined with water and silicates.

      Soon after the discovery of aluminium, it became clear that this metal would be

extremely useful in the manufacturing industry,  for military or for peaceful use.

Initially, the challenge was to find an economically viable way to produce the metal. As

described by Grjotheim and Welch (1988), already in 1825 Oersted succeeded in

producing a few milligrams of aluminium metal using a process of thermal reduction of

aluminium chloride with potassium amalgam. Then, in 1854, the French St. Claire

Deville produced aluminium metal from thermal reduction of NaAlCl4. This time it was

possible to produce household  artefacts, toys and military helmets, causing much

interest due to its lightness. However, the costs of production remained extremely high.

       After a few years, some researchers realised that electrolysis would be the best

viable process to produce aluminium. Then, in 1886, Hall (United States) and Héroult

(France) independently developed the electrolytic process of reduction of alumina. At

that time electricity had achieved rapid development and it was now available on an

industrial scale. Another important factor was the development of the Bayer process for

purification of alumina from bauxite, enabling access to pure alumina in high quantities.

Thus the Hall-Héroult process for reduction of alumina by electrolysis was established

as the standard way to obtain metallic aluminium and is still widely used in industrial

plants. Figure 2-1 presents the original patent pictures of Hall (1889) and Heroult (1892)

aluminium cells, when the patents were awarded in the USA. Figure 2-2 and Figure 2-3

present two of the earliest electrolytic cell lines in operation in France (Figure 2-2) and

United States of America (Figure 2-3).





                                                                      8

## PDF page 39

                                                      2 - Literature Review





         Figure 2-1: Aluminium electrolysis cell sketches patented by Hall in 1889 (left) and Heroult in

         1892 (Right), source: Google Patents, US patent numbers (Hall: 400664, Heroult: 473118)

       In the last 125 years, the thermodynamics of the electrolysis process remained

unchanged. However, along the twentieth century, the electrolytic cell design and

operation has immensely evolved. As a result, the specific energy consumption that was

originally around 72 kWh/kg at 1890 (Peterson and Miller; 1986), dropped to 28

kWh/kg in ~1920 and nowadays there are smelters producing at an energy consumption

below 12.5 kWh/kg. Nevertheless, the worldwide average is still above the 15 kWh/kg

(Iffert, 2007) and this fact suggests that there  is room for improvements in many

smelters.





                                                                      9

## PDF page 40

                                                2 - Literature Review





   Figure 2-2: Aluminium electrolysis cell of the Heroult type in 1889. Source: Peterson and

                                        Miller (1986)





Figure 2-3: Aluminium electrolysis cell of the Hall type installed in 1914. Source: Peterson and

                                       Miller (1986)



                                                               10

## PDF page 41

                                                      2 - Literature Review


       Figure 2-4 and Figure 2-5 show modern aluminium electrolysis  cells with

somewhat different designs. Both are capable of running at ~350kA, producing a few

tons of aluminium per day at each cell.





             Figure 2-4: Modern Aluminium Electrolysis cell at Venalum. Source: Berrueta (2004)





        Figure 2-5: Modern Aluminium Electrolysis cell at Chalco. Source: Jiaming et al. (2009)

## PDF page 42

                                                      2 - Literature Review


      2.2  Present Scenario of Aluminium Smelting Production


      According to the International Aluminium  Institute (July 2013), aluminium

production has reached more than 47 million metric tonnes of aluminium per year.

Aluminium is currently produced in all regions of the globe, as presented in Figure 2-6,

where the annual production in each region is plotted.





          Figure 2-6: Regional distribution of primary aluminium production during the last year (Aug

                  2012 - Jul 2013). Source: http://www.world-aluminium.org/statistics/

      Aluminium production is dependent on an immense amount of energy in order

to purify the alumina from the bauxite ore and, more intensively, to separate the element

aluminium from the oxygen during the smelting process. More than 85% of the overall

necessary energy for the primary aluminium production industry chain is used only by

the aluminium smelting process (US Department of Energy, 2007). Moreover, the

standard smelting process applied in industry (the Hall-Héroult process) must use

electrical energy.

        Electrical energy is not a worldwide transportable commodity, it has a limited

distance range near the power plants where the energy can be distributed, accepting a

reasonable loss. Therefore, its availability and prices are very distinct depending on the


                                                                     12

## PDF page 43

                                                      2 - Literature Review


region of the world. Unfortunately, in many countries smelters must compete for energy

with domestic consumers and other industrial activities, raising the energy prices. This

makes the smelting activity not profitable in these regions and these smelters are forced

to shut down the cell lines, as energy usually responds for more than 30% of smelter

costs. As a consequence, energy is the most important and determining factor in the

location choice of new aluminium smelting plants.

       Despite the economic crisis of 2008-2009, primary aluminium production has

increased in last ten years as shown in Figure 2-7.





          Figure 2-7: Aluminium production evolution during the last 10 years. Regional breakdown.

                             Source: http://www.world-aluminium.org/statistics/

       China’s production increase during the reported period is remarkable. Growth is

also observed in Middle East countries (GCC). Most of other listed regions presented

nearly constant production, the exception being West Europe, where production has

steadily decreased due to electrical energy rising costs.





                                                                     13

## PDF page 44

                                                      2 - Literature Review


      2.3  Aluminium Smelting Fundamentals


        2.3.1   Electrolytic Reduction of Alumina

       In the Hall-Héroult process, aluminium is produced by an electrochemical aided

reduction of alumina with carbon. The schematic cut view of an electrolysis cell and its

important parts is illustrated in Figure 2-8.





                    Figure 2-8: Cut view of a typical electrolysis cell for alumina reduction

      The overall dominant reaction in modern cells is given by equation (2.1),  it

occurs inside the bath at around 960°C provided that the necessary E0 is established

between the electrodes.

          2 Al2O3 ( dissolved )  3C ( s )  3CO2 ( g )  4 Al (l )    E0  -1.18V      (2.1)

      The carbon oxidation involved in the process provides part of the energy

required to separate the strong Al-O bounds of alumina. Carbon is introduced into the

cell in the form of consumable anodes. Modern cells use a set of 16-40 prebake anodes.

When the carbon is almost depleted, new anodes are introduced into the cell one at a

time, in a regular interval.

      Alumina  is introduced into the  cell in small batches by a feeding system

connected to alumina silos. The system tries to automatically match the feeding rate

                                                                     14

## PDF page 45

                                                      2 - Literature Review


with the reaction needs as close as possible, optimising the bath composition for

production efficiency.

      The  Faraday’s law  of  electrolysis  governs  the  rate  of consumption and

production of each involved substance. It states that the rate of mass transformations is

proportional to the electrical current provided to the cell.

                          CE M                      w             I t                               (2.2)
                                 100 F  n

       Unfortunately, not all electric current passing through the bath is used to perform

the reaction of equation (2.1), making the Faraday’s current efficiency ―CE‖ to be lower

than 100%. Nowadays, the majority of smelters is running the electrolytic cells at 90-

95% current efficiency. The 5-10% CE losses occur via several mechanisms, which can

be grouped into two types:

         -  CE loss Type 1: The main classical CE loss type cited in the literature is the

           reoxidation reaction (see e. g. Grjotheim and Welch, 1988), which produces

          alumina back from metallic aluminium dissolved in the bath. The metal is

           transported  to  a CO2  rich  region  near  the anode  surface where  the

           reoxidation reaction takes place generating CO as stated in equation (2.3). In

            this case, the CE loss magnitude is dependent on the aluminium dissolution

            kinetics. The rate of dissolution follows two paths, both depending on the

          degree of turbulence present at the metal/bath interface:

               The mass transfer at interface, which is enhanced by turbulence;

                In high turbulence regions, metal droplets are captured and dragged

               by the  bath. The  droplets enhance the  dissolution  area,  further

                  increasing the dissolution rate.

                 2 Al (soln)  3CO2 ( g )  Al2O3 (soln)  3CO( g )               (2.3)

         -  CE loss Type 2: In the  last decades, smelters are squeezing interpolar

           distance (ACD) increasing the chance of short-circuiting some anodes. In

            this case, electrical current flows without promoting any reaction (Hyland,

          1984; Iffert, 2007). The direct contact produces ohmic heat and there are

           several mechanisms known:



                                                                     15

## PDF page 46

                                                      2 - Literature Review


               Sometimes when the ADC is sufficiently squeezed, part of the anode

               becomes permanently in contact with the metal pad. In this short-

                    circuiting region, the carbon anode is not consumed, resulting in a

                 protuberance commonly known as ―anode spike‖.

                In cells where magnetic instability is observed, the metal is moved by

                  the MHD forces, creating a characteristic metal pad wave also known

                  as ―metal pad roll‖. If the metal wave reaches a sufficient amplitude,

                           it  starts to touch the anode bottom surface, creating successive

                    transient short-circuits.

               Another mechanism is when a metal droplet comes in direct contact

                 with the anode and the metal is electrochemically oxidised. In this

                   case, two chemical reactions occur and the net effect is equivalent to

                 a direct short-circuit.

       In general, the aluminium cell control system tries to adjust the ACD in order to

avoid the CE loss Type 2 and accepting a certain degree of CE loss Type 1. However, it

is a recent trend in the industry to develop low voltage cells, which need to operate with

squeezed ACD (Thibeault et al., 2013; Naixiang et al., 2013). These cells are likely to

present anode spike more often, and as well, the metal pad roll. Predictions on the CE

loss are very difficult to be made because its kinetics is strongly dependent on the cell

design and the cell operation conditions.

        2.3.2  The Electrolyte

      The electrolyte layer, also called ―bath‖, is the core of the aluminium reduction

cell. It floats over the metal pad layer because the two liquids are immiscible and the

electrolyte presents 10-15% lower density. The main general function of the bath is to

provide a proper environment that favours the reaction of equation (2.1). Grjotheim and

Welch (1988) listed the most important properties that the bath should present:

         -   Appropriated freezing point;

         -  Good solubility of alumina;

         -   Correct density in order to guarantee separation from metal pad;

         -   Appropriated electrical conductivity;


                                                                     16

## PDF page 47

                                                      2 - Literature Review


         -  Low vapour pressure to minimise emissions;

         -  Thermodynamic stability with respect to the alumina concentration;

         -  Low tendency to dissolve electrode products: Al, CO2;

         -  Good anode wetting.

      The electrolyte used in industry is composed of more than 80% molten cryolite

(Na3AlF6) that is actually a double salt of aluminium fluoride and sodium fluoride
(3NaF•AlF3).  In liquid state the two salts dissociate functioning as the solvent for the

alumina. In reality, many ion species are present, but for simplicity in this thesis, the

equivalent concentrations of the species listed above will be used.

       In order to improve electrolyte properties, some additives are normally used:

The aluminium fluoride (AlF3) is the most important additive used. Its concentration is

kept 9-14% above the stoichiometric cryolite content. This excess of aluminium fluoride

lowers the bath freezing point, also called ―T liquidus‖, and  it lowers the metal

solubility into the bath (Odegard et al., 1987), thus improving the current efficiency.

The Figure 2-9 presents the NaF-AlF3 phase diagram, showing the drop in melting point
when AlF3 molar fraction is above the pure cryolite ratio (25%).





           Figure 2-9: The NaF-AlF3 schematic phase diagram. Typical modern cells operating range
                             presented between brackets. Source: Drengstig, 1997

      Because the calcium oxide  is always entering into the bath as an alumina

impurity, the bath commonly contains 4-7% calcium fluoride (CaF2), also called

fluorspar. It also lowers the melting point of the bath. In the same way, the alumina

                                                                     17

## PDF page 48

                                                      2 - Literature Review


content dissolved into the bath lowers the liquidus temperature. One important task of

the control is to maintain the bath composition as stable as possible near the operational

target. The Figure 2-10 presents the combined AlF3 and alumina concentration levels

influence on the bath liquidus temperature. Alumina concentration is also responsible

for lowering the liquidus temperature. The cell control system tries to maintain the

alumina concentration between 2.5% and 3% as presented at the smelter target point.





         Figure 2-10: Impact of excess AlF3 levels on the bath liquidus temperature for a range of Al2O3
         concentrations (CaF2 is 5.6 wt.% for all lines). The point shows the average conditions pursued
                     by state-of-the-art reduction cells. Source: Coursol et al., 2012

     Some smelters have invested in adding other additives such as LiF and MgF2

aiming at the improvement of bath properties, further lowering the liquidus temperature,

metal solubility or bath resistivity. However, these additives did not become standard

for the majority of modern cells because of side-effect problems or too high cost.

      Bath properties such as density,  electrical conductivity and  viscosity were

obtained from experiments and are available  in publications (Wang  et  al. 1994;

Chrenkova  et  al. 1996), as functions of the bath composition. The bath liquidus

temperature for the Na3AlF6-AlF3-CaF2-Al2O3-LiF-MgF2-KF system was meticulously

studied by Solheim et al. (1996) for a wide range of compositions and it is a determinant

                                                                     18

## PDF page 49

                                                      2 - Literature Review


parameter for the location of the freeze interface. The liquidus measurements were fitted

to a mathematical  expression,  see  equation  (2.4), dependent on  the  substances’

concentrations.  It  is a very useful compilation  that can be inserted in modelling

calculations and cell control systems.

                                                                   2.2                %CaF2 TL  1011  0.50  % AlF3   0.13  % AlF3    3.45                0.124  %CaF2  % AlF3                                        1  0.0173  %CaF2 
                                                  1.5                           7.93  % Al2 O3                         (2.4)0.00542   %CaF2  % AlF3                                                           2
                           1  0.0936  % Al2 O3   0.0017  % Al2 O3   0.0023  % Al2 O3  % AlF3 
             8.90  % LiF                                                    2  3.95  % MgF2   3.95  % KF 
  1  0.0047  % LiF   0.0010  % AlF3 

      The  obtained  liquidus  temperature  (TL)  is  expressed  in  [°C],  while  the

concentrations are provided in weight percent. The above equation is valid for ranges:

%AlF3 < 20%, %CaF2 < 20%, %LiF < 20%, %MgF2 < 5%, %KF < 5%, and Al2O3

below the saturation. There are other similar equations provided in the literature, for

example: Dewing (1970), Lee et al. (1984), Peterson and Teberaux (1987), Grjiotheim

and Welch (1988), Rostum et al. (1990), and Kolas (2007).

      The electrolyte is so corrosive that the only practical material found suitable to

protect the cell walls was the solid cryolite itself. It works as a protective layer called

―ledge‖, and  it can meltdown  or  refreeze according  to  cell thermal  conditions.

Therefore, the  cell heat balance  is truly a concern and many research  efforts  in

modelling have been done in order to study the cell thermal behaviour along decades.

Section 2.4 presents a brief review of the most relevant works in this field.

       Taylor and Welch (2004)  studied  the  operational  difficulties  in  the bath

chemistry composition control. They plotted the cell behaviour on a bath temperature

versus AlF3 concentration graph to locate the target region where cells with good bath

chemistry control should navigate, see Figure 2-11. It has been noted, however, that

most studied cells passed half of time outside the good operational region. The reasons

pointed for the cell excursions outside the desired bath chemistry window were:

         -   Difficulty to obtain reliable measurements;

         -   Lack of understanding of the process to support the correct actions;

         -  Compensatory actions with a short-term focus only, not considering the long

           timescales of the cell response;


                                                                     19

## PDF page 50

                                                      2 - Literature Review
