          Figure 2-11: Optimal bath chemistry operational window represented by the green polygon,

                               %CaF2=5%, %Al2O3=3%

       In the temperature/AlF3 window of Figure 2-11, the bath superheat should be
maintained between 5°C and 15°C combined with AlF3 concentration of 9-14%. If the

bath temperature is too low, there is a high probability of sludge freezing over the

cathode disturbing the electrical current path. If the AlF3 content is too low, the current

efficiency is compromised as metal solubility increases and the reoxidation reaction rate

also increases (Grjotheim and Welch, 1988).

        2.3.3  Cell Thermal Behaviour

      Energy must be supplied  to  the  cell  in order  to perform  the aluminium

production and as well, to provide the conditions necessary for the process. Rodnov et

al. (2003) divided the cell energy use in three categories:

         -   Provide the necessary energy for alumina reduction, since the reaction is

          endothermic.

         -  Energy to create the conditions for the electrolysis to take place in the cell:

          Heat up the reactants, additives and impurities, and to perform parasite

           reactions of additives;

                                                                     20

## PDF page 51

                                                      2 - Literature Review


         -  A share of energy to be dissipated by the cell to the ambient air. It should

          maintain the reaction temperature in the bath and also to keep the protective

          ledge thickness.

     When designing a cell, it is very important to accurately predict the fraction of

cell energy used in each one of these cell processes. The quantity of energy to be lost by

the cell dissipation must be known before starting the cell insulation project. The cell

design should allow a certain controlled heat loss level to the potroom, not too high

unnecessarily wasting energy, and not too low, allowing a sufficient temperature

gradient inside the cell that is responsible for maintaining a stable freeze thickness.

      Energy sources and sinks inside the cell have been exhaustively studied using

voltage equations (Haupin, 1998; 2000) compiled from many experimental works done

decades ago (see e. g. Beck, 1959; Thonstad and Hove, 1964; Thonstad and Rolseth

1978). At that time, cell operation was different from modern cells standards. Alumina

was the only cover material used and there was no ―pacmaning‖ procedure. It was also

assumed that the current efficiency was lost only by the reverse reaction (equation (2.3)

), but in modern cells ACD is small and a great part of the current efficiency loss occurs

by short-circuiting the anodes with the metal. This also changes the heat balance

calculations because the energy source produced by the short-circuit is purely ohmic,

while in the reverse reaction energy is provided from the enthalpy balance between the

materials involved. Haupin’s equations also neglect many substances in the cell mass

balance, thus underestimating the heat necessary to heat up  all materials such as

aluminium  fluoride, alumina impurities, carbon excesses, anode cover and anode

assembly parts.

     Much of the cell thermal modelling literature (see section 2.4) rely on Haupin

compilation studies without considering its limitations in steady state models and, more

important, in transient models. Haupin and Kvande (2000) themselves even alerted the

readers specifically about those limitations. As a consequence, the modelling based on

these methods may be predicting cell thermal results outside the real cell operational

window. In fact, if a good cell mass balance is established and the total cell energy input

is known, the voltage equations are dispensable together with  all the uncertainties

present in their curve fitted data. Cell voltage equations may remain useful if ACD

prediction is a requirement, but there is no necessity in interfering in the cell heat and

material balance.

                                                                     21

## PDF page 52

                                                      2 - Literature Review


      The total heat dissipation of the cell can be obtained from the power input minus

the enthalpy balance of all substances involved in the process. The heat dissipation of

the cell to the environment can be divided into two zones (Bruggeman, 1998; Kiss and

Dassylva-Raymond, 2008) with respect to the heat flow ―driving force‖ as presented in

Figure 2-12.

      At the zone 1, heat passes through the freeze layer, cell insulation and then

reaches the environment. The heat flux is directly proportional to the bath superheat,

which is desired to be normally around 5-15°C. The freeze shape is active, varying its

thickness, and consequently, its thermal resistance according to the T liquidus.

      At the zone 2, heat passes through the cell top and bottom, which are fixed

thermal resistances. Heat flux is proportional to the total temperature difference between

the bath and the potroom. This delta of temperatures is typically around 930°C.





                      Figure 2-12: Schematic division of the heat losses zones of the cell

      The different zones present very distinct response to variations in the bath

temperature. If, for some reason, the bath temperature should suddenly increase by 5°C,

then  the  heat  loss by zone 1 would be up  to 50%  higher  with some  ledge

accommodation. However, at zone 2, changes in heat output would be negligible (less

than 1%) because the delta T of 5°C is a very small increase compared with 930°C.


                                                                     22

## PDF page 53

                                                      2 - Literature Review


      The zone 1 thermal stability is very susceptible to cell power input variations

because the other heat dissipation to ambient (zone 2) and heat sinks (enthalpy balance)

are relatively constant during cell operation. However, high fluctuations in heat flux at

zone 1 should not be permitted because a minimum ledge thickness must be maintained.

     As a consequence, the cell insulation lining design at the sidewalls is very

important for obtaining a thermally stable and energy efficient cell. Numerical models

have been used, as described by Dupuis (2002), to help the development of new cell

linings in the last decades. As the cell amperage and size increase, the thermal design

needs to become tighter. This is reflected in the needs of numerical models accuracy,

where the assumptions must be wisely chosen.

        2.3.4  Cell Ledge Dynamics

     As presented earlier, changes in the bath chemistry composition modify the bath

melting temperature (Figure 2-10). This implies that the ledge solidification front

position is dependent on the actual bath concentrations of additives: AlF3, CaF2 and

alumina. While the calcium fluoride and alumina concentrations are nearly constant in

time, the aluminium fluoride concentration varies because it is gradually consumed to

neutralise Na2O, CaO and because fluoride compounds escape from the cell in gaseous

form.

       Eventually AlF3 must be added by cell operation in order equilibrate the AlF3

inventory and to bring the bath composition back to the desired targets. This correction

in the bath composition will cause a cascade of events in the cell thermal behaviour that

may take hours or even days to stabilise. In a first moment, as AlF3 concentration is

increased, the melting temperature of bath decreases and a certain amount of ledge starts

to melt down. This reduces the thermal resistance of sidewall increasing heat losses.

After some time the bath temperature also decreases. Because the ledge presents

cryolite composition (3NaF•AlF3), when it melts the AlF3 concentration lowers a little

bit again and the effect of the original addition is reduced. At some point, the new

equilibrium state is achieved, but only if no other disturbance in the cell occurs during

this process, which is rarely the case. The Figure 2-13 shows a diagram explaining the

above self-interacting ledge dynamics mechanism for the AlF3 addition.




                                                                     23

## PDF page 54

                                                      2 - Literature Review





                 Figure 2-13: Ledge dynamics, interactions for AlF3 addition (SH=superheat)

    A similar process occurs if the disturbance is caused by, for example, an increase

in voltage set point. More heat is generated and the superheat increases. The cell starts

to dissipate more heat, melting part of the freeze. Ledge thickness decreases, then AlF3

concentration lowers, bath melting temperature (T liquidus) increases and some freeze

forms back until a new equilibrium state is achieved. The impact of some different types

of energy imbalances in the cell on resulting AlF3 concentration was also discussed by

Welch (1998).

      Sometimes a reduction in AlF3 concentration in the bath is necessary. The soda
ash (Na2CO3) addition neutralises part of the aluminium fluoride, forming cryolite. Now
the cascade of events follows the AlF3 addition pattern, but with opposite effects. In a
first moment, AlF3 concentration decreases being neutralised by the soda ash, the

melting temperature of bath increases and a certain amount of ledge starts to freeze. The

thermal resistance of sidewall increases, reducing the heat loss through the sidewall. In

consequence, the bath temperature will increase after a certain time. The new frozen

ledge presents cryolite composition (3NaF•AlF3) slightly increasing back the AlF3

concentration in the remaining bath. This cycle continues until a new equilibrium is

achieved, considering that no other disturbance in the cell occurs during this process,

which is unlikely to happen. The Figure 2-14 shows a diagram explaining the above

self-interacting ledge dynamics mechanism for the soda ash addition.



                                                                     24

## PDF page 55

                                                      2 - Literature Review





                Figure 2-14: Ledge dynamics, interactions for Na2CO3 addition (SH=superheat)

    A similar process is triggered if the voltage set point decreases. Less heat is

generated at the ACD and the superheat is reduced repeating the same cycle of the soda

ash addition.

      The aluminium fluoride addition criteria and control has been a source of debate

in the literature. The standard procedure in the industry now is to calculate the quantity

of fluoride of each addition function of the measured bath temperature, which can cause

strong disturbances in the superheat and ledge meltdown. In those smelters, the cell

control tries to adjust the bath temperature by AlF3 overfeeding or underfeeding. Such

exaggerated  additions  can  introduce more  cell dynamic  disturbances  instead  of

minimising them. Researchers tried to improve the industrial bath chemistry control:

Entner (1992; 1993; 1995) developed a systematic calculation procedure of the fluoride

addition mass based on linear regression of %AlF3, cell voltage and bath temperature

measurements. Some authors (Hyland et al., 2001; Drengstig, 1997) indicate a more

intelligent addition procedure, taking into account the mass balance of AlF3 during the

cell operation. Kolas and Stoere (2009) published a novel control system where they

combine AlF3 mass balance and energy balance considerations to decide the addition

mass.

       Quantifying the cell ledge dynamics experimentally is very difficult, although

some studies have been done (Taylor et al., 1983; Rye et al., 1999; Iffert, 2007). Tandon

et al. (2007) studied and recommended actions for minimising the cell thermal state

                                                                     25

## PDF page 56

                                                      2 - Literature Review


variations identifying its causes via experimental investigation and analysis of the root

cause of  the  cell imbalances  cycle. Because  of  the long  timescale,  several  cell

disturbances are superimposed such as: corrective additions, voltage modifying, anode

changing and metal tapping. Despite the cited advances in cell dynamics understanding,

it is extremely difficult to isolate the consequences of a specific input experimentally in

a long timescale.

      Mathematical modelling the cell response to disturbances is an option to study

and acquire a better understanding of the cell dynamic processes and to predict the ledge

dynamics. The objective of the dynamic modelling studies is to predict the extent of

disturbances and to develop optimised countermeasures, reducing the  cell thermal

excursions.

     Some mathematical models have been developed aiming to study the ledge

movement according to cell disturbances: Taylor (1984) used a one dimensional finite

difference model; Chen (1997) followed the same approach including the sidewall

materials in the calculation. The challenge of the dynamic modelling during the last

decades has been to combine the cell mass and energy balance with the ledge movement

associated with the cell control actions (Tikaz, 1994; Rodnov, 2003; Piskazhova and

Mann, 2006).

        2.3.5  Cell Operation in Modern Smelters

      There are two measures of the process efficiency universally adopted by the

smelters and both are dependent on cell design and good cell operation:

         -  The cell current efficiency. As explained in the section 2.3.1, the current

           efficiency (CE) is the % of the produced metal mass compared with the

        maximum possible production given by Faraday’s law.

         -  The specific energy consumption in kilowatts-hour per kilogram of produced

         aluminium [kWh/kg Al]. This measure is becoming more important as the

          energy costs are raising. There is also pressure to save energy because of

          environmental issues involved in energy generation. It is a more complete

         measure since the CE  is included as one of the factors influencing the

            specific energy consumption calculation.



                                                                     26

## PDF page 57

                                                      2 - Literature Review


      According to Welch and Al Zarouni (2010), the performance of aluminium

smelting cells is dependent on:

         -   Cell design, including eventual design modifications and modernisation;

         -   Operating parameters, on which the cell control is based;

         -  The quality of work practices, which is a human factor. Good practices aim

            to reduce the cell parameters’ deviations from the targets;

         -  Good quality of raw materials.

      Determining the optimum setup of cell operation is a great challenge because it

involves fine tuning of many parameters. Biedler (2003) discussed the main guidelines

in cell operation for obtaining high energy efficiency: maximise current efficiency,

minimise the number and duration of anode  effects, maximise the  cell  life, and

minimise the carbon consumption.

      The  actions commonly taken  in modern  smelters  to improve  the energy

efficiency are generally focused on lowering the cell voltage. They might include

(Welch and Al Zarouni, 2010):

         -  Changing  the  cathode  blocks  material  to  achieve  superior  electrical

           conductivity,

         -   Reduction of cathodic voltage by improving  collector bars shape and

           materials;

         -   Reduction  of anode  voltage drop by modifying  the anode  stub/yoke

          geometry or material and anode to carbon contact resistance;

         -   Reduction of external voltage drop by increasing busbar cross sectional area.

         -   Increase the anodes cross sectional area in order to lower the carbon voltage

           drop;

         -   Introduction of slots in the anodes. The slots promote an improvement in gas

           collection from the bath, reducing bubble voltage drop;

         -   Reduction of ACD to the minimum acceptable rage manageable by the cell

           operation;

      The current efficiency can be optimised by adopting the following guidelines:


                                                                     27

## PDF page 58

                                                      2 - Literature Review


         -  Minimising  the chance  of metal  shorting, which  requires good ACD

         management, reduction of MHD instabilities, reduction of carbon dust that

        may trigger the anode spike formation;

         -  The anode slots reduce the amount of CO2 in the ACD, reducing the chance

           of the re-oxidation reaction;

      Reducing anode effects frequency and duration is a great challenge for modern

smelters. During anode effects, the environmental harmful PFC (Perfluorocarbons)

gases are generated together with a giant voltage increase and abnormal energy input to

the cell. Anode effect occurs when alumina concentration reaches a low critical value.

Therefore, alumina concentration control within an operational band is necessary to

prevent anode  effects  and, on  the  other  side,  to avoid alumina  saturation and

precipitation over the cathode. Efforts to study the anode effect formation causes and its

early detection have been conducted by a few researchers (Al Zarouni 2011; Al Zarouni

et al., 2011).

       Cell voltage and alumina concentration are controlled by a computerized system.

The cell voltage is maintained near the target by automatically adjusting the interpolar

distance (ACD).  Eventually  the  target  has  to be changed  if  the  cell becomes

magnetically unstable.

       Determination of alumina concentration in the cell is complicated and must be

done indirectly by the control system monitoring cell voltage, cell current and ACD.

The basic principle applied is that when alumina concentration drops, as it is consumed

inside the bath, the cell voltage rises and new alumina is introduced into the cell by the

feeders. The ACD has also to be determined indirectly from cell voltage readings,

making the task more difficult. The anodes must be continuously moved to compensate

their consumption together with metal level variations.

       In most  industrial  cells,  the  control system  calculates  the  cell  ―pseudo-

resistance‖ (the word ―pseudo‖ is because some cell voltage components are not ohmic

resistances) that is known to have a behaviour as described in Figure 2-15, function of

alumina concentration and ACD. There are many control systems strategies applied to

find the optimum alumina concentration point and to decide the alumina feeding rate

associated with anode movement, depending on the cell technology. Typically, they



                                                                     28

## PDF page 59

                                                      2 - Literature Review


alternate alumina overfeeding and underfeeding cycles  in an attempt  to find the

minimum pseudo-resistance and to estimate the current ACD condition (Bearne, 1998).

       Additionally, the cell voltage can be modified by some other variable in the

process (bath temperature, electrolyte composition, sludge on cathode top, etc.) causing

errors in control systems’ decisions. Many efforts are being made by control system

builders trying to incorporate as many process variables as possible.





          Figure 2-15: Cell pseudo-resistance function of alumina concentration and ACD, reproduced

                                  from Paterson and Miller (1986)

      There are tasks of cell control that are still being done manually, such as: anode

changing, anode cover replacement and bath composition measurements. Combining

control system with human work practices in a successful way has been an immense

source of concern for process engineers.

          It is a general understanding that a good cell control is achieved maintaining the

variations of cell key parameters as low as possible. A list of cell parameters to be

controlled by cell operation was given by Bearne (1999), it includes:

         -  Bath chemistry (AlF3, alumina, CaF2 and other additives composition);

         -  Bath temperature and superheat that determines heat losses and ledge shape;


                                                                     29

## PDF page 60

                                                      2 - Literature Review


         -  Anode cathode distance (ACD) should be maintained as low as possible,

          minimising Joule effect, as long as the cell remains stable;

         -   Liquid bath and liquid metal volumes.

       Control system developers are usually very secretive about the algorithms

applied, the number of variables included and the extent of influence of each parameter.

Bearne (1999) listed the variables usually monitored by the smelters: cell voltage, line

current, observation of the flame, bath depth, metal depth, tapped metal weight, bath

temperature, bath chemistry, metal purity, cathode voltage drop, alumina feed dump

weight, anode beam position and individual anode currents distribution. Occasionally

are measured: crust breaker position, AlF3 feed dump weight, sludge/ridge depth, ledge

position,    direct   alumina   concentration,   superheat,   sidewall   temperature,

cathode/collector bar temperature and sidewall heat flux.

      Not surprisingly, due to the large number of variables involved in the process

and that must be monitored, the control system of the cell is turned into a complex

labyrinth. Mathematical modelling of aluminium cells is becoming a powerful tool

(Tabsh et al., 1996; Drengstig, 1997; Biedler, 2003; Rodnov et al., 2003; Piskazhova

and Mann, 2006) in helping control systems development. Many virtual experiments

can be performed without disturbing a cell in operation. In addition, because the

electrolytic cells are difficult to instrument, some aspects can be assessed only by

modelling.

      2.4  Previous Studies on Aluminium Reduction Cell Modelling


        Historically,  the  mathematical models  of  electrolysis  cells  started  to  be

developed aiming  to study a  restricted aspect of the  cell physics (heat balance,

magnetism, mechanical stresses), according to the objectives of the research. As a

general trend,  it is observed in the literature that with the increase of computational

capacity, models became more complex geometrically (2D to 3D) and, more important,

coupling of interacting phenomena was introduced.

      There  are a  significant number  of physicochemical phenomena  occurring

simultaneously during the cell operation. Cross et al. (2000) discussed the most relevant

processes worthy to be studied by modelling, their list includes: electromagnetism, heat

transfer with phase change, multi-component free surface flow and the development of

                                                                     30

## PDF page 61

                                                      2 - Literature Review


thermal stress/deformation. In addition, there is a certain degree of interdependence

between  all these mechanisms and hence a real comprehensive modelling involves

coupling of complex multiphysics differential equations.

      The models found in the publications can be grouped into three main categories,

according to the focus proposed by each study: spatial distributed models, cell state

simulators and the combined models which present some degree of both kinds of

modelling.

        2.4.1  Cell Spatial Distributed Models

       This category of models is applied when the focus of the study is a particular

aspect of the cell physics or there is a localized region of interest such as: insulation

lining design, current density inside the collector bars or other parts, temperature at the

shell, fluid flow velocities, etc. Only the specific physics of interest is simulated in a

very detailed geometry (Tabsh and Dupuis, 1995). It is common to use these models in

steady state mode, representing a typical average of the reduction cell. In this review,

the focus is on the evolution of cell thermal modelling, although sometimes other

coupled phenomena are referred.

       Important principles of cell heat loss calculations were laid down by Haupin

(1971), when a method for calculation of the frozen bath thickness was presented. His

one-dimensional  analytical model used a  series of equivalent thermal  resistances

representing the cell walls insulation. Heat transfer coefficients at bath-ledge and metal-

ledge interfaces were estimated using typical velocity values inserted in  classical

Nusselt correlations. A whole cell heat balance was not established in that article. With

the same approach, but using more computational intensive tools, Arita et al. (1978)

presented a two-dimensional model helped by measured data inputs.

      Bruggeman and Danka (1990) implemented a 2D thermal steady state model

where many of three-dimensional heat sources and sinks were averaged in a two-

dimensional representation. Voltage equations were used for the heat balance. They

achieved reasonable results for the proposed objectives of cell lining design. The cited

authors modelled the liquid bath and metal, using the enhanced conductivity strategy.

This allows representing the convective heat transfer promoted by the fluids using only

the conductive heat flow differential equation. However, this approach needs fine

calibration to give good results.

                                                                     31

## PDF page 62

                                                      2 - Literature Review


       In the middle of 1990s, 3D distributed models started to be used to calculate

coupled thermal and electrical fields. Dupuis and Tabsh (1994) analysed the cell heat

losses using the commercial code Ansys.  In 1998, the same Dupuis made several

studies on variations of 2D and 3D thermo-electrical models with many different

geometric  assumptions:  slice  model,  slice/corner  models  and  quarter  models.

Advantages and limitations of each version were pointed out. Finally, a description of

many  historical models was published by Dupuis  in 2002. Nowadays computer

resources allow the construction of detailed 3D thermal-electrical models as shown in

Figure 2-16 and Figure 2-17. Even so, several hours are  still required to obtain a

converged steady state solution in 3D refined models.





          Figure 2-16: 3D electrolytic cell thermal electric model, one quarter model - external view of

                         calculated temperatures. Courtesy: CAETE Engenharia -Brazil





                                                                     32

## PDF page 63

                                                      2 - Literature Review





         Figure 2-17: 3D electrolytic cell thermal electric model, one quarter model - internal cut view

                       of calculated temperatures. Courtesy: CAETE Engenharia -Brazil

       Recently, the power of the fast and simplified models has been rediscovered.

Dupuis and Haupin (2003)  built  their  fast trend  analysis models  to be used  in

―brainstorming‖  because any new  idea can be checked  almost  instantaneously.

Calibration with measured data and 3D simulations was necessary to represent the cell

thermal resistances with reasonable accuracy. They studied the heat losses and voltage

drop  at anode panel and cathode blocks. Following a similar approach, Kiss and

Dassylva-Raymond (2008) constructed a simplified steady state mathematical model

aiming the study of the sidewall freeze thickness (ledge). They used the so-called ―2-

zones approach‖ represented by 2 parallel series of thermal resistances, connecting the

bath temperature (heat input location) to the environment temperature (heat output

location). It is a very elegant approach, since it does not require intensive computational

effort, and therefore, results can be obtained instantaneously. It enabled the authors to

show, mathematically,  the influence of many parameters on the  resulting freeze

thickness. In the referred article, a 2D thermal sub-model was used to obtain the walls

thermal resistances.


                                                                     33

## PDF page 64

                                                      2 - Literature Review


     More complex models have been developed in the  last years to study the

coupling between the thermal, electrical, magnetic and fluid flow fields inside the cell.

In 2005, Dupuis and Bojarevics presented their so-called ―weakly coupled‖ model

where fluid flow simulation provided the heat transfer coefficients for the electro-

thermal models. They supplied the calculated velocities near the solid walls to Nusselt

correlations and then, the cell solid interfaces (ledge, cathode top) were divided into

many regions, each one subjected to a different heat transfer coefficient. The cited

model is computationally very intensive and the user has to interact many times.

       Safa (2005) successfully built a fully coupled mathematical model to calculate

steady state thermal, electrical, magnetic and fluid flow fields. Ledge position was then

defined by the isothermal position inside the liquids’ space. It was possible to produce

results relating ledge shape with local fluid velocities. Although this is a very complex

and computer intensive model, the heat sources and sinks of the cell are too simplified,

only Joule heat is accounted and the material balance was not established. Nevertheless,

chemical composition of the bath is provided to the model in order to calculate the

freezing temperatures.

        2.4.2  Cell State Simulators

       Cell state simulators are a class of mathematical models developed to study the

cell dynamic behaviour. The geometry is here simplified, or even reduced into a lumped

parameter system, in order to be able to account for the transient and multiphysics

coupling complexities.

     Some models simply focus on a single specific process variable. For example:

Huang et al. (2009) proposed a heat and mass balance model in order to predict the AlF3

concentration inside the electrolyte. Their model predicted the measured results with

maximum error of 2% in the AlF3 concentration. The measurements also showed the
dependence of AlF3 concentration on the cell age, as the older cells presented higher
%AlF3 at the same electrolyte temperature. Unfortunately, a correction considering cell

age in the model was not implemented, perhaps because more detailed measurements

would be necessary.

      Other models intend to simulate the whole cell behaviour and there are examples

of cell state simulators used to train smelter personnel and serving as an auxiliary tool

for decision making.

                                                                     34

## PDF page 65

                                                      2 - Literature Review


      Hashimoto and Ikeuchi (1980) published a simplified mass and energy balance

model where the electrolysis cell was divided into eight regions. Their model aimed to

calculate the ledge variation in time, together with the alumina concentrations and bath

temperature. They calculated the gradient of components concentration inside the bath

using diffusion equations in order to consider the boundary layer effects. The entire

model was built using only one-dimensional transport equations. At that time, models

had to be very simplified because the computational resources were extremely limited

compared with  the  currently  available computers. The  liquidus temperature was

considered as constant during the entire process, which is an important weakness of

their model. In addition, the heat transfer coefficients from bath and metal to the ledge

and sidewalls were unknown at that time and assumptions had to be made.

       Tikasz et al. (1994) presented a model dividing the cell into eleven lumped

volumes for heat and mass conservation. The focus was to study the cell temperature,

alumina concentration and freeze position.  Little attention was given to the mass

balance of bath additives such as the AlF3. Alumina impurities and carbon consumption

excesses were not considered  at  that  time. The  referred authors proposed  three

applications for their models: steady state simulation for cell fine tuning, dynamic

simulation  of  normal  operation  and  dynamic  simulation  with  low  alumina

concentration.

       In 1996, Tabsh  et  al. presented a model of heat and mass balance of the

reduction cell. They implemented a control model, simulating some of the control

system actions in the cell relating beam position with the cell state. Mass balance for

many fluorides was tracked: LiF, MgF2, CaF2, and AlF3 along with cell temperature,

voltage, mass of metal, sludge and bath and ledge thickness. The cited model was built

on Haupin`s voltage equations for the heat balance, although they are not recommended

for transient calculations (see section 2.3.3). Despite the theoretical flaw mentioned

above, successful reproduction of cell behaviour has been reported by the authors

according to their judgement.

       In 1997, Drengstig presented an aluminium cell mass and energy balance model

aiming  the  development  of  fluoride and  temperature  control  procedures  to be

implemented in the electrolytic cell. His model was built using the Simulink code (a

Matlab extension) where the user can connect systems of equations using blocks. The

heat sinks were modelled using mass balance for the substances: Na3AlF6; Al2O3, AlF3,
                                                                     35

## PDF page 66

                                                      2 - Literature Review


CaF2, CO2, CO, C; Al. The focus of his work was on the bath layer and the connected

parts, thus the gases reactions under the cell hood were neglected. Energy losses

associated with anode butt replacement were also not accounted. Nevertheless, from the

point of view of substances considered in the enthalpy balance,  this  is the most

complete model found in the literature so far.

      Rodnov et al. (2003) created a dynamic model of reduction cell energy balance

based on enthalpy balance of Al2O3, C, Al, AlF3, CO2 and CO. Melting and freezing

enthalpies of ledge are also accounted. The balance was applied on a daily basis to the

cells in order to study the technological condition of the cells. The most interesting
contribution of the cited work  is the invention of the complex criterion ―‖ that

quantifies how efficiently the energy supplied to the electrolysis cell is used for the
alumina reduction reaction. When ―‖ was found to be higher than 0.62 the cited author

considered the cell ―healthy‖. Otherwise, the cell was considered ―alarmed‖ or ―sick‖,

meaning that the energy efficiency was low for that day and the reasons should be

investigated.

     More recently, Piskazhova and Mann (2006) developed two models at Rusal

capable of forecasting the consequences of cell disturbances. The operators become then

aware of the possible necessary actions, when a known cell disturbance occurs. The first

model was developed to predict cell state variables such as: bath temperature, cell

current, voltage, cryolite ratio, etc. A second model was dedicated only to the cryolite

ratio stabilisation procedures. These models presented more considerations on AlF3

balance. However, the cell material balances did not include carbon excesses and other

mass and energy balance inputs, such as alumina impurities and cell top losses.

        2.4.3  Cell State Simulators Combined with Spatial Distributed Models

     Some researchers realised that combining different modelling strategies allows

reproducing more of the cell behaviour complexity. One of the most complete cell

simulation models found in the literature was developed by Taylor et al. in 1996. In

their approach, fluid flow model was steady state while the combined heat balance

model was calculated on a transient basis. The modelling domain was divided into 3

distinct fluid regions: the bath at the upper location, the liquid metal and the sludge

located at the metal pad bottom. In addition, the solid walls containing the cell liquids

and the frozen bath are also included in the model. Three different thermal transient

                                                                     36

## PDF page 67

                                                      2 - Literature Review


imbalances were studied with the help of the model. Although their model presented a

very complex set of inputs and equations, one weakness is the heat transfer coefficients

―h‖ used  at  the  solid/liquid  interface, as the heat flow  is proportional  to those

coefficients. It is not clear in the paper, how the coefficients were obtained.

       In his thesis, Biedler (2003) built a system of combined models, aiming to

estimate the cell state during the cell opration. He used spatial distributed finite element

models for electrical and thermal field calculations. In addition, two other simplified

models were superimposed to the finite element model. One is a mass balance and

chemical model developed to estimate the transient alumina concentration in the bath,

including a simplified voltage calculation routine. Good correlation with practice was

reported. A second model was implemented to estimate bath temperature variations and

AlF3 concentration. Limited success was reported this time. For the voltage and thermal

modelling, Biedler used the Haupin’s voltage equations (1998) in transient modelling,

the same theoretical flaw that appears in many other mathematical models.

       Jessen (2008) used the Simulink Matlab extension to build up and combine a

material balance model with heat balance model and cell voltage model. Some two

dimensional geometry is included in the heat balance model. It resembles the Biedler’s

(2003) model with more careful material balance considerations. The model was

successful to predict voltage behaviour in tested cells. In the energy balance model, no

details about heat transfer coefficients between ledge and bath or metal were provided,

compromising the accuracy of the ledge thickness calculations. This would require

further fluid flow modelling, probably outside the scope of the referred work.

       In a more recent work, Cheung (2013) published a  cell spatial distributed

transient  model,  where  each  anode  temperature  is  individualised.  The  spatial

discretization  is in the line of Taylor’s (1996) model, dividing the  cell in many

volumetric subsystems. The model permits the use of anode current signals as model

inputs allowing estimation of local cell dynamics. Anode changing and anode shorting

conditions were tested. The spatial and temporal variations in the bath temperature was

calculated and compared with industrial cell measurements. Spatial distribution of ledge

thickness was also estimated.





                                                                     37

## PDF page 68

                                                      2 - Literature Review


      2.5  Justification  of  the  Research: Gaps on  the Knowledge

            Identified


      The literature review showed that mathematical modelling can be applied to

study many  aspects  of  the  aluminium  electrolysis  cell  behaviour.  Modelling

assumptions and techniques have evolved in the last decades, but there are still gaps in

the understanding of the reduction cell process. The main improvement opportunities on

aluminium cell modelling identified in the literature review are:

         -   Better cell mass balance representing a more complete set of materials flow.

          This  is an important step to achieve more accurate cell energy balance
            entirely based on the 1st law of thermodynamics, independent of the curve

             fitted data of the voltage equations. In addition, cell emission rates can also

          be predicted for a variety of substances.

         -  Heat losses associated with cathode top cleaning (―pacmaning‖) during

         anode change have never been studied. The cell energy balance proposed in

            this thesis should include considerations about pacmaning based on in situ

         measurements of masses and temperatures.

         -  Extend the cell control volume to include the cell hooding as suggested by

         Welch and Keniry (2000). Many previous models do not include the cell

          under hood space in the control volume, but important exothermic reactions

          occur at that place, influencing heat balance.

         -  The convective heat transfer between the ledge and the cell internal liquids is

          a source of uncertainty in the cell heat balance. In this thesis, 3D fluid flow

          modelling will be used in order to account for the MHD flow and the

          bubbles induced flow inside the bath.

         -  The complex transient interaction between mass imbalances and energy

          imbalances needs  to be  better understood by  researchers and process

           engineers. A  robust dynamic model  constructed  following  the above

           principles would be very useful for the improvement of electrolysis cell

          thermal stability.

      The aluminium smelters require more understanding of the process in order to

improve the cell operation procedures and decisions. The literature review (Section

                                                                     38

## PDF page 69

                                                      2 - Literature Review


2.3.4) presents the theoretical relationship between bath temperature, bath composition,

ledge thickness and cell heat losses. The mathematical modelling of those interactions

provides a tool for forecasting the cell response to state perturbations and for optimising

the corrective actions necessary to bring the  cell  state inside the desired  targets.

Operating the cell close to optimal conditions of mass and energy balances might save a

huge amount of electrical energy and raw materials and, as well, decrease the harmful

emission levels to the environment.

       In the following section 3, the model’s mass balance is explained in detail

regarding each cell mass stream and the respective assumptions adopted. In section 4,

the cell energy balance is described and the calculation methodology of each parcel of

energy input and energy output is explained. In section 5, the present model is applied

to steady state studies, and in section 6, the model is used to study the electrolysis cell

transient behaviour.





                                                                     39

## PDF page 70

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance



     3  CELL BALANCE MODEL: MASS BALANCE


      The basis for the proposed aluminium reduction process model is the first law of

thermodynamics. Consequently, mass balance for a given control volume must be

established on an elemental basis.

       In general, the mass conservation law is applied in the steady state form for each

substance.

                win _ i  wgenerated  wout _ i  wconsumed  0                     (3.1)

       In this chapter, a detailed description of calculation of each substance’s input

and output mass flow is presented. Moreover, it is shown how the mass flow can be

obtained for each case using information commonly available in smelters operation,

together with the necessary assumptions.

       In this work, two innovations are presented regarding the mass and energy

balance of the cell:

         -  The number of substances considered in the mass balance is the largest ever

          found in a published model;

         -  The mass balance control volume encompasses the entire cell and the hood,

           unlike the traditional approach. This enables the correct evaluation of cell

           top heat losses.

      3.1  Control Volume Definition


      The wise choice of the control volume boundaries is determinant for a successful

mass and energy balance model. In general, earlier studies (Tikasz et al., 1994; Tabsh et

al., 1996; Drengstig, 1997; Bruggeman 1998; Rodnov et  al., 2003; Biedler, 2003;

Piskazhova and Mann, 2006) usually adopted a control volume involving the cell

interior and cover as shown in Figure 3-1. This approach was useful to study chemical-

physical processes occurring only inside the bath layer, disregarding many reactions that

take place under the cell hood, but above the cell crust. These reactions are responsible

for an important amount of cell heat source, since most of them are combustion

reactions.


                                                                     40

## PDF page 71

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance





                      Figure 3-1: Control volume adopted by earlier heat balance models

    A more extended control volume was proposed by Welch and Keniry (2000) as a

future option for improving aluminium cells thermal modelling. Gadd (2003) used the

extended control volume in his cell energy balance monitoring studies. It comprises the

entire hooded cell, allowing the cell gases to cross the control volume at the cell hood

gaps and gas collection duct entrance.  The advantage of this extended cell control

volume is the elimination of many uncertainties of the proportions and contributions of

various intermediary reactions occurring under the cell hooding.

       In the present work, this new approach for control volume is implemented as a

basis for the cell mass and energy balance. The main difficulty is that it implies the

knowledge of  cell duct flow  rate, temperature and composition. However, many

substances’ flow rate can be deducted by analytical methods and the duct temperature

and flow rate are easily measured and even monitored regularly by the smelters.

      The Figure 3-2 presents the aluminium  cell control volume, including  all

relevant substances flow considered in the present approach.  It aims to be the most

complete mass balance used in modelling up to the present moment.





                                                                     41

## PDF page 72

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance





                     Figure 3-2: Relevant materials flow through the cell control volume

          It is necessary to identify and to quantify all substances entering and leaving the

control volume. Many substances’ mass flow rates are measured and controlled by the

smelters in normal potroom operation, such as: carbon, alumina, AlF3 aluminium, and

soda ash. Other mass flow inputs must be estimated using analytical methods, such as:

sulphur dioxide, fluorides evolution, water vapour (humidity), carbon dioxide and

carbon monoxide.

      The best option to represent the mass flow quantities is in units of kg of the

substance per ton of produced metallic aluminium [kg/ton Al], since this is the objective

of the smelting process and aluminium production rate is very well measured. Hence, it

enables the comparison of performance between different cell technologies and size.

      The aluminium production rate is calculated by the Faraday’s Law:

                       M Al                       wAl  CE       I                                (3.2)
                               F  n

      The faradaic current efficiency (CE) is around 90-95% for most aluminium

smelters. It is dependent on many factors such as: cell design, working practices, cell

                                                                     42

## PDF page 73

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


current. The relevant CE loss mechanisms are described in chapter 2.3.1. Reliable and

precise information about CE can be obtained only by monitoring cell current and

aluminium production.

      3.2  Material Inputs


       Material inputs comprise every bit of mass crossing the control volume from the

outside to the interior. The major mass flow inputs are alumina and carbon (anodes),

necessary for the alumina reduction reaction. However, for a sufficiently good mass and

energy balance, considering only these substances is too simplistic.

       Important contributions to be considered are alumina impurities such as: Na2O,
CaO, water and AlF3. Part of the alumina impurities comes originally from the primary

alumina and part is gained by alumina in the dry scrubbers. Monitoring the primary and

secondary alumina composition  is very important.  It gives information about bath

correction necessities and fluorides evolution levels, as it will be explained further in

this section.

      Carbon anodes enter the control volume accompanied of anode stubs, yokes and

guide rods. These materials do not react inside the control volume, but they consume

energy to be heated up. There are also carbon impurities to be considered, mainly

Sulphur. Anode cover is also added at each anode change and it is usually a mix of

alumina and crushed bath, in modern cells.

      Because the control volume includes the cell hood, a certain quantity of air is

sucked through the hood gaps. It also provides oxygen and water for some under-hood

reactions.

       In the next subsections, a more detailed sequence of methods and considerations

for the determination of each substance’s mass flow will be presented.

        3.2.1  Alumina Feeding Necessities

      The pure Al2O3 necessity to make aluminium can easily be calculated by

stoichiometry following the equation (2.1), resulting in 1889 kg/ton Al.

      However, alumina provided to the cell is in fact ~95% Al2O3, the rest being
composed of H2O, Na2O, CaO, AlF3 and other minor impurities. The major relevancy of

these impurities  lies in the fact that they are not  inert, modifying the target bath

                                                                     43

## PDF page 74

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


composition and generating different emissions. As a consequence, other material input

must be associated in order to correct the bath chemistry, such as AlF3 and soda ash.

      Another important aspect associated with alumina  is the phase content of

alumina fed into the cell. Nowadays, it is common to use aluminas rich in gamma phase

(more than 75%) and low alpha phase content (less than 25%). Different alumina phases

present different enthalpy and thus influencing the energy balance of the cell. The alpha

alumina content is usually provided by the alumina suppliers.

        3.2.2  Dry Scrubbing and Alumina Impurities

       In modern aluminium cells, dry scrubbing is applied to the cell exhaust gases in

order to collect the cell emissions harmful to the environment. ―Primary‖ alumina (also

called ―fresh‖ alumina) is injected into the cell exhaust gases stream. This mix travels to

the bag houses where the clean gases are separated from the, now called, ―secondary‖

alumina (or ―enriched‖ alumina, or ―reacted‖ alumina). The secondary alumina is then

directed to the aluminium reduction cell feeders, recycling almost all cell emissions

(typically, more than 98%). Figure 3-3 shows, schematically, the alumina and gases

streams allowing the recycling of cell emissions.





          Figure 3-3: Alumina and cell duct gases streams. Fluorides and other emissions are recycled

                                      applying dry scrubbing process


                                                                     44

## PDF page 75

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


      The most important substances of interest in this process are the fluorides

(mainly HF and NaAlF4, converted in AlF3 and Na2O), but other impurities also are

recycled, such as Fe. Metal purity is affected by alumina impurities level. The Table 3-1

presents  the impurity  levels  typically found  in primary and secondary alumina.

Unfortunately, not many elements are commonly analysed in the secondary alumina and

data values were normally found only for Na2O, AlF3 and CaO. In the table, the values
presented for P2O5, SiO2 and TiO2 in secondary alumina were obtained from one

smelter.

           Table 3-1 Typical impurity levels of primary and secondary alumina (in wt %), [*] values

                                       obtained from one smelter only


                                    Primary Alumina    Secondary Alumina

                 Na2O               0.25 - 0.55           0.50 – 0.85

               CaO                0.01 – 0.1           0.02 – 0.15

                    P2O5                ~0.001          0.0022-0.0037*

                    SiO2              0.013 - 0.02         0.0058-0.011*

                   TiO2              0.002 - 0.006        0.0013-0.0034*

                   Fe2O3               0.01 - 0.03         NA

                    AlF3                 0                  1.5 – 3

                Alpha Phase            2 - 25              2 - 25

           Loss on Ignition (L.O.I.)        0.5 – 3.5         NA

       For the mass and energy balance purposes, the relevant impurities to be

considered in this work are those influencing the bath chemistry (Na2O, CaO, AlF3) and
H2O that is correlated to the L.O.I. They need to be measured in primary and secondary

alumina.

      The hydrogen fluoride is chemisorbed through the reaction (3.3).

                   6 HF ( g )  Al2O3 ( s )  3H 2O( g )  2 AlF3 ( s )                 (3.3)

      The sodium  tetrafluoraluminate  adsorption  involves two  steps,  first  it  is

converted in  cryolite and hydrogen fluoride following equation  (3.4). The HF  is

chemisorbed as above (equation (3.3)). Cryolite is captured by the alumina stream and


                                                                     45

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


appears dissociated in the secondary alumina as aluminium fluoride plus sodium oxide

(equation (3.5)).

            3 NaAlF4 ( g )  3H 2O( g )  Al2O3 ( s )  6 HF ( g )  Na3 AlF6 ( s )                                                                                        (3.4)

                 2 Na3 AlF6 ( s )  Al2 O3 ( s )  4 AlF3 ( s )  3 Na2O( s )                                                                                        (3.5)

      The total alumina demand of the reduction cell, including impurities can be

expressed, using secondary alumina compositions in [kg/ton Al]:

                                      1889
         malumina               % Na2 O _ s  %CaO _ s  % H 2 O  % AlF3 _ s                     1 
                                        100                               (3.6)

      Not all alumina demand is provided by the alumina feeders. Eventually part of

the added cover falls into the bath, providing up to 10% of alumina requirements.

       Typically, modern cells present alumina consumption rates around 1940 kg/ton

Al, composed of:

           mAl2 O3  malumina  mAlF3 _ s  mNa2 O _ s  mCaO _ s  mH 2 O _ s             (3.7)

      Note that all the secondary alumina impurities must be heated to reach the bath

temperature. This energy sink is taken into account in the energy balance proposed by

this thesis.

        3.2.3  Bath Corrections: AlF3 Additions

     One of the key factors necessary to obtain optimal aluminium production

efficiency is to maintain the bath composition as stable as possible around specified

target values. As discussed in the literature review (Section 2), bath acidity is normally

                                                                     46

## PDF page 77

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


expressed as the %AlF3 in excess of the pure cryolite proportion (3NaF·AlF3). Usually,
the maintenance of the %AlF3 between 10-14% is desired because it lowers the melting
point of bath to ~950°C, improving current efficiency. In the smelters, %AlF3  is

monitored by analysing bath samples in the laboratory regularly.

      There are two basic oxides entering the cell together with alumina: Na2O and
CaO. In the bath, they will form cryolite and CaF2, according to equations (3.12) and
(3.13), and consuming AlF3 continuously.

                     3 Na2 O  4 AlF3  2 Na3 AlF6  Al2 O3                  (3.12)

                       3CaO  2 AlF3  3CaF2  Al2 O3                     (3.13)

       Eventually, aluminium fluoride has to be added into the cell to readjust bath

chemistry back to the targets. The amount of AlF3 addition rate is very well predictable

(Kolas and Stoere, 2007), according to alumina composition and scrubber efficiency.

The deficit in AlF3 is originated by the primary alumina composition, since the AlF3 and
Na2O gained in the scrubbers are produced by the bath and come back in a closed loop.

       Considering 100% scrubber efficiency, the aluminium fluoride mass flow can be

expressed by:

                        4 M AlF3        2 M AlF3               xsAlF3
       m AlF3 _ add  mNa2 O _ p       mCaO _ p       mNa3 AlF6 _ formed             (3.14)
                        3M Na2 O        3M CaO            1  xsAlF3

      Where:

                    % Na2 O _ p                 mNa2 O _ p        malumina                      (3.15)                                    100

                    %CaO _ p
                 mCaO _ p       malumina                       (3.16)
                                    100

                         M Na3 AlF6
                 mNa3 AlF6 _ formed  mNa2 O _ p                              (3.17)
                          M Na2 O

      Note that cryolite is produced by the Na2O reaction and extra AlF3 must be

added in order to achieve the target bath composition.

      The equation (3.14) is valid considering no sodium uptake by the cathodes.

Depending on the cathode type, AlF3 demand is below the presented prediction because


                                                                     47

## PDF page 78

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


part of the sodium provided to the cell by the alumina is stored inside the cathode in the

first months of cathode life. In the special case of early cell life, the mass balance has to

be adapted in order to account for a certain sodium accumulation.

        Ideally, a continuous fluoride addition would be recommended, but due to

technical limitations, many smelters use to add a certain amount once a day. Other more

modern smelters have a special feeder for AlF3, injecting at small shots many times per

day, reducing the bath composition variability.

       In modern cells, the net AlF3 demand observed in the practice is around 15-20

kg/ton Al.

        3.2.4  Bath Corrections: Na2CO3 Additions

       Eventually, the bath AlF3 concentration is found to be higher than the target,

caused by a number of reasons:

         -   Excess of addition by wrong AlF3 demand prediction;

         -  Change in AlF3 concentration target;

         -  Sodium uptake higher than expected by cathodes;

       In this case, sodium carbonate (Na2CO3) is added in order to neutralise the

      aluminium fluoride, see equation (3.18).

                 4 AlF3  3 Na2 CO3  2 Na3 AlF6  Al2 O3  3CO2             (3.18)

      Sodium carbonate additions occur much less often than aluminium fluoride

additions. It has the characteristics of ―emergency‖ addition, since it is not predictable

by analytical methods. In ideal cell behaviour, no addition is required. For this case:

                     mNa2 CO3 _ add  0                             (3.19)

        3.2.5  Carbon and other Anode Associated Material Inputs

      During  electrolysis,  the aluminium  is obtained by electrochemically aided

decomposition of alumina using carbon, see equation (2.1). Carbon oxidation reduces

about 50% the necessary energy input of the reaction and makes the aluminium

reduction feasible and efficient at temperatures around 960°C.




                                                                     48

## PDF page 79

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


      Modern cells use prebaked carbon anodes, usually 16-40 anodic blocks are

placed in contact with bath simultaneously and electrically in parallel. These blocks are

gradually consumed at a predictable rate, and replaced when necessary. Typically, each

~30h one anode block has to be replaced in the cell. Note that not  all carbon  is

consumed and ~20% of the original carbon is retrieved from the cell in the form of a hot

anode butt, as shown in Figure 3-4.





           Figure 3-4: Anode replacement procedure: spent anode butt being retrieved from the cell,

                                              source: Iffert (2007)

      The theoretical minimum carbon requirement, calculated by stoichiometry, is

333.9 kg/ton Al. In real cell operation, however, a number of factors (Sadler and Welch,

2001) are responsible for increasing  this consumption  rate up to ~550 kg/ton Al

considering the total carbon provided to the cell (gross carbon):

         -   Part of the carbon is not used (anode butt) and it is recycled. This brings the

           net carbon consumption to the range of 450 kg/ton Al;

         -   Part of the carbon is consumed by the Boudouard reaction;

         -   Part of the carbon is lost by oxidation when hot carbon is in contact with air;


                                                                     49

## PDF page 80

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


         -   Part of the carbon is lost in the form of carbon dust.

         -   Part of the anode mass is formed by impurities (~2%);

         -   Electrolysis inefficiency due to the re-oxidation reaction.

      The amount of carbon wasted by the above processes is dependent on cell

working practices and anode quality. As a consequence, a reliable prediction is very

difficult. The aluminium smelters keep tracking gross and net carbon consumption and

it is a reliable source of carbon consumption information for mass balance calculations.

Nevertheless, gross carbon consumption can also be estimated from anode mass and

anode life in the cell as follows:

                        WAn                    mC _ in  1000                                 (3.20)
                                                                      t An wAl

      The most important carbon impurity to be considered in a mass balance is the

sulphur content (typically 1.5-2%). It forms two gases during the anode consumption,

COS and SO2, influencing cell emissions composition. In the mass balance calculations,

the sulphur input rate is considered as fraction of carbon rate, as follows:

                        % S
                    mS _ in  mC _ in                                (3.21)
                                        100

     When changing the anode, a cold anode assembly is introduced into the cell, and

a hot anode assembly is pulled out of the  cell. Although there are no associated

chemical reactions, this has an influence on the energy balance. This work considers

only two chemical substances in the composition of the anode assembly parts besides

carbon:

         -   Iron, for the yoke and cast iron parts;

         -  Aluminium for the anode rod. In some technologies, the rod is made of

          copper instead of aluminium, and the equations for mass balance have to be

           adjusted accordingly.

       Mathematically, the mass flow of these components entering in the cell can be

expressed by:

                       WFe                    mFe  1000                                  (3.22)
                                                                     t An wAl


                                                                     50

## PDF page 81

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


                       WRod                    mRod  1000                                  (3.23)
                                                                     t An wAl

        Finally, during the anode change procedure, there is addition of dressing cover

over the anodes. The careful covering of the anodes is a key aspect for successful cell

operation. In modern  cells, the cover  is a mix of crushed bath and alumina, the

proportions being determined by smelter operations. Some implications of the chosen

mix proportion on the cell mass and energy balance have been discussed in the literature

(Richards, 1998; Taylor 2007). Blasques (2007) showed that improving the cover

addition control is important to reduce cell thermal excursions, also proving in the

smelter practice that covering practices affect the overall cell energy balance.

    A certain amount of cover has to be replaced at each anode change because:

         -   Part of the cover is carried out of the cell with the anode butt;

         -   Part of the old cover falls into the bath during the break of the consolidated

            crust;

         -   Part of the cover is spilt into the bath during the dressing of new anodes;

      With the information on the cover addition at each change, the mass flow rate of

       cover is obtained considering the substances: alumina, cryolite, AlF3 and CaF2:

                           WCov                                  1  BF  1000                          (3.24)                mCov _ Al2 O3 _ in  

                                             %CaF2    WCov              mCov _ CaF2 _ in  BF          1000                        (3.27)
                                              100          t An wAl

      Note that part of alumina is falling into the bath reducing the alumina demand

from the alumina feeders (up to 10%). For the same reason, bath level tends to increase

because crushed bath is molten when part of the cover falls into the cavity. Sometimes

fallen cover does not dissolve or melt, generating the so-called ―bottom sludge‖ or



                                                                     51

## PDF page 82

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


―mud‖ at the top of the cathode. Cathode cleaning procedure will be further discussed in

materials output subsection.

        3.2.6  Potroom Air Drawn into the Cell

     More than 95% of the gases collected by the cell exhaust system are originally

potroom air drawn into the cell by the negative pressure present under the cell hood.

The remaining ~5% is produced inside the cell, mainly in the bath. The under-hood

pressure is intentionally made negative as a way to guarantee that the bath emissions are

collected by the cell duct.

      With the information of exhaust flow mix and the knowledge of cell internal

processes, it is possible to quantify the amount of air sucked into the cell. In this work

the composition of  air entering in the  cell  is considered as atmospheric standard

composed of the substances: N2, O2, CO2, Ar, H2O.

                                  q Duct Duct                m Airin  1000        mbath _ ev                     (3.28)
                           wAl

      The water vapour content is evaluated according to potroom relative humidity

and temperature as presented by Dupuis and Haupin (CIM 2003):

                                HumF
                mH 2 O _ in  Sat H 2 O (TA )    m Airin                    (3.29)
                                         100

      The other substances mass flow inputs are given by:

      Other minor  inert gases  contributions  present  in atmospheric  air  are not

considered relevant for the mass and energy balance.

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


      3.3  Material Outputs


       Material output is considered any amount of mass crossing the control volume

from the inside to the outside. The alumina reduction process produces metallic

aluminium, carbon dioxide and a small amount of carbon monoxide. However, parallel

reactions produce other substances which are relevant for the heat and mass balance.

There are also inert materials entering cold in the control volume and leaving at higher

temperatures, carrying energy that must be provided by the cell.

      The cell duct flow is a mix of atmospheric gases and the gases produced inside

the control volume. In addition to the CO2 produced by the cell, the gaseous fluoride

substances are of relevancy. A certain amount of sulphur dioxide is also expected,

originated by the anode sulphur content. Some vaporised cryolite is also present as cell

emission.

      The neutralisation of alumina impurities by AlF3 produces cryolite and CaF2,

gradually increasing bath volume. Cover spillage also contributes to the bath volume

increase. Consequently, bath tapping must be performed periodically.

      Carbon anode butts leave the control volume together with the anode stubs,

yokes, and guide rods at each anode change, carrying energy associated with their

standard working temperatures. Part of anode cover is also taken out of the cell.

       In many modern smelters, during the anode change operation, the cathode

surface is cleaned by an item of equipment called the ―pacman‖ or the ―bath scoop‖.

Pieces of broken crust and sludge are the most relevant components of the removed

material.

       In the next subsections, a more detailed sequence of methods and considerations

for the determination of each output substance’s mass flow is presented.

        3.3.1  Aluminium Tapping

      Aluminium metal  is produced by electrolysis and  its  rate  is governed by

Faraday’ law at a certain current efficiency (equation (3.2)). Typically the metal is

tapped out once a day and it leaves the control volume at ~960°C, in liquid state. By

definition the aluminium mass flow in units of [kg/ton of Al] is:

                      mAl  1000                               (3.34)

                                                                     53

## PDF page 84

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


        3.3.2  Spent Anode Assembly Removal

      Carbon anodes are consumed at a predictable rate inside the cell. Unfortunately,

due to practical reasons, a fraction of the carbon anode (anode ―butt‖) must remain

attached to the rest of the anode assembly to protect the steel parts from the bath attack.

These parts of the carbon anodes leave the cell without performing any chemical

reaction. Later the anode butt carbon can be recycled outside the potroom.

       Considering  that  smelters keep  reliable  tracking of  gross and  net carbon

consumption, the output carbon mass flow can be determined by the difference between

gross and net carbon:

                   mC _ out  mC _ in  mC _ net                          (3.35)

       In the absence of net carbon consumption information, the other way to obtain

the mass flow is to use the anode butt mass:

                        WButt                    mC _ out  1000                                 (3.36)
                                                                      t An wAl

       Part of the sulphur content associated with the new anode also leaves the cell

with the anode butt:

                        % S
                    mS _ out  mC _ out                                (3.37)
                                         100

      The entire anode assembly leaves the cell (yoke, cast iron collars, guide rod) at

high temperatures. It is necessary to use the same consideration for material outputs

composition that was made for material inputs. Mathematically, the mass flow of these

components leaving the control volume is expressed by the same equations (3.22) and

(3.23), since there are no transformations involving these materials.

     When the butt anode assembly is removed, part of anode cover is also removed

together. Other part falls into the bath and remains inside control volume, at least in a

first moment, then this amount of mass will leave the cell in some other form: Pacman

material, or excess of bath. These fractions of cover destination are expressed by

equation (3.38).

                      Fbutt  Fpacman  Fbath  1                         (3.38)



                                                                     54

## PDF page 85

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


      For the cover accompanying the anode butt we can write mass flow expressions

for the substances: alumina, cryolite, aluminium fluoride and calcium fluoride:

                          WCov Fbutt                                 1  BF  1000                           (3.39)               mCov _ Al2 O3 _ out  

        3.3.3  Cathode Top Cleaning

      Modern cell operation practices recommend the cleaning of the cell cavity

during the anode change procedure. Figure 3-5 shows the cathode cleaning work being

carried out. If the cathode blocks’ top surface is not in good contact with the metal, the

electrical current paths are disturbed, as well the entire cell behaviour in many aspects

(electrical, thermal, magnetics, fluid flow). ―Pacman‖ material can be of different

origins and nature:

         -   ―Sludge‖ or ―mud‖ is formed by undissolved alumina that falls inside the

          metal layer. This alumina can be of feeder origin or part of cover material

             spilt into the bath.

         -  During the anode removal procedure, part of consolidate cover (crust) is

          broken in pieces and they are very likely to deposit over the cathode blocks.

         -   Pieces of broken anode (carbon that falls to the cell cavity).





                                                                     55

## PDF page 86

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance





             Figure 3-5: Cathode cleaning material being removed from the cell. Courtesy : SAUR

                                    Equipamentos S.A. - Brazil

       In the present work, for the sake of simplicity, the pacman material is considered

as having the same chemical composition of the original cover (cover spillage plus solid

consolidated cover).

                         WCov Fpacman                                1  BF  1000                            (3.43)              mPacman _ Al2 O3  
                                                                               t An wAl

                                     100  xsAlF3  %CaF2   WCov Fpacman
