                                            100             t An wAl

       In the mass and energy balance, we shall observe that the cleaning device

crosses the control volume boundary, entering the cell in a cold state and leaving at

higher temperature, thus removing a certain amount of energy from the cell associated

with the enthalpy difference of the equipment. Considering the device made of pure

iron, the associated device mass flow can be expressed in [kg /ton Al]:

                          WFe _ device
               mdevice _ in  mdevice _ out  1000                            (3.47)
                                                                               t An wAl




                                                                     56

## PDF page 87

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


      Sometimes the heat stored in the cleaning device is in the order of 0.1 [kWh/ton

Al] and has  to be taken  into account  in order  to achieve  precise heat balance

calculations.

        3.3.4   Bath Tapping

      There are two sources of bath formation in the cell:

        a)      The neutralisation of Na2O and CaO entering the cell control volume
              by  AlF3. Formation  of Na3AlF6 and CaF2  occurs  according  to

                 equations (3.12) and (3.13).

       b)       Cover spillage into the bath. The alumina part of the cover spillage is

              consumed to make aluminium metal. However, the crushed bath will

                  likely melt and increase the bath volume.

      The rate of bath production by mechanism a) is predictable by knowing the

alumina composition and applying equation (3.14). The amount formed by b) process is

more complex to analyse and it is dependent on cell working practices and strategies.

The total mass flow of bath to be tapped out from the cell can be expressed by:

                    2 M Na3 AlF6     xsAlF3       M CaF2     WCov Fbath    mbath _ out  mNa2 O _ p           1              mCaO _ p      1000 BF          (3.48)
                     3M Na2 O     1  xsAlF3      M CaO                  t An wAl

      The bath leaving the cell is composed of the substances:

                       2 M Na3 AlF6        100  xsAlF3  %CaF2   WCov Fbath     mNa3 AlF6 _ out  mNa2 O _ p        BF                         1000          (3.49)
                       3M Na2 O              100                   t An wAl

                       2 M Na3 AlF6   xsAlF3         xsAlF3   WCov Fbath
      m AlF3 _ out  mNa2 O _ p                       BF          1000           (3.50)
                        3M Na2 O   1  xsAlF3        100           t An wAl

                 M CaF2        %CaF2   WCov Fbath
          mCaF2 _ out  mCaO _ p      BF          1000                  (3.51)
                  M CaO         100           t An wAl

      The first term of the equation (3.48) expresses the formed cryolite by the Na2O
neutralisation plus extra AlF3 to maintain bath chemistry composition targets. The
second term is the formed CaF2 by the CaO neutralisation. The b) process is represented

by the last term, comprising the quantity of bath originated from cover spillage. The

excess bath generation mechanism has begun to represent a concern for the overall


                                                                     57

## PDF page 88

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


aluminium industry (Lindsay, 2011), as it is not possible to recycle the totality of the

surplus bath in starting up new cells.

        3.3.5  Cell Emissions

      The cell duct emissions are composed, in a general manner, of the air drawn into

the cell, including humidity, plus the gases generated inside the control volume minus

the gases consumed by the cell inside the control volume.

      There are many transformations inside the aluminium cell involving gases as

reactants and/or products. The chemical reactions involving gaseous substances that are

considered in this work as of importance for the heat and mass balance are listed below:

         -  Alumina reduction to produce metal:

                        2 Al2 O3  3C  3CO2 ( g )  4 Al                     (3.52)

         -  Aluminium reoxidation reaction, causing loss of current efficiency:

                      3CO2 ( g )  2 Al  Al2O3  3CO( g )                   (3.53)

         -  Anode sulphur content is responsible for the appearance of SO2 in the cell

           emissions.  First COS  is formed  in  the anode  at higher  temperatures.

          Subsequently, COS is burnt under the cell hood.

                          Al2 O3  3C  3S  2 Al  3COS ( g )                   (3.54)

                   2COS ( g )  3O2 ( g )  2CO2 ( g )  2 SO2 ( g )               (3.55)

         -  Anode carbon air burn: Part of anode surface is exposed to air and it is hot

         enough to combust. The carbon quantity loss by air burn has been estimated

            to be 50-100 [kg/ton Al], depending on the conditions of anode exposure

           (Sadler and Welch, 2001).

                     C  O2 ( g )  CO2 ( g )                          (3.56)

         -  Boudouard reaction: Part of carbon dioxide produced by electrolysis passes

          through the anode pores, an environment rich in carbon at high temperatures.

                    CO2( g )  C  2CO( g )                         (3.57)



                                                                     58

## PDF page 89

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


         -  As an assumption, all the carbon monoxide produced by the reoxidation

           reaction and Boudouard reaction is burnt under the cell hood where the

          temperatures are usually around 150°C. Only CO2 leaves the control volume.

                        2CO( g )  O2 ( g )  2CO2 ( g )                      (3.58)

         -  Hydrogen fluoride evolution from bath, alumina water content is responsible

            to provide the hydrogen.

                       2 AlF3  3H 2 O  Al2 O3  6 HF ( g )                    (3.59)

         -  Sodium tetrafluoraluminate evolution from bath. In a second step,  it  is

          considered that sodium tetrafluoraluminate is decomposed into hydrogen

            fluoride, cryolite and alumina under the cell hood before leaving the control

          volume. The water involved here comes from air humidity.

                    Na3 AlF6  2 AlF3  3 NaAlF4 ( g )                    (3.60)

               3 NaAlF4 ( g )  3H 2O( g )  Al2O3  Na3 AlF6  6 HF ( g )          (3.61)

      The determination  of each  individual  substance’s mass balance  is  highly

interdependent of the extent of many of reactions above. However it can be predicted by

adopting reasonable assumptions.  If duct gas flow composition measurements are

available, all the analytical part of composition prediction is not compulsory, but it is

recommended  to be performed  in  order  to check  the  consistency  of modelling

assumptions  and  to  analyse  possible  deviations  from  the  theoretical  averaged

conditions.

        3.3.5.1 CO2 Balance

       Several processes inside the aluminium cell control volume produce CO2. On the
other hand, there are reactions that consume CO2. The amount of carbon dioxide leaving
the control volume is the original CO2 mass flow entering the control volume plus the

internal generation minus the internal consumption.

                                               100  CE mS _ in 2 M Al  3M CO2     mCO 2_ out  mCO 2_ in  m Al  1  1  SC Factor                     
                                              CE   m Al  3M S  4 M Al                                                                                  (3.62)
       M CO2      M CO2     M CO2        mS      mC _ airburn      mC _ Boud
       M S      M C      M C


                                                                     59

## PDF page 90

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


      The equation (3.62)  is in units of [kg/ton Al] and  it takes into account  all

contributions from reaction equations (3.52), (3.53), (3.54) combined with (3.55), (3.56)

, (3.57) and (3.58), allowing for the proper rearrangements and cancellations. For

example, (3.53) and (3.57) consume CO2 to form CO, but before leaving the control
volume  all CO  is burnt under the cell hooding producing CO2 again. The factor
introduced (SCFactor) accounts for the part of current efficiency lost by short-circuiting

the anodes with the metal pad.

        3.3.5.2 CO Balance

     As mentioned before in this work, it is assumed that all CO produced inside the

cell burns before leaving the control volume. This is a good assumption since the under

hood space present temperatures in the order of 150°C and the gas velocity is low

(typically 1 m/s):

                                  100  CE 3M CO        2 M CO         mCO _ out  m Al 1  SC Factor              mC _ Boud
                            CE   2 M Al      M C
                                                                                  (3.63)
                            100  CE 3M CO        2 M CO             m Al 1  SC Factor              mC _ Boud      0
                       CE   2 M Al      M C

     When CO is burnt under the cell hood, heat is generated at that location. A fine

heat balance model has to consider the combustion heat sources.

        3.3.5.3  O2 Balance

      Oxygen is consumed by a few combustion processes inside the cell. Anode

carbon air burn is of relevancy and as well is the CO combustion under the cell hood. A

minor contribution of O2 consumption is due to COS combustion.

                                    3M O2      M O2
             mO2 _ out  mO2 _ in  mS _ in      mC _ airburn    
                                     2 M S      M C                                                                                  (3.64)
                               100  CE 3M O2     M O2             m Al 1  SC Factor              mC _ Boud
                          CE   4 M Al     M C

      The last two terms are a contribution of the original CO forming processes

(Aluminium reoxidation and Boudouard reaction) combined with CO combustion that

consumes oxygen.

## PDF page 91

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


        3.3.5.4 HF and NaAlF4 Balance

       Fluoride evolution from the bath  is a great source of concern in smelter

operations. These emissions are harmful to the environment and human health. This is

the reason why dry scrubbers are applied in order to recycle these fluoride compounds.

Patterson (2001) studied in detail many mechanisms of fluoride evolution. In this work

the two more important fluoride substances leaving the bath are considered: HF and

NaAlF4.

       In the past, Haupin (1984) and Haupin and Kvande (1993) developed models for

predicting fluorides evolution rate. Their models are dependent of a high number of

geometric and operational parameters. However, in modern cells, there is another way

to discover the evolution rate, analysing the primary and secondary alumina impurities

since dry scrubbing processes nowadays are almost 100% efficient. In his thesis, Iffert

(2007) successfully used this method to study Sodium and Fluorine balance in the cell.

      Under the cell hood the sodium tetrafluoraluminate in contact with humidity will

likely produce alumina, cryolite and hydrogen fluoride following equation (3.61). In this

work, it is assumed that 100% of NaAlF4 is transformed and the substances leaving the
control volume are only Al2O3, Na3AlF6 and HF.

      The difference in AlF3 and Na2O concentrations in primary and secondary

aluminas indicate the levels of fluoride emissions according to the next equations,

combining the stoichiometry of the processes occurring in the scrubbers, see equations

(3.3), (3.4) and (3.5):

                     mNaAlF4 _ out  0                              (3.65)

                                              2 M Na3 AlF6                                                                                  (3.66)             mNa3 AlF6 _ out _ ev                        mNa2 O _ s  mNa2 O _ p 
                                               3M Na2 O

                                                  2 M AlF3  3M HF            mHF _ out   m AlF3 _ s  mNa3 AlF6 _ out _ ev                            (3.67)                                                                                                                          M Na3 AlF6                              M AlF3                                                                                                

                         M Al2 O3
                 m Al2 O3 _ out _ ev  mHF _ out                              (3.68)
                                         6 M HF





                                                                     61

## PDF page 92

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


      These relations are valid for 100% scrubber efficiency. If this is not the case, a

multiplier for the fluoride emissions’ quantities in order to account for the extra amount

lost to the atmosphere is introduced in the modelling.

        3.3.5.5 H2O Balance

      The two main sources of water entering into the  cell control volume are:

humidity from potroom air and water associated with the alumina. Inside the cell, water

is responsible for producing volatile fluoride substances. Inside the bath at 960°C HF is

formed by reaction (3.59) using, most likely, alumina water content. Under the cell

hood, at much lower temperature, humidity water is used to produce HF following the

reactions   (3.60) and (3.61). The water consumption can be quantified  if fluoride

emission levels are known and the remaining water leaving the control volume is:

                             M H 2 O
              mH 2 O _ out  mH 2 O _ in  mH 2 O _ s  mHF _ out                      (3.69)
                                                2 M HF

       In this work, no account is taken regarding the influence of hydrogen present in

the anodes, which can have a minor contribution in HF formation inside the bath. The

result obtained by equation (3.69) might be slightly overestimated.

        3.3.5.6  SO2 and COS Balance

      The anode sulphur content leaves the control volume boundary in the form of

SO2. In this work, the assumption is that all COS formed at the anodes (equation (3.54))

is burnt (equation (3.55)) before the cell exhaust duct. According to Thonstad et al.

(1998), more than 95% of sulphur gases leave the cell in the form of SO2, thus justifying

the simplification adopted here.

                     mCOS _ out  0                              (3.70)

                          M SO2                      mS _ in  mS _ out                            (3.71)                mSO2 _ out  
                           M S

      Only the sulphur present in the consumed part of the anode reacts, the anode butt

sulphur content leaves the cell in its original form, quantified in equation (3.37).

        3.3.5.7  Inert gases: N2 and Argon

       Nitrogen and Argon enter the cell control volume as part of the air drawn into

the cell. They do not have relevant participation in the cell chemistry. For the purposes

## PDF page 93

                                        3 - Cell Balance Model: Mass Balance


of this work these gases are considered inert, leaving the cell by the duct at the same

mass flow rate, but at different temperature which have an impact on energy balance.

                     mN 2_ out  mN 2_ in                             (3.72)

                    mAr _ out  mAr _ in                             (3.73)

        3.3.6  Internal Generation of Gases

        Finally, the mass of gases generated inside the cell control volume can be also

obtained adding the contributions of all substances:

                           100  CE  mS _ in 2 M Al  3M CO2              m Al  1  1  SC Factor                           

      The air drawn into the cell can be now calculated back using equation (3.28).

The entire calculation process as presented here is recursive and computational tools are

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance



     4  CELL BALANCE MODEL: ENERGY BALANCE


      The aluminium electrolysis cell obeys the principle of energy conservation. The

difference between heat inputs and outputs is the heat accumulation. The energy inputs

are expressed by the enthalpy associated with the materials entering the cell and the cell

electrical energy generation. The heat outputs are expressed by the enthalpy associated

with the materials leaving the cell control volume and by the heat dissipation to ambient

(Figure 4-1). The equation (4.1) presents the heat balance function of materials flow,

Joule heat and dissipation to the ambient in the context of the aluminium electrolysis

cell:

Qin  Qout  Qac 
     wAl                                                 (TB - TA )            (4.1)                                                        mi _ out H i (Ti _ out ) VCell I                  mi _ in H i (Ti _ in )        1000                           REqAp





                              Figure 4-1: Energy balance of the electrolysis cell

     Some authors (Haupin, 1998; Bruggeman, 1998) convert the cell energy balance

into a cell voltage balance, dividing all equations by the cell current ―I”. However, this


                                                                     64

## PDF page 95

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


approach should be carefully applied because the first law of thermodynamics states the

conservation of only mass and energy.

      4.1  Energy Source: Cell Voltage and Line Current


      The smelters keep tracking the total cell voltage drop and the current passing

through the cell. The data acquisition frequency is in the order of one sample per second

or even higher. Consequently, the  electrical energy input to the cell  is very well

monitored and controlled in real time for economic reasons inclusive.

       In the past, many efforts have been made (Thonstad and Hove, 1964; Thonstad

and Rolseth 1978) to study in detail the parts of the total cell voltage. The voltage

breakdown can be expressed by equation (4.2) as a sum of chemical voltages and ohmic

voltages (one entire cell  is considered including busbars). Figure 4-2 presents the

voltage breakdown diagram for one cell, time averaged.

Vcell  E0 sa ca cc  Irbath  Ircathode  Iranode  Irext  Irbub  dVAE  dVInst  dVAR  (4.2)

       In fact, all cell overvoltages produce heat inside the bath layer. For the cell heat

balance purposes, it is irrelevant to know the exact values of overvoltages because all

will end up in the form of heat generation together with other heat sources. Only if ACD

estimations are required, the overvoltage values must be known.





                Figure 4-2: Voltage breakdown of a typical cell, including all sources and losses




                                                                     65

## PDF page 96

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


      4.2  Energy Sink Associated with Mass Balance


       After the implementation of the mass balance described in chapter  3, the

enthalpy balance associated with all substances can now be established. In this work,

the thermodynamic tables offered by the HCS5 program are used. The enthalpy

associated with a certain mass of a substance at temperature T is:


                                                            T
                 H i (T )  f H 2980 298 c p (T ) dT                        (4.3)

      The first term on the right hand side of the equation is the enthalpy of formation

for the substance at standard temperature (298.15 K) and the second term represents the

enthalpy difference from the standard temperature to the evaluation temperature. The

specific heat  is represented by a polynomial function of temperature valid for  all

substances, where only the proper coefficients (A, B, C and D) of each substance have

to be supplied. In the HSC5 database for the Cp(T) is presented in the form:

                 C p (T )  A  BT  CT 2  DT 2                        (4.4)

      The total heat sink of the cell is represented by the sum of all substances’

enthalpy entering and leaving the cell control volume. This can be mathematically

organised for modelling purposes:
                 mi _ out H i (Ti _ out )  H Tot          mi _ in H i (Ti _ in )  

    m Na3 AlF6 _ out _ ev          H Na3 AlF6 (TDuct )  m Al 2 O3 _ out _ ev H Al 2 O3 (TDuct )  mdevice _ out H Fe (TDev _ out )                                                                                                                                                                                      
     mrod H Al (TRod )  mFe H Fe (TYoke )                                                      

                                                                     66

## PDF page 97

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


         If good modern cell operation is considered, the heat sink calculated by the

equations above is found to be in the order of ~8.0-9.0 kWh/kg Al.  It includes the

amount of energy transferred from the cell interior to the gases leaving the cell duct.

          It is possible to define the minimum heat sink if only the alumina reduction

reaction is considered. It is obtained by calculating the enthalpy difference between

products (Al, CO2) at reaction temperature TB and reactants (C, Al2O3) at ambient
temperature TA.

                    3M CO2             M Al2 O3           3M C           H Min   H Al (TB )     H CO2 (TB )       H Al2 O3 (TA )     H C (TA )       (4.6)
                     4 M Al              2 M Al           4 M Al         

      The result for the ideal minimum required energy for aluminium production is

6.25 kWh/kg Al at TA=25°C and TB=960°C and using pure gamma alumina. Therefore,

all other energies exceeding this value are not used to produce aluminium directly, only

to create the proper conditions or simply being wasted. The cell energy efficiency can

be defined as the ratio between the minimum energy required for the reduction reaction

and the total energy provided to the cell.

                              H Min wAl                        E Eff  100                                        (4.7)
                                VCell I

      The best aluminium smelters in the world with the actual technological status

present energy efficiency in the order of 50%. Some older technologies produce at

energy efficiencies as low as 40%. Dramatic improvements in the cell energy efficiency

will only be possible with technological breakthroughs in materials or in alternative

processes (Keniry, 2001) that would tolerate the absence of the protective ledge at

sidewalls and a more effective thermal insulation. However, even maintaining the

current Hall-Héroult technology, a few percent in energy efficiency improvement would

represent a relevant energy saving amount and also an important gain in the smelter

competitiveness.

      4.3  Heat Losses  to Ambient: Analogue Thermal  Resistances

         Model

         If steady state condition is assumed (no energy accumulation Qac=0), the heat

losses are often described  in the  literature using the so-called 2-zones approach


                                                                     67

## PDF page 98

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


(Stevens, 1995; Bruggeman, 1998; Kiss and Dassylva-Raymond, 2008). The 2-zones

approach can be represented by two parallel series of thermal resistances, connecting

the bath temperature (heat input location) to the environment temperature (heat output

location).

       In this thesis, a more complete description of the cell heat losses is proposed: the

3-zones approach, where the heat flowing through the cell top insulation leaves the cell

at duct temperature and not at environment temperature. This reflects the chosen cell

control volume, including the under hood space. The Figure 4-3 shows the analogue

thermal circuit representing the cell heat losses of the 3-zones approach, and Figure 4-4

shows a schematic representation of resistances inside the cell.

      There are three parallel groups of thermal resistances: one for the heat passing

through the ledge and cell walls, one for the bottom heat flow without ledge and another

for the top heat losses to the duct air collection. The study of the cell duct heat losses is

very important to the correct cell energy balance understanding. Gadd (2003) showed

that the energy flowing throughout the duct is correlated to many cell energy imbalance

events.





                         Figure 4-3: Thermal resistances representing cell heat losses




                                                                     68

## PDF page 99

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance





          Figure 4-4: Schematic representation of thermal resistances inside the cell (sidewall cut view,

                             for endwall cut view, subscript ―W‖ is substituted by ―E‖)

      Nomenclature  for  Figure 4-3 and  Figure  4-4: ―R‖  is  thermal  resistance,

subscripts define the resistance position: ―W‖ stands for wall, ―M‖ is metal level, ―B‖ is

bath level, ―h‖ is convective resistance at fluid-solid interfaces, ―C‖ is cathode panel,

―A‖  is anode panel, ―S‖  is sidewall and ―E‖ endwall. Note: for simplicity, wall

resistances ―RW‖ are represented as the sum of solid conductive resistance and external

natural convection resistance around the shell.

     One series of resistances conducts a certain heat flow ―Q1‖ through the regions

covered by frozen bath. In this region, the superheat plays the major role as "driving

force" since Q1 is proportional to the superheat value, determining the heat flow and
ledge thickness. The other two series of thermal resistances conducts the ―Q2‖ and ―Q3‖

heat through the regions not covered by frozen bath, generally, anode panel and the

cathode blocks. The heat flow is proportional to the delta of temperature between liquid

bath and environment (for cell bottom) and between liquid bath and duct temperature

(for cell top).

                                                                     69

## PDF page 100

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


      Thermal resistances at the anode panel may not be constant. The cover thickness

variation (investigated by Shen, 2004) during the process affects the top heat losses. The

"apparent" thermal resistance increase by heat generation (see section 4.3.2.1) inside the

electrodes, pins, yokes and guide rods is a function of cell current. Liquidus temperature

is a function of bath chemistry and bath superheat is calculated iteratively from the

balance considering heat generation and losses inside the cell.

        4.3.1  Sidewall and Endwall Heat Losses (Q1)

       Here, the resistance network of the regions covered by ledge is described. In this

work, the total wall resistance (Rh1+RLedge+Rw1) includes:

      Bath-ledge and metal-ledge convective heat transfer resistance obtained from

        the bath-metal CFD sub-model. The determination of h1 for bath and metal will

       be explained in the section 4.3.1.1, they are dependent on a numerous of cell

        parameters, such as: current density, magnetic fields, bath and metal height, etc.

                                                 (TB  TL )   1                       Rh1                                          (4.8)
                        Q1     h1 AL

      Ledge resistance  is dependent on the ledge thickness  (dL) and  it can be

       determined if other thermal resistances are known. Any variation of the bath

        temperature moves the ledge solidification front and dL, thus changing the ledge

       thermal resistance.

                                           TL  TA      (TB  TL )                Q1          
                                d L     Rh1                      Rw1 
                       K L AL                                         (4.9)
                                   TL  TA                               d L   Rh1                    RLedge                 Rw1

      Other material thermal resistances (shell, insulation, refractory materials) plus

        the external heat transfer convection and radiation resistances,  all  serially

        connected, grouped in a single value (Rw1). Four parallel circuits are modelled:

        sidewall bath, sidewall metal, endwall bath and endwall metal.
                          Rh1  TL  TA    d L                      Rw1                                         (4.10)

## PDF page 101

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


       In this work, Rw1 values are obtained by a 3D finite element model (exemplified

in Figure 4-5 and Figure 4-6) where a refined numerical mesh is applied. The heat

transfer equations are coupled with the  electrical  field equations  for ohmic heat

generation. The obtained thermal  resistances  are considered  constant during  cell

operation, since they are not dependent on bath temperature, cell current or any other

operational parameter. If cell geometry, or electrode properties or insulation properties

are modified, then the 3D model has to be recalculated to obtain new Rw1 values.





             Figure 4-5: 3D electrolytic cell thermal electric sub-model, external view of calculated

                                               temperatures





          Figure 4-6: 3D electrolytic cell thermal electric sub-model, external view of calculated heat

                                                 flux distribution


                                                                     71

## PDF page 102

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


        4.3.1.1  Convective Internal Thermal Resistances (ledge/bath and ledge/metal)

      The heat transfer coefficients at the bath/ledge and metal/ledge interfaces are key

factors in the prediction of ledge size and heat flux through the cell walls. Heat transfer

coefficients can be predicted by numerical models using CFD codes. The models

calculate the main flow patterns in the bath and in the metal using different approaches.

Subsequently, heat transfer coefficients are post-processed using appropriated wall

functions (Severo and Gusberti, 2009). The modelling results are in accordance with the

magnitudes found experimentally by several researchers (Sohlheim and Thonstad, 1983;

Taylor and Welch 1987; Fletcher et al., 1996).

      The majority of  the  existing  electrolysis  cell energy balance models use

empirical heat  transfer  coefficients. In  real  industrial  cells, these  coefficients are

determined indirectly through measurements of temperature, external cell heat flux, and

ledge thickness. When a new cell design is proposed however, no such measurements

would be available and a predictive computational model would be necessary to obtain

the convective coefficients.

      The methodology used in this thesis for the cell cavity heat transfer coefficients

determination is the same as presented by the author’s previous publication (Severo and

Gusberti, 2009).  It is based on local calculated fluid velocities and known physical

properties  of  the  materials  involved.  In  the bath  region, a Computational Fluid

Dynamics (CFD) sub-model was built to take into account the bubble driven flow and to

calculate the bath velocity field. Afterwards, the coefficients are obtained with classical
wall function laws, which are implemented inside the commercial code ANSYS CFX®.

In the metal pad layer, the metal velocities are calculated taking into account the MHD

forces produced by the strong electromagnetic field present in the cell. A special Nusselt

correlation for liquid metals must be chosen for the correct heat transfer coefficient

estimation. The modelling procedure is not independent of empirical correlations, but it

relies on classical thermal boundary layer laws found in specialised heat transfer

literature (Rohsenow, 1998), which are applicable to a wide range of geometries.

        4.3.1.1.1 Bath-Ledge Heat Transfer Coefficients - Sub-Model 2a

      The sub-model used for the determination of bath/ledge convective heat transfer

coefficients was implemented taking into account the bubble driven flow in a quarter of

cell, assuming symmetry, where the following physics is solved simultaneously:

                                                                     72

## PDF page 103

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


     Fluid flow Navier-Stokes equations for bath and for CO2;

     Continuity equation for bath and CO2;

     Momentum transfer between bath and CO2: bath is treated as continuous phase
       and CO2 is treated as dispersed gas phase (bubbles);

     Thermal energy transfer equation;

     K-epsilon turbulence model.

      The bath thermal conductivity is an important model input. A value of 0.8 W/mK

was used following the recommendation of Khokhlov et al. (1998), who measured

thermal conductivities of many different bath compositions. The model domain and

boundary conditions are shown in Figure 4-7. The bath top is treated as a ―degassing

boundary‖, which means that the gas is able to leave the calculation domain and the

liquid phase  is confined within the domain. The bath and metal are considered

immiscible fluids. Instead of modelling the metal space, a ―free slip wall‖ boundary

condition was adopted at the interface. No shear stress between metal and bath is

present in this approach.





             Figure 4-7: Schematic view of the model used to calculate bath heat transfer coefficients

      Heat transfer coefficients are function of the liquids’ properties and fluid flow

characteristics, and  independent  of  solid  wall  properties  (except  wall  rugosity).

Therefore, a high level of detail is concentrated in the liquid space.

      The multiphase flow is calculated in the bath region with a continuous phase

(bath) and a disperse phase (CO2, bubbles mean diameter must be specified). Figure 4-8


                                                                     73

## PDF page 104

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


and Figure 4-9 present the streamlines examples of the bath flow for the described

model.





                             Figure 4-8: Bath bubble driven flow: general view





                               Figure 4-9: Bath bubble driven flow: cut view

       Figure 4-10 shows, as an example, a map of heat transfer coefficients calculated

for the bath-ledge interface. The coefficients for the bath-anode interface are also

plotted. The thermal boundary layer is modelled using the thermal law-of-the-wall
function of Kader (1981), which is implemented inside ANSYS CFX® commercial code

by the set of equations:

                                                                     74

## PDF page 105

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


                   c p u *
                                hb                                       (4.11)                                 T

                                                                                                                          ( 1/  )                 (4.12)                  T   Pr y *e (  )   2.12ln( y                                                                       * )   e

                        Figure 4-10: Wall heat transfer coefficient distribution at bath


       Generally, the bath flow is more intense in the regions near the narrow channels

between anodes because CO2 is leaving at higher velocities in that region, resulting in
higher heat  transfer  coefficients.  In the  central region under the anodes, a CO2

stagnation zone is formed (in fact, it is an unstable equilibrium zone), where the bubbles

start to move slowly towards anode edges, resulting in lower heat transfer coefficients at

these locations.

        4.3.1.1.2  Electromagnetic Forces: Sub-Model 3

      The electromagnetic sub-model was developed  at CAETE Engenharia and

published in the author’s past article (Severo et  al.; 2005). A brief description  is

provided in the next paragraphs.


                                                                     75

## PDF page 106

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


          It is necessary to use a detailed Finite Element Model. The magnetic field is

sensitive to many geometric structures of the electrolysis cell and potline: external

conductors (busbar arrangement), internal conductors (liquid layers, collector bars,

anodes and cathodes), and the steel shell.

       In the commercial code ANSYS, the electric potential V is calculated as a

primary variable from the Laplace equation (4.15),

                              (k V )  0                             (4.15)

      The current density vector (J) in each material is obtained from the gradient of

the electrical potential and the electrical conductivity, equation (4.16).

                                 J k V                               (4.16)

      The electric model boundary conditions are: reference potential equal to zero at

the negative terminal of the power supply and the line current entering at the positive

terminal. Surrounding the pot of interest, an amount of air should be modelled, where

the magnetic field and magnetic flux density are calculated. The magnetic flux density
vector (B) is obtained by multiplying magnetization factor ( ) by the magnetic field

vector (H) as expressed in equation (4.17).

                         B  H                                 (4.17)

      The electrolysis  cell geometry presents  electrical conductors surrounded by

magnetisable materials  (steel parts of the shell and superstructure). This problem

requires the use of the Magnetic Scalar Potential approach available in ANSYS. It is an

iterative Finite Element Method that combines the Biot-Savart Law calculation with the

magnetic field diffusion influenced by the steel parts magnetization; see Schneider et al.

(2008) for more details. At the end of the calculation process, B and H vectors are

established for every element of the model.  The methodology was validated by

Schneider et  al. (2008) comparing simulation results with industrial magnetic flux

density measurements.

      The Figure 4-11 shows an example of the mesh used in the electromagnetic

modelling. All electrical conductors are represented as well as part of the surrounding

air.



                                                                     76

## PDF page 107

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance





                 Figure 4-11: Electromagnetic finite element model mesh. Severo et al. (2005)


      The Figure 4-12 shows the magnetization of the steel shell. The Figure 4-13

presents the vertical component of magnetic flux density in the metal pad horizontal

plane, obtained by the described methodology.





                  Figure 4-12: Shell magnetization [mT] as presented in Severo et al. (2005)

         Figure 4-13: Magnetic field component Bz at the middle of the metal pad [mT], as presented in

                                         Severo et al. (2005)

      The volumetric electromagnetic forces inside the bath and liquid aluminium are

obtained by the vector product of the current density and the magnetic flux density,

equation (4.18).

                           FEM  J×B                               (4.18)

        4.3.1.1.3 Metal-Ledge and Metal-Cathode Heat Transfer Coefficients: Sub-

              Model 2b

       Inside the metal, MHD forces are dominant and non-symmetric. A full cell

model for metal flow is necessary in order to represent the flow patterns correctly. The

electromagnetic forces and magnetic flux density obtained by the previously described

electromagnetic sub-model are used as input. They are applied as body forces (4.21) in
the metal pad space, including induced currents forces ( m ( v×B) ×B). Metal velocities

are obtained solving the Navier-Stokes equations (4.19) and continuity equation (4.20),

upon numerical convergence.

           ( v v ) p eff ( 2 v )  g  FEM                 (4.19)

                    FEM  J×B  m ( v×B) ×B                       (4.21)

      Using the metal flow sub-model, at the metal-ledge interface, a special approach

for heat  transfer  coefficients  calculations must be used. Most of wall  functions

implemented in commercial codes are fitted for fluids presenting high Prandtl number,

near 1.0 (air, water, etc.). Normally, liquid metals present lower Pr numbers, in the

order of 0.05. Therefore, appropriated liquid metals Nusselt correlations must be

programmed in order to represent heat transfer adequately.

       In this case, a Nusselt correlation (Rohsenow et al., 1998) for liquid metals was
used to obtain the coefficients, which depends on the skin shear stress (wall) in the fluid

near the walls, as described below. Fluid properties can be found in Mills (2002).

transfer coefficients increase to more than 1000 W/m2/°C.

        4.3.1.1.4 Bath Film Correction

       In the past, several authors (Haupin, 1971; Hansen et al., 1996; Solheim, 2006)

proposed the existence of a continuous liquid bath film between liquid metal and frozen

bath. The bath film would explain the ledge formation in the metal region by the

migration of liquid from the bath to the metal-ledge interface.

      According to Hansen et al. (1996), it is a phenomenon dependent on the bath

surface tension gradient and the molar bath ratio gradient (NaF/AlF3) between the top

and bottom of the film. They presented methods of evaluation of the film thickness

(typical values found between 0.1 mm and 0.4 mm). The bath film decreases the metal-

ledge overall heat transfer coefficient, with the addition of the extra thermal resistance

of the bath film:

                                       1
                               heff  1 BF                             (4.26)                                                      
                                               hm  K b 

                                                                     81

## PDF page 112

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


      Bath film thickness is evaluated following the procedure proposed in Hansen et

al. (1996) for the so-called rigorous model:

                                BF              BF  2.64                                        (4.27)                               LBF  m  b  g x

      BF is the interfacial tension along the film, which is related to the NaF/AlF3

molar ratio along the film "BR" by the relationship:

                      BF 0.0875BR                          (4.28)

    A value of 0.6 was used for BR in all calculations, as recommended by Hansen

et al. (1996).

        4.3.2  Cell Bottom Heat Losses (Q2)

       In this work the bottom thermal resistances are described generally by a sum of

internal convective resistance (Rh2) and the combined electrode-insulation-radiation and
convection to the environment resistance (Rw2). This separation is based on the fact that
Rh2  is dependent of operational parameters, and  is calculated by the methodology
presented in the section 4.3.1.1. Rw2 is independent of operational parameters, materials

and geometry remain constant, except, if cell current is modified, the thermal shielding

of Joule effect in the electrodes also is modified, see section 4.3.2.1 below. Rw2 is

obtained by 3D finite element models and as well the coefficient for Joule effect

shielding (Cratio). This methodology is valid only for steady state calculations. For

transient studies, the heat accumulation inside the materials must be allowed, together

with thermal capacitance. In section 6, adaptations for the dynamic model are presented.

        4.3.2.1  Joule Effect and “Apparent” Thermal Resistances

      The thermal resistance of a material is dependent on its thermal resistivity and

the geometry. When this material is subjected to electrical current, heat is generated by

Joule effect, decreasing the capacity to extract heat from the material's hot side to the

cold side. If properties and current density are homogeneous, according to heat diffusion

laws, half of the heat generation is redirected to the cold side increasing heat output, and

half to the hot side decreasing heat input or in extreme cases inverting the heat flux

direction.



                                                                     82

## PDF page 113

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


       This shielding effect will be called, from now on in this work, ―apparent thermal

resistance‖, as  it works as if the total thermal resistance was increased by the heat

generation, in the point of view of cell internal energy dissipation to the potroom

environment.

      The heat flow leaving the hot side plus the Joule Effect power is equal to the

heat flow of cold side:

                    QHot +rI 2 =QCold                             (4.29)

      Heat generated inside the conducting body decreases the heat entering by the hot

side, and increases the heat leaving by the cold side. Considering homogeneous source

and material properties, QHot decreases half of the generated heat and QCold increases the

other half of the heat source, compared with the zero Joule effect heat flow.

                   TB  TA   rI 2              TB  TA   rI 2           QHot =              and  QCold                      (4.30)
                 RPhysical   2             RPhysical   2

      Of course, in the electrolytic cell, top and bottom sources are not homogeneous,

nor the cathode/refractory/insulation materials present homogeneous properties. Hence,

the 1/2 coefficient is modified (originating the Cratio in equations 4.30 and 4.31) and it

must be calculated numerically by 3D modelling (Figure 4-5 and Figure 4-6). This can

be done by running the same model with a few different  cell current boundary

conditions.

              TB  TA             2              TB  TA                    2       QHot =       CRatio rI     and  QCold        1  CRatio  rI     (4.31)
            RPhysical                   RPhysical

         If one considers the point of view of heat leaving the bath boundary (hot side

QHot), the real physical thermal resistance is expressed in the form:

                                 TB  TA                      RPhysical                    2                         (4.32)
                        QHot  Cratio rI

                           TB  TA                   RApparent        RPhysical  RJEff                    (4.33)
                      QHot

       Substituting equation (4.31) in equation (4.33), we obtain expressions for total

apparent thermal resistance and the extra resistance generated by Joule effect:



                                                                     83

## PDF page 114

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


                                        1
                     R Apparent                         2                       (4.34)
                                    1   C Ratio rI                                    
                            RPhysical  TB  TA

      According to the authors’ 3D numerical model investigations of several real cell

geometries, values between 0.55-0.70 are typically found for CRatio, for both cell top and

cell bottom regions due to carbon heat generation.

        4.3.3  Cell Top Heat Losses (Q3)

      The cell top heat transfer behaviour is complex because the access to the cell

cavity for material fluxes and for maintenance is made in this region. Carbon anodes

have to be replaced periodically and the cover is always changing, a function of local

anode age. Open holes at the top crust are necessary for the alumina feeding. In

addition, the gases produced at the bath escape to the cell top space, where they are

collected by the cell exhaust duct. The gases also are subjected to chemical reactions at

the cell top.

       In this study, an innovative way to calculate the cell top heat balance was

developed. The duct temperature is used as input in the model, which enables the study

of the influence of the duct gas flow rate on the cell energy balance.

       This approach is very useful to the industry since the duct flow rate can be easily

regulated by smelter operators. In the industry, there  is a lack of understanding

concerning the relationship between the cell heat balance and the cell duct flow rate.

       In the present thesis, it was decided to divide the cell control volume in two

smaller sub-volumes (Figure 4-17), namely top and cavity sub-volumes. The new

methodology results in three mass and energy balances:

           1) Mass and energy balance for the entire cell as presented in Chapter 3.1,

              Figure 3-2.

           2) Mass and energy balance for the cell cavity (bottom sub-volume).

           3) Mass and energy balance for the cell top (top sub-volume).





                                                                     84

## PDF page 115

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance





                         Figure 4-17: Cell control volume, divided into 2 sub-volumes

     An important amount of the cell energy is drained by the cell exhaust duct in the

form of enthalpy of the gases. It is worthwhile to investigate the optimum cell exhaust

mass flow rate that minimises the heat losses, and simultaneously guarantee a good

collection efficiency of environment harmful substances. The Figure 4-18 shows the

most relevant mass and energy inputs and outputs of the cell top sub-volume. Note that

some exothermic reactions occur inside this sub-volume. They also must be accounted

in the energy balance.

      The energy flux passing from sub-volume 2 (cell cavity) to sub-volume 3 (under

hood space) has two sources:

         -  Energy flux a): heat lost by the crust and anode assemblies surfaces that

        come from the bath heat generation and anode/yoke/rod heat generation. The

          bath heat transfer to the cell top is controlled by the thermal resistances from

           the bath temperature region (TB) and the gases average temperature under the
         hood (TDuct) before the chemical reactions inside the cell hood are computed.

         The cell top heat transfer resistance is a combination of bath convective heat

            transfer coefficient, conduction through the anode carbon and cover/crust

           plus convection and radiation under the cell hood. The shielding effect of


                                                                     85

## PDF page 116

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


          anodes heat generation receives the same treatment as the cathodes (Chapter

            4.3.2.1), where the apparent thermal resistance is evaluated with the help of a

          3-dimensional sub-model.

         -  Energy flux  b): Enthalpy of gases evolved from bath, considering the

           substances: CO, CO2, COS, HF and NaAlF4 at bath temperature.

       In addition, the other important energy input is the enthalpy of the potroom air

entering in the sub-volume 3 due to negative pressure generated by the potline exhaust

system.

      The  relevant energy outputs  are: The enthalpy  associated with  cell  duct

emissions after accounting for the chemical reactions that take place inside the control

volume (combustion of COS, C, CO and secondary HF generation), and the heat lost by

convection and radiation at external surfaces to the potroom environment.





        Figure 4-18: Cell top control sub-volume, most relevant continuous mass and heat fluxes are

         represented




                                                                     86

## PDF page 117

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


       The cell top energy balance model requires knowledge of the relationship of

duct flow versus heat transferred from cell cover and anode assemblies. In order to

determine the heat transfer coefficients of these surfaces, a 3D CFD sub-model must be

employed. The sub-model was specifically developed by this author in order to help in

mass and energy balance calculations and it has also been shown in recent publications

(Gusberti et al. 2011; 2012).

      The cell top sub-model uses a CFD commercial code in order to simulate the gas

flow and heat transport at cell top and exhaust duct coupled with electrical fields at

conductors for ohmic heat generation. The model domain contains anode blocks, yokes

rods, the crust and cover. In addition, the volume occupied by the gases is included with

the collection duct. Navier-Stokes equations, continuity equation, electrical charge

transport, energy transport equation and Shear Stress Transport (SST) turbulence model

are solved simultaneously inside the commercial code ANSYS CFX. The fluids present

variable density with the temperature and the buoyancy forces are also included in the

simulation.

      The boundary conditions applied (Figure 4-19) for a particular case (180 kA

cell) are listed below:

         -  The potroom air enters the model by the gaps at a temperature of 50.5°C, it

             is the averaged potroom temperature informed by the smelter;

         -   Total gas flow leaving the cell by the duct is provided (5900 Nm3/h in this

            case);

         -   Anode/bath heat transfer coefficients obtained from the bath sub-model

          (Chapter 4.3.1.1.1, sub-model 3a) are applied together with the bath bulk

          temperature (~960°C);

         -  The gases generated inside the bath enter into the domain by the crust holes

          used for alumina feeding at bath bulk temperature (~960°C). The chemical

           reactions  heat  sources  are  also  included  (Carbon  airburn, CO, COS

          combustion and secondary HF generation).

         -   In this case, the geometry is symmetric, half of the geometry is sufficient for

           the simulation.

         -  At the anode bottom surface, the reference voltage is applied, V=0;

                                                                     87

## PDF page 118

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance


         -  At the top of the guide rods, the electric current is applied. The cell current is

           equally distributed to each anode rod.





                  Figure 4-19: Boundary conditions of the gas collection sub-model

      The calculated gas temperature field for this situation is shown in Figure 4-20.

The gas velocity streamlines are shown in Figure 4-21. The output cell duct temperature

is obtained by the model, as well as the heat transfer coefficient for each surface is also

quantified and this information is transferred to the cell balance model.





    Figure 4-20: Temperature distribution of the cell gas calculated by the gas collection sub-model




                                                                     88

## PDF page 119

                                      4 - Cell Balance Model: Energy Balance





              Figure 4-21: Calculated gas velocity streamlines of the cell gas collected by the duct

     By observing the temperature field and the flow patterns,  it  is possible to

identify regions where the flow is dominated by the buoyancy forces, under the cell

hood near the cover. In the duct collection region, the flow is dominated by the under

pressure forces imposed by the potline exhaust system.

       In the presented case results, it was learned that changes in the duct flow rate

have a limited effect on the energy flow of the cell top. Typically, around one third of

cell top thermal resistance is convective (gas-cover). The other two thirds are anode

panel and crust/cover material resistances. The exact shares of each thermal resistance

depend on cell technology and cover practices. These facts mean that a reduction in the

duct flow by 50% would produce ~16% top heat loss reduction. In the study published
at 10th Australasia Conference (Gusberti et al. 2011), it was shown a case where cell

consumption could be reduced up to ~0.7 kWh/kg Al by modifying the duct flow rate.





                                                                     89

## PDF page 120

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State



     5  CELL MASS AND ENERGY BALANCE SOFTWARE  -

      AVERAGED STEADY STATE


      The global cell balance model was implemented in a computational algorithm,

using the balance equations presented in chapters 3 (mass balance) and 4 (energy

balance). The two balances were integrated using Visual Basic computational code; the

software application was named ―Cell Mass & Energy Balance‖. In this section, only

the averaged steady state is considered, although real cells are never found in such ideal

conditions. The model was also expanded to obtain the transient simulation results,

which requires the inclusion of extra subroutines to calculate the ledge dynamics. This

will be presented in section 6.

       In the first part of this section, the computational algorithm is presented and the

system user interfaces are shown. After, a case study is presented to illustrate the types

of results that are possible to be obtained from the cell balance model. The main

differences found  in the present method  in comparison  to  classical methods  are

discussed.

        Finally, a comparison of three different cell technologies presenting different

size and amperage is presented in order to demonstrate how the mass and energy

balance is affected by the technology evolution of the electrolysis cells.

      5.1  Implementation of the Cell Balance Computational Code


      The Figure 5-1 presents a flowchart showing how the different sub-models are

integrated in order to obtain the complete mass and energy balance modelling. The 3D

electromagnetic sub-model  (sub-model  3)  provides MHD  forces  for  fluid  flow

calculation at metal (sub-model 2b). In parallel, the gas induced flow is evaluated at the

bath (sub-model 2a). Once the fluid flow is obtained, wall heat transfer coefficients at

the ledge, anode and cathode surfaces are calculated. The top cover heat transfer

coefficients are calculated from the cell top gas flow sub-model (sub-model 4). All

These coefficients are supplied to the thermal 3D sub-model (sub-model 1) and also to

the global cell balance model. The 3D sub-models supply the balance model with the

wall thermal resistances for a specific cell design. Finally, the global cell balance model



                                                                     90

## PDF page 121

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


processes all the inputs and determines the resultant cell state: bath composition, mass

balance for each substance, associated heat balance and ledge thickness prediction.


    Sub-model 3:                         Sub-model 4:
    Electromagnetic Model                     Cell Top CFD Model




  Sub-model 2a: Bath Flow              Sub-model 1:
  Sub-model 2b: MHD Metal Flow       3D Electrical – Thermal Model




            Global Cell Mass and Energy Balance Model
               Substances’ concentrations;
               Calculation of bath temperature;
              Ledge movement.


                                   Figure 5-1: Models flowchart

      The  computational  flowchart  of  global mass and  energy  balance model

subroutines  is presented in Figure 5-2, for averaged steady state simulations. The

transient simulation algorithm will be discussed in section 6. The global balance model

is implemented in Visual Basic computational code named ―Cell Mass & Energy

Balance‖. The program solves mass and energy balances for a cell in averaged steady

state condition. The user must provide a set of data regarding: raw materials (gross and

net carbon consumption, alumina  impurities), mass flow inputs and outputs, and

measured cell parameters (voltage, current, CE). The thermal resistances obtained by

3D sub-models are inserted via an advanced user area.

         Figure 5-2: Global model computational flowchart (averaged steady state simulation)

      The algorithm is solved iteratively until all variables achieve convergence. In the

program, the user can easily modify operational parameters, such as: line current, bath

composition, cathode resistance, bath temperature, metal and bath depth, alumina

composition and other material input quantities for a new cell state calculation.

      The outcome  of  the  simulation can be  divided  into  five  sets  of  results

information:

            Primary Result 1: Mass balance of the cell detailing inputs and outputs

               for each relevant substance;

                                                                     92

## PDF page 123

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


            Primary Result 2: Energy balance of the cell based on the mass balance

               enthalpy, electrical energy inputs and energy dissipation losses;

            Complementary Result 1: Detailed emissions mass flow at cell duct,

               calculated composition and origin of the duct gases;

            Complementary Result 2: Cell top energy balance, which is the energy

              balance of sub-volume 3, chapter 4.3.3.

            Optional Result: Cell voltage breakdown and ACD prediction, useful for

             comparisons with historical methodologies based on voltage equations.

       Besides the accurate cell energy balance calculation, other possible applications

of the presented program are: determination of the cell duct gas chemical composition,

prediction of AlF3 correction needs, prediction of emission rates when modifying

alumina composition,  calculation of cathode cleaning impact on  the  cell energy

consumption. Improvements on the process operation can be virtually tested. Actions to

reduce energy consumption and emission rates can be studied and quantified.

        5.1.1  Cell Input Screens

      The program starts with the initial selection screen (Figure 5-3) which also

presents an illustration with the studied system and its main components (inputs and

outputs) considered in the model. The user must initially go to the cell input data screen

(Figure 5-4). There, the cell configuration  file containing the geometry dependent

parameters (thermal resistances, heat transfer coefficients) obtained from the sub-

models is read (*.txt input file).

       Additionally, the user will specify mass flow and energy inputs and outputs such

as: cell current, cell voltage, current efficiency, alumina composition, bath chemistry

targets, duct flow, prediction of gross and net carbon, anode change information, cover

addition mass and composition. Bath liquidus temperature and bath  resistivity are

calculated from the informed bath composition according to the chosen equations

(several options are provided from literature correlations). Alternatively, the user can

also provide  its own values for liquidus temperature and bath resistivity,  if better

correlations are available.




                                                                     93

## PDF page 124

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State





                                  Figure 5-3: Initial selection view screen





                                     Figure 5-4: Input data view screen

     From the input screen the user runs the simulation and, after the calculation is

done, the results are available to be assessed in the ―cell output screens‖ described in the



                                                                     94

## PDF page 125

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


next topic. Any parameter from the input screen can be changed and the simulation is

re-run in a few seconds.

        5.1.2  Cell Output Screens

       In the mass balance results screen (Figure 5-5), detailed mass flow of each

substance is presented in units of kg/ton Al. The source or destination of each mass

input/output stream is also identified and listed as detailed as possible. For example, the

total carbon anode input is broken down into the predicted consumption parcels: gross

carbon (total), net carbon, electrolytic consumption, air burn of carbon, sulphur impurity

mass, COS formation consumption and carbon dust. Of course, in steady state averaged

results, total mass inputs and total outputs must be equal, as observed in the output

screen.





                                  Figure 5-5: Mass balance results screen

     From the mass balance results screen, it is possible to have access to the detailed

emissions information (see Figure 5-6). Each one of the gases stream is detailed, the

production and consumption in each chemical reaction inside the control volume are

listed.


                                                                     95

## PDF page 126

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State





                            Figure 5-6: Detailed cell emissions mass flow screen

      The cell energy balance screen is shown in the Figure 5-7. Energy consumptions

are presented in kWh/kg Al at the left side of the screen. The total energy consumption is

divided into two major categories:

         -  Energy used for the mass balance: Heat up of materials and to promote

          chemical reactions. Energy used for electrolysis is separated from energy

          used in other processes. The enthalpy specifically gained by the duct gases is

           also identified.

         -  Energy dissipated  to the potroom, subdivided  in  shell  dissipation, top

           dissipation and cell external busbar dissipation.

      Heat generated in electrical conductors and dissipated by each cell region are

presented in kW at the right side of the screen. Using the heat transfer coefficients and



                                                                     96

## PDF page 127

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


thermal  resistances obtained from sub-models,  the bath superheat and  the ledge

thickness estimation are calculated and shown in the energy balance screen.





                         Figure 5-7: Energy balance and ledge thickness results screen

      The user shall verify if the numerical convergence was achieved, by checking

the % error field and the number of performed iterations. Normally, less than ten

iterations are necessary to achieve good convergence and the computational code stops

the process automatically when the convergence criterion is satisfied (error < 0.01%). In

extreme cases when convergence is difficult, the process stops after 100 iterations, and

the user is asked to verify the input data. Frequently, lack of convergence is a sign that

invalid or physical impossible conditions are proposed as input.

      The energy balance screen offers also access to the optional voltage breakdown

results screen (Figure 5-8), which includes the ACD estimation. In this work the voltage

calculations will not interfere in the energy balance as in classic models (Haupin, 1998).

However, the user might be interested to assess the ACD value and this can be

estimated by the ohmic bath voltage component.

       Voltages are calculated from the literature compiled by Haupin (1998). The bath

ohmic voltage drop is obtained from the total voltage minus the reversible voltage;


                                                                     97

## PDF page 128

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


bubble layer estimated voltage and estimated  polarisation voltages. ACD  is then

obtained from the bath ohmic voltage, effective interpolar area and bath resistivity.





                                Figure 5-8: Voltage and ACD estimation screen

      The equivalent voltage consumed to provide the chemical reactions enthalpy is

listed using two  different methods. The ―The Equivalent Energy Sink  to Make

Aluminium‖ voltage is obtained by the complete mass balance presented in chapter 4.

The ―The Equivalent Energy Sink by Haupin (1998)‖ is the voltage obtained by using

the widely known literature. It is expected to find higher values for the complete mass

balance equivalent voltage because  it accounts for many previously neglected mass

streams.

      The cell top heat balance screen is shown in Figure 5-9. It describes the energy

balance of the cell top control sub-volume, (see chapter 4.3.3). In the top right side of

the screen, the energy generated by each of chemical reactions inside the control volume

is listed. Below these values, the two energy sources provided by the cell cavity are

listed: the energy carried by the gases enthalpy evolved from the bath, and the heat

transfer of exposed surfaces such as cover, yokes and guide rods. All these energies are

summed up and after discounting the heat lost by the external hood surfaces, the energy


                                                                     98

## PDF page 129

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


absorbed by the duct gases is finally obtained. The duct mass flow will be the sum of

potroom air mass flow with the cell cavity emissions mass flow.





                      Figure 5-9: Cell top heat flow screen, energy balance of cell hood

      During the anode change period, the top heat balance is drastically deviated from

the averaged conditions. The cell hood is open and after the old anode is removed, the

bath is exposed to the ambient. This increases the heat losses during anode changing. In

the cell the energy balance, heat lost during this period is also accounted in a time

averaged basis.

        5.2.1  Mass Balance Results

      The information of Table 5-1 was inserted in the Cell Mass & Energy Balance

input files and the mass balance of each chemical substance was obtained. The Table

5-2 and Table 5-3 show the resulting mass flow rate of input and output materials,

respectively. The tables also include the enthalpy associated with each substance and

the temperature of its evaluation. Each mass stream (in bold characters) is subdivided

in order to provide information regarding the origin or fate of each parcel of the

substance. The detailed mass flow breakdown is useful for the understanding of the

relevancy of each parameter or process taking place in the cell.

       All enthalpy values shown in  this thesis are referenced to the substance’s

standard state at 25°C plus its enthalpy of formation, as shown in equation 4.3. For a

single element substance at standard state (e. g. O2) the enthalpy of formation is equal to

zero by definition. Other substances usually present negative enthalpy of formation at

standard state, which can be interpreted as the balance of the energy released in the

chemical reactions that have produced the substance from its basic elements.

        5.2.2  Energy Breakdown Results

      The cell energy consumption can be divided into three types, according to its

utilisation in the aluminium electrowinning process:

            1. Thermodynamic unavoidable energy consumption in order to satisfy the
             alumina reduction reaction enthalpy ( H ) and to heat the reactants (C,

             Al2O3) from the ambient temperature up to the reaction temperature

             (~960°C).

            2. Energy consumption of parallel reactions (reactions involving fluorides,

               sulphur, Al  reoxidation,  etc.) and  to  heat up  other  materials  not

             producing aluminium (alumina impurities, AlF3, excess of carbon, anode

               butt materials, cathode cleaning materials, duct gases, etc.).

            3. Energy dissipated by the cell external surfaces in the convection or

               radiation processes, responsible to maintain the  cell ledge and bath

              superheat at stable values.

      The cell energy consumption breakdown presented in Figure 5-10 was obtained

by the Cell Mass & Energy Balance Program for the case of Table 5-1 inputs. In this

cell, the energy consumption Type 1 represents 44.23% of the total consumption, which

also  represents  the  cell  energy  efficiency  defined  in  equation  (4.7).  As  a

thermodynamical requirement, this energy consumption is impossible to be reduced.

Actions must be focused on energy Type 2 and Type 3.

      The energy losses Type 3 are highly dependent on the cell thermal design and

can be modified only by changing the ledge thickness (and superheat) or by changing

the cell lining. The importance of the energy Type 2 in the cell energy balance is often

underestimated in the industry. The energy consumption Type 2 can be reduced by

smelter operation with careful control of cell mass balance streams, mainly cell duct

flow optimisation.





                                                                    104

## PDF page 135

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State



             Cell Energy Consumption Assessement, Mass Balance Method,
                              Line Current=180.7 kA
                4.60%


                                   44.23%

      The most important observed difference  is  that the  classic methods often

underestimate the energy consumed in minor chemical reactions and to heat up many

materials (Type 2 energy consumption) such as: alumina impurities, anode carbon and

other parts, fluoride additions, cover material and cathode cleaning materials. In the

classic method (Haupin, 1998), only the reoxidation reaction is taken into account

besides the electrolysis reaction. Comparing the energy necessary to heat up materials,

0.2 kWh/kg Al is neglected by the classical method. As a consequence, the classical

method also overestimates the predicted cell losses to the potroom, leading to lower

expected ledge thickness. For  this reason, thermal design of  cell lining could be

negatively affected when using the classical approach resulting in lower than necessary

thermal insulation of sidewalls.





                                                                    106

## PDF page 137

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


        5.2.4  Energy Losses Associated with Cell Duct Flow Rate

      The largest share of cell energy consumption Type 2 is carried by the duct gases

enthalpy collected by the potline exhaust system. Optimisation of the duct flow rate

represents an important opportunity to save energy. Unfortunately, the cell duct flow is

ignored in many traditional cell thermal models.

       In the previously presented case (180 kA cell) case, the duct flow rate can be
decreased from 5900 Nm3/h to 4000 Nm3/h and the new duct temperature is predicted by

the CFD sub-model 4 (Chapter 4.3.3) to be 20.5°C higher (168°C). Assuming the same

cell current, ledge thickness, shell heat flow and that there is room for ACD squeeze,

cell consumption can be reduced from 14.090 kWh/kg Al to 13.401 kWh/kg Al by

modifying the duct flow rate, see Table 5-5 results comparison for both duct flow rates.

         Table 5-5: Energy balance results using mass balance method, for different cell duct flow rates.

                                                   Duct Flow:         Duct Flow:
                                                    5900 Nm3/h         4000 Nm3/h
