      Note that the cell energy efficiency increased to 46.5% and the new cell voltage

was reduced to 4.141 V. In fact, the gas duct flow can be used to regulate cell heat

dissipation breakdown  in a limited  extent. The same  effect can be obtained by

modifying the cover thickness increasing the thermal resistance from the cell cavity to

the cell top space.



                                                                    107

## PDF page 138

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


      The results of Table 5-5 assume that the losses by convection and radiation

(Type 3 energy consumption) remained unchanged with the duct flow modification. The

exposed hood surfaces dissipate circa of 20% of the top energy balance (Figure 5-9). In

reality, it could be expected some increase in dissipation from cell top (convection and

radiation) as the hood temperature increases, reducing the effect of the duct flow

decrease in some extent.

        5.2.5  Other Cell Material Flow Energy Losses

      Around 0.35 kWh/ kg Al of cell energy is consumed by parallel materials heat up

and reactions (excluding air heat up) inside the cell. This fraction is important for

precise cell energy balance calculations. Energy is consumed by several processes

worthy of investigation: excess of carbon consumption, stored anode butt energy

including hot cover, yoke and guide rod, radiation from the open cavity exposition,

cavity cleaning material losses, alumina impurities heat up, aluminium fluoride heat up

and reaction.

       All these contributions can be evaluated in detail with the mass and energy

balance calculations. The Figure 5-11 shows the resulting energy losses associated with

each one of these processes for the studied cell technology:

         -  The back  reaction carbon consumption depends on  the  extent  of  the

           reoxidation reaction, which is strongly related to the interpolar distance. This

          carbon is heated up consuming 61 kWh/ton Al. Carbon is used in the regular

            electrolysis  first, producing CO2, which  in turn  is used  in reoxidation

            reaction. Mass and energy balance of both reactions have to be included in

           the correct energy waste calculation.

         -  The  net carbon consumption  that  is used  in  other  processes  besides

            electrolysis: Air burn, Boudouard reaction and carbon dust consumes 65

         kWh/ton Al to be heated up.

         -  Heat up of the carbon of the anode butt consumes another 60 kWh/ton Al.

         -   Other anode assembly parts heat up such as yoke, guide rod, cast iron and

         anode cover removed with the butt totalise 27 kWh/ton Al.

         -  During anode change, which is the period when the liquid bath is exposed,

           strong radiation loss occur. For the studied case, considering the time of

                                                                    108

## PDF page 139

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


          exposure and the area of the anode hole, the averaged loss is estimated at 13

         kWh/ton Al.

         -  Energy used to heat up the cathode top cleaning material plus the energy

          absorbed to heat up the bath scoop has achieved 46 kWh/ton Al in this case.

         -  The alumina impurities (Na2O and CaO) and the related AlF3 additions

          necessary to correct the bath acidity consume 76 kWh/ton Al. The value

           already includes the bath production reaction enthalpy balance.

         -  Bath is also produced by the cover spillage; in this case 50% of cover mass

             is made of crushed bath. 7 kWh/ton Al are consumed to heat up and melting

           of the cover bath powder.

         reactions

      The results obtained indicate several energy losses that can be minimised by

improving the cell operation and control of mass and energy inputs and outputs.

Examples in this direction are: reducing time spent per anode change, improving the

cover practices to generate less sludge, reducing gross and net carbon consumptions,

using alumina presenting lower levels of impurities, avoiding anodic short-circuits and

taking other actions that favour current efficiency.


                                                                    109

## PDF page 140

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


        5.2.6  Energy Losses Associated with Cover Practices

      The cover material balance impacts on the cell energy balance. In the present

studied case, 436 kg of cover material is added to the cell at each anode change. The

cover leaves the cell control volume in a variety of forms:

         -   Part of the cover remains on the anode butt top leaving the cell at anode

          change;

         -   Part falls in the bath, precipitates over the cathode blocks and leaves the cell

          during cathode cleaning in the form of sludge;

         -   Part falls into the bath and dissolves: 50% is alumina and it is consumed and

           the other 50% forms more bath increasing the cell bath level. Occasionally,

           the excess of bath is tapped out.

      Assuming that the cathode cleaning material composition (200 kg) is the same

as original cover, the share of each destination of the cover material is shown in Figure

5-12. The energy consumed in the heat up is represented by the orange bars.

Figure 5-12: Estimation of cover material fate in studied case and the energy lost with material flow

(orange bars and values)

      The energy loss associated with cathode cleaning material is the most relevant

because it comes out of the cell at almost the bath temperature (~960°C), while the

anode butt cover  is  at ~600°C. Bath production must be tapped out also at bath

                                                                    110

## PDF page 141

                5 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Averaged Steady State


temperature. The alumina content of cover dissolved into the bath contributes to make

aluminium and thus, by definition, no cell energy inefficiency comes from this process.

      5.3  Comparison of 3 Different Cell Technologies and Size


      The mass and energy balance was performed for three different cell technologies

using the modelling procedures described in this work. All necessary information was

provided by different smelters that asked to remain anonymous.

      For  the purposes of  this  thesis,  the  results  will be shown following the

nomenclature below:

     a) Case I is a 125 kA cell of an end-to-end technology;

     b) Case II is a 180 kA side-by-side, end-riser technology;

     c) Case III is a 350 kA side-by-side, side-riser cell, magnetically compensated.

      The mass and energy balance results were published by this author in the TMS
article (Gusberti et al. 2012). Note that Case II was published also in the 10th Australasia

conference (Gusberti et al. 2011).

        5.3.1  Energy Balance Results for the Three Cases

      The Table 5-6 shows the cell consumption results for the three cases, presenting

the energy divided by Type (1, 2 or 3) and including subdivisions for each type in

kWh/kg Al.

      Observing the results, it is shown that the total energy consumption decreased

and energy efficiency increased with the cell capacity, in agreement with the expected

outcome of contemporary industry practices. In the industry, there is a tendency of

increasing the new cell sizes, aiming to reducing costs by scaling production, which is

probably beneficial also in the point of view of the energy consumption. Bigger cells

present  better  relationship between  cell volume and surfaces  area, reducing heat

dissipation rates in units of kWh/kg Al.

loss, see study presented in section 5.2.4. However, some other cell conditions are

required to enable the duct flow reduction: possibility of ACD squeezing, enough under

pressure at cell top necessary to collect the cell emissions efficiently, possibility of

averaged cover thickness readjustment.

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation



     6  CELL MASS AND ENERGY BALANCE SOFTWARE  -

      DYNAMIC SIMULATION


      The model and software presented in section 5  is useful to calculate and

understand the averaged state of the electrolytic cell mass and energy balance. However,

real cells are never found in such ideal conditions. It is commonly known that thermal

disturbances applied to the cell take many hours, or even days to dissipate the produced

energy imbalance. This long timescale is caused by the large thermal capacity and the

diffusion time of the cell materials coupled with the phase change of the electrolyte.

      Thermal disturbances  in the present  cell technology are unavoidable. The

consumable anodes must be changed periodically. Addition of reactants and product

removal cannot be performed continuously. Additionally, the cell sometimes faces

variations on line current or voltage, modifying the energy input rate.

      The transient thermal behaviour becomes complex to predict by the existence of

the frozen ledge  that can melt  or  solidify according  to  local  temperature,  also

dynamically modifying the bath composition.

       In order to study the cell transient dynamics,  it is necessary to incorporate a

special code using the Finite Volume Method capable of tracking the ledge/bath thermal

and chemical interactions, and also including the thermal inertia effects of the other cell

solid volumes. The computational dataflow of the dynamic model is shown in Figure

6-1.  It is an expansion of the averaged steady state algorithm previously described

(Chapter 5).  It  is recommended starting the dynamic simulation from a converged

steady state solution because this is a numerically more stable situation. If required, it is

possible to start the transient calculation from a transitory cell state, provided the initial

heat  fluxes and energy or mass accumulation  rate  are known. Also,  the  initial

imbalances must not result in a thermal situation that is unbearable for the electrolysis

cell.

      The next subsections will describe in more detail how the ledge dynamics is

evaluated during the simulation using the transient mass and energy balance model,

including a brief review of the Finite Volume Method applied for solidification/melting

front,  the  so-called  ―Stefan  problem‖. The method  is  iterative  and  numerical

convergence must be achieved for each time step.

                                                                    115

## PDF page 146

          6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation





                                               Start





      External Submodel Input:                          External Submodel Input:

      6.1  Ledge Dynamics Simulation


        6.1.1  Problem Description and Boundary Conditions

       In 1997, Wei et al. published a one-dimensional dynamic ledge model, which

presented the following features:

               Moving grid phase change model - Fixed grid for the sidewall plus a

                deforming grid at the ledge region;

                Finite Difference Method was used;

               At the solidification/melting front, a specific latent heat boundary

                  condition was applied:

      Using the same numerical method and boundary conditions, Barantsev et al.

(2000) presented a similar one-dimensional dynamic ledge linked to a cell state model.

      They calculated the cell dynamic response for:

               Alumina feeding;

               AlF3 addition;

               Anode effect.

       Inspired by the work of Wei et al. (1997), the ledge dynamics can be inserted in

the existing mass energy balance model presented  in chapter  5. A moving grid

technique is adopted to track the solidification/melting front. Latent heat boundary

condition similar to Wei et al. (1997) is used.

       In this thesis, the Finite Volume Method was chosen to calculate the transient

ledge energy equation and movement. This method ensures the exact energy balance

calculation independent of the grid spacing once the convergence is achieved, which is

not guaranteed by Finite Difference and Finite Element methods. The simulations are

transient and an appropriated time step size must be chosen according to the process to

be studied. For example:

               Alumina addition: small time step is necessary ~1 s;

               Power modulation: time steps of 10 min might be sufficient.



                                                                    117

## PDF page 148

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      Linked with the solidification front movement, the mathematical model must

recalculate at each time step:

                Cell energy state and energy fluxes;

               Liquidus and bath temperature;

               Mass fluxes, concentrations, and accumulation of each substance.

       Figure 6-2 presents the schematic view of the cell walls calculation domain,

where the different materials are represented. The shell, insulation/refractory bricks are

considered as fixed geometry, whereas the ledge is a deformable geometry. At the

surfaces exposed to the potroom environment, the heat dissipation  is applied via

correlations found in the classical literature (Incropera and De Witt., 1985) for vertical

walls (convection) combined with radiation heat transfer laws.

      At the ledge-liquids interfaces, only convection heat transfer is applied. Two

different treatments are considered for the metal level and bath level. At the bath level

the heat transfer coefficient  is obtained by the methodology presented in section

4.3.1.1.1. Similarly, at the metal level the referred coefficient is obtained using the

methodology found in the section 4.3.1.1.3.  It is important to include at the ledge

boundary, the energy of the phase transformation (latent heat).





                   Figure 6-2: Boundary conditions applied to the dynamic ledge calculation




                                                                    118

## PDF page 149

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      The differential equation solved by the Finite Volumes Method is the energy

transport equation where the advective term represents the temperature transport by the

grid movement at the ledge domain, see equation (6.1). This approach was successfully

used earlier by some researchers (Wei et al.; 1997, Barantsev et al.; 2000).

                           T     x T  T       T 
        ( x, t ) cP ( x, t )    K ( x, t )          K ( x, t )                 (6.1)                            t     t  x   x       x 

      Boundary conditions at cell external walls:

                         T                               4        4
               K ( x, t )    hext (T  Tamb )  (T  Tamb )                  (6.2)
                          x

      At the ledge-bath interface (equation (6.3)) and ledge-metal interface (equation

(6.4)),   the  boundary   conditions   present  an   extra  term   representing   the

melting/solidification latent heat.

                             x                  T
           ( x, t ) L   hb (TB  TL )  K ( x, t )                        (6.3)
                                     dt                        dt

                            x                  T
           ( x, t ) L   hm (Tm  TL )  K ( x, t )                        (6.4)
                                    dt                        dt

     On both solidification fronts, the front temperature is the melting point:

                               T  TL                                    (6.5)

      The solved energy equation is transient and necessitates the initial condition for

all grid points.

                           T  T ( x,0)                                  (6.6)

       In this thesis, it is proposed to use the initial temperature field obtained by the

steady state calculation, which would represent the perfectly undisturbed operating cell.

However, it is possible to investigate other arbitrary initial conditions using the same

methodology, and to observe the cell thermal behaviour after such condition is applied.

        6.1.2  Finite Volume Method with Grid Movement

       In this section, a brief review of the Finite Volume Method applied to the energy

transport equation is presented. For more details, the specific literature is recommended

(Patankar; 1980; Versteeg et al.; 1995).


                                                                    119

## PDF page 150

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      The moving grid heat transfer equation (6.1) can be compared to the convective-

diffusive transient transport equation used in Finite Volumes Method, where the moving

grid is the convective term (6.7) in one-dimensional form.

                             T    T     2T
                             u       2                              (6.7)                             t    x    x

      The transport equation above is integrated over each finite volume and, in the

fully implicit transient formulation, it results in a set of algebraic equations, one for each

finite volume, of the type:

                          aPTP  aE TE  aW TW  aP0 TP0                          (6.8)

      Where:

     TP is the temperature at the finite volume ―P‖;

     TP0 is the temperature at the finite volume ―P‖ at the previous time step;

      a P is the transport coefficient for the calculated volume;

      a 0  is the transport coefficient for the calculated volume at the previous time
              P
step;

     TE and TW are the calculated temperatures at the neighbour volumes;

      a E and aW are the transport coefficient for the neighbour volumes;





            Figure 6-3: Description of variables and coefficients position in a 1D finite volume mesh

The transport coefficients ―a‖ represent the sum of diffusive and advective transport at

each interface between volumes. They are obtained from the material properties and

volume topology (Figure 6-3) as follows:


                                                                    120

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation

This is a fully implicit transient numerical method. It is iterative and intrinsically stable.

The operator ―[[ ]]‖ means the maximum of the two values.

      The diffusive parcels ―D‖ are obtained from thermal conductivity as follows:

                               k w                    k e                Dw          and   De                         (6.13)
               xw         xe

     And the advective parcels are obtained from mass fluxes:

                                                                                  (6.14)                  Fw   u  w                                     and  Fe   u  e

      The mesh Peclet number ―P‖ is the ratio between advective and diffusive heat

flux in a finite volume:

                         Fw           Fe                     Pw        and  Pe                           (6.15)
                     Dw         De

      The  interpolation function ―A‖  is  the  prediction of  the behaviour  of  the

temperature between two grid points. The classical ―central averaged‖ or ―upwind‖

functions are easy to implement, but in this work a more accurate interpolation function

was adopted, usually known as the ―Power Law‖ function:

                                                              5                      AF  P     0,  1  0.1 P                          (6.16)                                                           

       All the equations, (6.1) to (6.16), are applied to each finite volume generating a

system of algebraic equations. The system  is solved iteratively by a Gauss-Seidel

algorithm speeded up by a SOR technique (Successive Over-Relaxations).

      The solution algorithm is programmed in Visual Basic computational code and

three different test studies are presented in order to verify its stability and accuracy:



                                                                    121

## PDF page 152

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


Iteration convergence  study,  spatial mesh convergence study and temporal mesh

convergence study (time step size).

        6.1.3  Algorithm Testing – Iteration Convergence

      Within each time step, convergence of temperature field must be checked. Only

when the temperature residual is found to be below a prescribed criterion, the time step

is declared converged and the following time step calculation starts. The temperature

absolute residual is defined in equation (6.17).

                                              Tk 1( x )  Tk ( x )                     Rconv  max  abs                                    (6.17)                                                                                                               Tk ( x )      

      The residual is the maximum relative difference in temperature found among all

grid nodes (―x‖ positions) between two successive iterations, at the same node position.

If the convergence criterion is tight, a longer computational time is required to achieve

convergence and a better accuracy is expected. The choice of the criterion value is a

compromise between result quality and computational effort.

       In order to test the computational code stability, convergence and sensibility to

the parameter inputs, a test of cell current modification is proposed. From the averaged

steady state condition (Case II of section 5.3 was chosen), the line current is suddenly

decreased from 180.7 kA to 175.7 kA. The cell dynamic behaviour is to be calculated

during the next hours after the current decrease. It is assumed to be the unique mass and

energy perturbation during the simulation. Other parameters are: The  cell voltage

remains unchanged and equal to 4.44V during this simulation. Ledge is composed of

stoichiometric cryolite composition (3NaF*AlF3). The cell geometry and mass fluxes

are the same from Case II of section 5.3.

      The Figure 6-4 illustrates how the ledge thickness solution deteriorates when

using a convergence criterion lower than Rconv = 1e-4, see equation (6.17).

     When analysing the previous graphs, it is concluded that good solution accuracy

        6.1.4  Algorithm Testing - Mesh Convergence Study

          It is important to find the sufficient mesh size to represent the solution in space

(grid nodes distance and number). This is called the ―mesh convergence study‖.

      The ledge  thickness  evolution  versus time was  calculated using  different

numbers of grid points: 10, 20, 40, 80 and 160 nodes. The time step size was chosen as

120 s for all runs. The results for the ledge thickness at the sidewall bath level are

shown in Figure 6-6. The resulting thickness curve converges to its final shape when the

number of finite volumes increases. It has been noted that the solution using a low

number of finite volumes tends to be higher than the final solution. Above 40 finite

volumes, less than 1 mm error due to spatial discretization is found, which is considered

an acceptable range.

      The results are almost identical, for smaller time steps 1 s, 4 s, 36 s and 120 s.

Even using 360 s, good results were obtained. The numerical error becomes important

when using the 1200 s time step and the solution accuracy completely deteriorates at

3600 s time step. The best compromise between numerical accuracy and computational

effort seems to be found by setting the time step size around 360  s, for ledge

calculations. This conclusion might be different if we propose to study other process

variables such as alumina concentrations inside the bath, which will require smaller

timescales.

coefficient of the materials involved.

      6.2  Theoretical Studies of Cell Operation Dynamics

        6.2.1  AlF3 Variation Study

      The bath composition control is very important to obtain efficient cell operation.

The cell consumes the excess AlF3 dissolved in the bath at a nearly constant rate for

oxide neutralisation during cell operation. Fluoride emissions might be considered

constant, except during anode change procedures or any other procedure requiring the

cell top to be open. To maintain the bath composition and properties, the aluminium

fluoride content in the bath must be periodically corrected. In many cell technologies,

this procedure is done by dissolving a certain amount of AlF3 in the bath once a day.
Recent technologies present a special AlF3 feeder, which makes the fluoride correction

closer to continuous addition.

       In this study, the cell dynamic behaviour induced by the AlF3 corrections is

investigated using the dynamic model. It is assumed, by simplification, that fluoride

consumption is continuous and constant in time. The AlF3 additions occur once a day,

and the mass  is the  theoretical necessary in order  to compensate the one  day's

consumption. All other cell disturbances are neglected in this study.

      The following sequence of events is expected to occur (see Figure 2-13):

               AlF3 addition;

               T liquidus decreases, consequently superheat increases;

                Cell wall heat flux increases;

               Ledge melts down;

               AlF3 concentration decreases as bath volume increases;

               After enough time, the equilibrium concentration would become

                lower than immediately after the addition because the bath volume

                  increased;

      The following pictures present the simulation results for 10 days considering the

same cell technology of the previous study (Case II of chapter 5). The added mass is

22.85 kg AlF3/day. Figure 6-17 shows the excess AlF3 inside the bath during the

process (in mass).

                                                                    134

## PDF page 165

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      The behaviour is somewhat complex to understand because the mass of bath is

varying with the ledge meltdown and solidification. After the addition, % AlF3 suddenly

increases, and then two phenomena occur simultaneously:

    •  %AlF3 decreases by continuous consumption to neutralise Na2O, CaO, and HF

       evolution. It is a nearly constant consumption rate superimposed on the other

        variations.

    •  At some point the ledge  is increasing, reducing the liquid bath mass and

       increasing the %AlF3 of remaining bath. There are moments when the rate of the

       concentration increase by the volume variation  is greater than the  rate of

       concentration decrease caused by the standard cell consumption.

       This dynamic behaviour directly affects the liquidus temperature, shown in

Figure 6-19. The variation amplitude is around 2.5 °C. Here, the liquidus temperature is

directly affected by AlF3 only, as the calcium fluoride and alumina concentrations are

kept constant during the dynamic model simulation.

      The superheat movement in the bath temperature versus AlF3 diagram caused by

fluoride additions  is shown in Figure 6-21. The cell states variation  is cyclically

repeated each 24h; the horizontal displacement of the blue line corresponds to the

addition and dissolution of the fluoride that is fast compared to the thermal diffusion

timescale.

       Afterwards, because the superheat increased, the bath volume increases as the

ledge melts down. This is represented by the blue line returning to a closer position in

relation to the T liquidus line. Later, the fluoride concentration slowly decreases by the

cell standard consumption. At the same time that the bath temperature slowly increases

before the next addition, responding to the bath chemistry change and trying to

equilibrate the cell heat losses.

      The results of this study show that the cell dynamics is affected by the AlF3

addition  strategies. The  additions  should be performed  as  close  as  possible  to

continuous to decrease their induced perturbations. Therefore, the use of dedicated AlF3

feeders is important for achieving a more stable bath chemistry.

        6.2.2  Power Modulation Study

        Electrical energy is one of the biggest costs of the aluminium reduction process.

In some regions of the world the energy tariffs are a function of the hour in the day in a

24h cycle. Normally the hours of peak consumption in the evening are more expensive,

and overnight the electrical energy starts to become cheaper. In this context,  it  is

worthwhile for the smelters to modulate the power inputs in order to decrease the

averaged energy cost.

      Power modulation has been applied in the practice by some smelters with some

reported success, for example, at Valesul – Brazil (Nunes et. al., 1998) and at ALCOA

Warrick (Kuhn and Malcolm, 2009). Stam and Schaafsma (2007) tested different power

modulation setups at Aluminium Delfzijl – Netherlands. They reported some difficulties

during the low energy input period: excessive freeze of bath, low superheat with

consequential poor alumina dissolution. After a long power modulation period, the cited


                                                                    139

## PDF page 170

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


smelter reported deterioration of the controllability of the bath levels, bottom ridging,

metal quality decrease, anode spike occurrence due to low temperature/superheat. The

standard deviation of measured bath and liquidus temperature increased from 0.7°C to

1.4°C. Important reductions in power cost were reported.

       In another study, Reek (2011) carried out tests done at TRIMET Hamburg -

Germany to determine the effect of increased or decreased energy input to the cells, and

as well  as  the time necessary  for  the  cell  to  return  to  stable  conditions. Bath

temperature, liquidus temperature and bath superheat were monitored. The approach

presented by the cited author is to keep track of the energy accumulated during the

different cell states. Limits on cell accumulated energy state variation were set in order

to maintain the bath temperature between 955-970°C. Good cell performance was

reported maintaining the modulation inside the theoretical window.

      With the help of the cell dynamic model, it is possible to investigate the impact

of power modulation in the cell transient behaviour. The power input is applied at each

time  step, following the modulation function, and the  resulting perturbations  are

obtained.

       In this study, four different modulation functions are applied to the model as

shown in Figure 6-25. The general boundary conditions adopted are:

               Averaged line current is 180.7 kA, the cell design is equal to the

                 previous study.

               Line current amplitude is 20 kA in a 24 h modulation cycle.

               No other transient effects are included.

               The cell voltage remains unchanged during this simulation 4.44 V.

               Time step size: 360 s.

      The power modulation  is a  cyclical  process,  thus  the behaviour of bath

temperature/AlF3 is repeated at each period. Clearly, in the modulation function 1, the

trajectory of cell states presents less variation represented by the smaller cycloid area.

The fraction of the cycloid area below the superheat line  is also the smallest  at

modulation function 1, in accordance with the observations of the superheat graphs. The

movement is always counter clockwise in the graphs due to the nature of the process.

                                                                    150

## PDF page 181

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      The variance in the applied power modulation  is the lowest in function 1,

followed by function 2. Functions 3 and 4 present the highest variances. This might

explain also why the function 1 is the best, followed by function 2.

the resulting shell temperature in the sidewall bath region.

      With the help of the present dynamic modelling of the cell,  it is possible to

develop countermeasures in order to mitigate the effects of power modulation on the

bath superheat.

        6.2.4  Voltage Management of Power Modulation

      The voltage compensation of power modulation is one strategy that can be used

to maintain the bath superheat during the power modulation. It consists in increasing

ACD at the same time as the cell current is decreased. If the bath energy generation

surplus  is maintained, the superheat and ledge thickness will be stable along the

modulation  cycle.  This  approach  results  in power  modulation  amplitude  to be

proportionally smaller than the cell electrical current modulation amplitude. Using the

cell technology currently available, it is estimated that around 25% of the modulated

energy (via electrical current) would be sufficient to maintain the bath superheat and

freeze at constant levels. The voltage compensation results in a slight increase in the

energy consumption expressed in kWh/kg Al because during the low energy period, less

                                                                    153

## PDF page 184

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


aluminium  is produced combined with high ohmic energy at ACD. The intention,

however, is to decrease the total financial cost of energy by using the cheaper energy

available.

      Eisma and Patel (2009) published experiments of power modulation using

voltage  compensation  strategy  to  stabilise  the  cell  heat  balance.  Their  voltage

compensation  was  insufficient  to  avoid  bath  temperature  waves.  The  voltage

compensation along with other measures was also successfully used to reduce the cell

current maintaining heat balance in the work of Peyneau et al. (2013).

      The dynamic model was  applied  to  study  situations where  the power

modulation is combined with ACD readjustment to maintain superheat. The line current

modulation function 3 (stepped 12 h) was chosen for this study, see Figure 6-25. Several

different voltage compensation scenarios were applied to the ACD, identifying the

optimum  voltage  to compensate  the power  modulation. The energy modulation

parameters for each case are listed in Table 6-1.

  Table 6-1: Power modulation cases using voltage management to compensate heat losses at the ledge,

                                   current modulation amplitude 20kA


   Average Cell      Average Cell    Current Modulation  Voltage Modulation  Power Modulation

      Current           Voltage          Amplitude          Amplitude         Amplitude

       [kA]              [V]              [kA]             [mV]            [kW]

      180.7            4.44            20              0               88.8

      180.7            4.44            20             100              70.7

      180.7            4.44            20             130              65.3

      180.7            4.44            20             132              64.9

      180.7            4.44            20             160              59.9

      180.7            4.44            20             240              45.4

      The Figure 6-47 shows the superheat behaviour in each case during 120h of

process corresponding to five modulation cycles.

      Analysing the superheat results, clearly there is an optimum value for the voltage

modulation to compensate the cell current modulation. In this particular case, 132 mV

was found  to be the optimised voltage modulation amplitude  that maintains the

superheat, bath heat losses and ledge thickness at constant levels. It is observed that for

modulated voltages higher than 132 mV, an antiphase perturbation was induced by the

excess of voltage variation. In the Figure 6-48 the calculated cell states are presented in

the bath temperature/AlF3 space.

      The  behaviour  is  cyclical,  repeating  itself  each 24  hours, which  is  the

modulation wave period. Negative superheat is observed in two cases where the cycloid

crosses the T liquidus red line: the case without voltage compensation and the 240 mV

voltage compensation. The optimum voltage compensation (132 mV) is represented by

accumulated points at the same place, which is the ideal undisturbed cell behaviour.

                                                                    156

## PDF page 187

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


          It  is important to remark that other cell disturbances are neglected in this

particular study, such as, fluoride additions, metal tapping, anode change, MHD effects

and anode effects. Those would certainly modify the superheat, either by changing the

bath chemistry or by creating an energy imbalance inside the cell control volume. In

real cells the graph of Figure 6-48 would always be dynamic, never stabilising to one

single position.

      The relationship between the modulated power and the resulting superheat

amplitude  is shown  in  Figure  6-49. The  constant  voltage  case (88 kW power

modulation) produced 14°C  variation  in  superheat,  while  decreasing  the power

modulation to 64.9 kW by introducing voltage compensation of 132 mV  totally

cancelled the superheat variation.

resulting freeze profile.

        6.2.5  Shell Forced Cooling versus Power Modulation

       In 2011, Lavoie et al. studied the shell forced cooling as a way to increase the

modulation window in aluminium reduction cells. They measured the shell external

temperature for different air flow velocities, using the same forced cooling technology

previously presented by Nambothiri et al. (2009). It was proven there, that the shell

temperature is highly affected by the forced cooling.



                                                                    158

## PDF page 189

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      Using a steady state energy balance model, they (Lavoie et al.; 2011) verified

that the ledge thickness would be sensitive to the cooling of the shell, allowing for a

wider power modulation window. However, in the referred article, the transient effects

not accounted  in  their  simulations could change  the  conclusions,  if  the power

modulation period was too short. In such cases the time necessary for the heat travelling

through the ledge and sidewalls would be greater than the modulation period and the

sidewall cooling would be useless.

      With the help of the present cell dynamic model, the forced cooling effects on

power modulation can be studied. The line current modulation function 3 (stepped 12 h)

was chosen for this study, see Figure 6-25. Several cases with different wall heat

transfer coefficients ―h‖ were calculated as shown in Table 6-2, representing different

forced cooling intensities, applied only when the cell current is at a high level (190.7
kA). The base case (―h‖= 7.5 W/m2/°C) represents the cell without forced cooling. No

voltage compensation is applied.

                              Table 6-2: Shell forced cooling cases

   Average Cell                       Current Modulation                          Shell-Air Heat

      Current          Cell Voltage        Amplitude      Modulation Cycle  Transfer Coefficient
       [kA]              [V]              [kA]                   [h]            [W/(m2°C)]

      180.7            4.44            20             24                7.5

      180.7            4.44            20             24             15

      180.7            4.44            20             24             30

      180.7            4.44            20             24             100

      180.7            4.44            20             24             200

      The Figure 6-51 shows the resulting external shell temperature for each of Table

6-2  cases. The temperature  at the sidewall bath  level (Shell T SB)  is used  for

comparisons.

         Little influence of forced cooling on ledge thickness is observed in the dynamic

model  results. Apparently, the timescale of the power modulation  is too short to

accommodate the forced cooling thermal effects because the heat wave takes many

hours to  travel through the sidewall. In addition, the  shell external heat transfer

coefficient is not the major thermal resistance of the system. The total change in the

wall  thermal  resistance by manipulating  shell  cooling  is  limited  to ~20%-30%

depending on the ledge position.

      The shell forced cooling alone  is not capable of compensating the power

modulation in a 24h period. This conclusion disagrees with the observations and

calculations published by Lavoie et al. (2011). There are two reasons why a different

outcome has been found here:

             In this thesis a dynamic model was used instead of a steady state heat

              balance used by Lavoie et al. (2011).

                                                                    163

## PDF page 194

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


             In the present work, the isolated effect of forced cooling was studied

             while  in  the  referred   article,  another  modification   is  applied

              simultaneously: bath voltage was increased by widening the ACD,

             compensating part of the power modulation.

      The shell forced cooling strategy might be useful for capacity creep, where the

changes are not cyclical based, and slowly implemented in incremental changes in the

heat balance.

        6.2.6  AlF3 Additions versus Power Modulation with Voltage Compensation

       In  section  6.2.4,  it has been shown how  the power modulation can be

compensated with a certain amount of voltage increase at ACD, with the objective of

maintaining the cell heat balance stability.

       In this section, the voltage compensation is combined with intermittent fluoride

additions and its effect on the cell dynamic behaviour is investigated. Two cases are

proposed:

        a)     Dumps of AlF3 each 32 h, equal to the theoretical consumption of the

                   cell during the period (30.6 kg). Current amplitude 20 kA, voltage

               compensation of 132 mV;

       b)     Dumps of AlF3 each 32 h, four dumps weighing the triple of the

                  theoretical consumption followed by a period without additions (91.8

                  kg). Current amplitude 20 kA, voltage compensation of 132 mV. Some

                 smelters occasionally increase the addition dump weight aiming to

                decrease the bath temperature. More discussions about this procedure

                 are found in Section 6.3.2.2.

       Figure 6-56 presents the superheat obtained during the process for both cases. In

the first hours after the addition, the superheat is disturbed and then it reaccommodates

again to the initial value.

      The examples of scenarios a) and b) show how the thermal equilibrium obtained

by a nearly perfect voltage compensation of power modulation can be disrupted by

fluoride additions, which represents a chemical imbalance of the process causing an

energy imbalance.

      The scenario b) strategy  is observed in many smelters where the fluoride

addition mass is very different from the theoretical consumption. Process engineers

erroneously believe  that  it  is possible  to  control the  cell heat balance by AlF3
overfeed/underfeed. In Section 6.3.2.2, simulations using a real cell AlF3 input data will

be shown and discussed.

      6.3  Dynamic Model used to Study Real Cells


        6.3.1  Cell Dynamic Simulation of Real Cells: Case 1 - 145kA Cell

      The relevant cell data concerning the historical mass and energy inputs of a few

electrolysis cells was obtained for a 145 kA cell technology, with the objective of

employing the dynamic model to study the cell transient response. An example of such

data from one of the studied cells is listed in Table II-1 of Annex II.

    A version of the dynamic model was programmed to include the capability of

reading a text file containing: voltage versus time, line current versus time and AlF3

addition versus time. The program applies all these values at the specified times as mass

and energy inputs. In the results, it is possible to see the dynamic consequences of those

inputs on bath temperature, superheat, %AlF3, ledge thickness and shell temperature.

                                                                    166

## PDF page 197

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


       Unfortunately, the voltage and cell current inputs are an average of 24h. This

might be a too long window; and the results should be carefully interpreted. The

aluminium fluoride is added each 24h. The calcium fluoride concentration in the bath

was considered constant during the period.

      The cell geometry and material properties were also provided to perform all

simulations. One of the cells was chosen in 85 days time window for analysis (age 1015

to 1100). The next graphs (Figure 6-59 to Figure 6-61) show graphically each one of the

energy/mass inputs for the same studied cell, data provided by the smelter.

        6.3.1.1  Bath Temperature: Dynamic Model Simulation versus Measurements

      The Figure 6-62 shows the bath temperature obtained by the dynamic model for

the cell using the above inputs for voltage, current and fluoride additions, compared

with the temperature measured by the smelter. As measurements were usually taken at

each two days, many values were repeated in order to obtain a plot with one point at

each 24 h. The bath temperature measurements done in real cells have some reliability

limitations as discussed by Whitfield et al. (2001):  It is normally a manual process

subjected to several sources of error:

            Variation in the depth of immersion of the sensor;

            The crust must be broken and the bath is exposed, cooling the bath

                 locally;

             Spatial temperature variations inside the bath, caused by the setting of

           new cold anodes, which can be minimised, if the measured location and

              time is correlated with the position of the last anode change.


                                                                    168

## PDF page 199

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


       Despite the difficulties listed in the previous paragraph to obtain accurate bath

temperature, reasonable agreement between measurements and dynamic simulation was

obtained for this case, even considering that some assumptions applied to the dynamic

model must be remembered: anode change is not included in the simulation, cell duct

flow was maintained constant along the simulated time and bath mixing is very fast

compared with the duration of the simulated phenomena and simulation objectives.

       In the dynamic model, one time step is equal to 6 minutes of process. Measured

temperatures were obtained at each two days, explaining why part of the thermal

dynamics found in the model response was not present in measurements, some fast

temperature variations after voltage variations or after fluoride additions, for example.

      Measured and calculated bath temperatures follow the applied electrical power,

with a certain drift between day 1065 and day 1095. The drift is explainable by the

changes in bath chemistry, mainly fluoride concentration. Once the concentration

decreases, the liquidus temperature increases and the bath temperature must also

increase to maintain the same superheat and cell thermal balance. The bath temperature

readjustment may take several hours to reflect the chemistry changes.

        6.3.1.2  Results: Bath Temperature versus Excess AlF3

       Figure 6-65 presents the dynamic model results for AlF3 concentration versus

bath temperature. As expected, an inverse  correlation can be seen. The fluoride

concentration inside the bath is the ratio between the excess fluoride inventory and the

total bath inventory. Fluoride  is added periodically and, simultaneously, the bath

inventory is changing dynamically because the ledge is melting or solidifying. In this

particular simulation, when the bath temperature increased, part of the ledge melted

down, diluting the %AlF3. Figure 6-66 the shows the measured bath temperature versus
the excess AlF3 concentration.

       In the graph one point each day is plotted. In the smelter, the bath temperature

measured each 4 days. Values are repeated and averaged in order to obtain one point per

day. Even though the measurement frequency is limited, the inverse correlation of bath

temperature versus fluoride concentration is also noticeable in the graph, similar to the

observations from the dynamic model results.

     One visual  interesting analysis  is to track how the  cell moves inside the

temperature/AlF3 operational window presented in Figure 2-11. Figure 6-67 presents the

measured  cell  states compared with  the  bath  chemistry optimal window  (green

polygon). It is observed that the cell is floating from low to high superheat on many

occasions; in one opportunity very high superheat is found, more than ~30°C while in

other moments, low or even negative superheat  is found. Those low or negative

superheat points are likely to produce sludge at the cathode top of the cell since the

alumina dissolution process  is endothermic.  It needs to extract energy from bath

superheat.

      The majority of the measured  state points  is outside the optimal window

boundaries, caused either by AlF3 imbalances or by energy imbalances produced by

power input variations.

     As mentioned before, the measurement frequency is limited and many cell states

between two points are unknown. The transition between two states is represented here

by a straight line, but the reality would be very different.

       In the dynamic model, however, one point is produced in each six minutes of the

process and the frequency could be made higher if necessary. The results obtained

during the dynamic simulation are plotted in Figure 6-68. It is noted that the range of

cell states is wider in the simulation than it is in the measurements, probably due to

measurements frequency limitation. The fluoride additions can be identified as straight

line ―jump‖ in the ―x‖ coordinate direction. The bigger cell excursions are represented

by the longer cycloid curves leading to important modifications in the cell state.

     Figure 6-68: Calculated bath temperature versus AlF3 (wt %) using the provided cell input data





                                                                    173

## PDF page 204

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


       Similar to the measurements graph, more than half of the points are located

outside the green optimal window. It is possible to see zones where the superheat is

negative (below the red line). The higher calculated superheat reaches more than 25°C.

      The negative superheat issue deserves a reflection. Despite the fact that the

theory would indicate the impossibility of sustaining an operating cell in such condition,

negative superheat observations during long periods in real cells (many hours) are not

rare. Haupin (1992) called this inconsistency a ―Liquidus Enigma‖. He proposed five

hypotheses explaining why the electrolysis cells can operate below the theoretical

liquidus temperature for extended periods:

         -   There are impurities in the bath not considered in bath chemical analyses.

          Normally, all impurities lower the bath melting point;

         -   Limitations on bath composition measurement accuracy;

         -   Limitations on bath temperature measurement accuracy, including spatial

            variations;

         -  Bath has the ability to remain supersaturated without freezing for a long

           period;

         -   Solid cryolite may be suspended in the bath influencing the bath composition

         measurements obtained from samples.

     Moxnes et al. (2006) have compared direct superheat measurements done using

Heraeus probe (Verstreken and Benninghoff, 1996) with the bath sample analyses. They

discovered that the bath composition error in bath sample analyses is the main source of

superheat underestimation. In the present simulation, however, the superheat results are

obtained from mass balance calculations and the model  is not dependent on bath

composition measurements avoiding the mentioned error.

      The model suggests that negative superheat really happens in the real cells,

perhaps less frequently and during shorter times than calculated by bath composition

sample analyses. In the simulations, the negative superheat occurs in periods when the

cell voltage is decreased too fast and the bath heat generation is lower than the heat

losses. Eventually, after some ledge growing, superheat comes back to the positive

range.



                                                                    174

## PDF page 205

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


     Kwande (2004) pointed out that values measured by the superheat probes are

always positive. Possibly, the sludge precipitation occurring at low superheat periods is

capable of maintaining superheat values always positive. Alumina dissolution and

precipitation processes are not included in the present dynamic model.

          It is interesting to analyse the bath mass variation compared with the ledge mass

variation and the mass of fluoride excess in the bath during the simulated period. Figure

6-69 presents the dynamic model results during the simulated period for bath mass,

AlF3 mass and ledge mass inside the studied cell. Note that AlF3 spikes in mass are
related to additions, and the AlF3 consumption rate is nearly constant. The AlF3 mass

almost halved during the 7 days period when no fluoride was added (days 1076-1083).

Bath mass and ledge mass are complementary in the cell and their variation is strongly

influenced by power input.

                                          bath (magenta)

       In  the  smelters,  the  fluoride concentration  is measured  in order  to have

information about bath chemistry and to decide the next cell control steps. However, the

real mass of fluoride inside the cell is difficult to estimate because the amount of

freeze/bath must be also known. The present dynamic model could become a valuable

tool to improve cell control procedures based on the real mass balance of the substances

involved in the process.



                                                                    175

## PDF page 206

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


        6.3.1.3  Ledge Thickness

      The variations in energy input to the cell are reflected in the ledge thickness

dynamics. In the Figure 6-70, the transient ledge thickness is compared with the applied

cell power. As expected, when the voltage rises, the thickness decreases and vice versa.


       0.2                                                                      760

                                         Ledge SB[m]

  [m] 0.14

     0.12                                                                                                                                                                  [kW]

       0.1                                                                      680                                                                                                                                                                                                           Power       Thicknesss
     0.08
    Ledge 0.06                                                                     660 Cell
                                                                              640

                 1010  1014  1018  1022  1026  1030  1035  1039  1043  1047  1051  1055  1059  1063  1067  1071  1075  1080  1084  1088  1092  1096
                                            Cell Age [days]


               Figure 6-70: Ledge thickness (sidewall-bath level ―SB‖) versus applied cell power

      The variations in bath chemistry are reflected only in short scale variations (few

hours) in the ledge thickness. In long timescale analysis, the cell power input is the

dominant factor in the sidewall heat balance. The freeze control of the cell should have

a  record  of  the  total  energy  input  history,  enabling  prediction  of  the  heat

balance/imbalance state of the cell control volume. A measurement of the ledge shape

gives only information about the current ledge position, but no indication of the

direction of the ledge movement. The freeze can be considered a thermal buffer with a

large timescale delay. Energy is being stored when the ledge is melting, and energy is

being consumed as ledge freezes.

      The dynamic simulation could be used to foresee the ledge movement, according

to the cell energy input history. The cell control could use such information to improve

the next actions and countermeasures in order to make the cell operation more stable

from the thermal balance point of view and, consequently, more efficient.


                                                                    176

## PDF page 207

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


        6.3.2  Cell Dynamic Simulation of Real Cells: Case 2 - 170kA Cell

      Another application example of the cell dynamic modelling is presented in this

section for a 170 kA electrolysis cell. The necessary cell mass and energy input data

was provided by the smelter. An example of such data is listed in the Annex I and

Annex II.

       Similarly to the previous analysis, the dynamic model was programmed to

include the capability of reading a text file containing: voltage versus time, line current

versus time and AlF3 addition versus time. The program applies all these values at the

specified times. In the results, it is possible to see the dynamic consequences of those

inputs on bath temperature, superheat, %AlF3, ledge thickness and shell temperature.

      The voltage and cell current are an average of 24 h and the results should be

interpreted accordingly. The aluminium fluoride  is added each 32 h. The calcium

fluoride concentration in the bath was considered constant during the simulated period.

      The next graphs (Figure 6-71 to Figure 6-73) show graphically each one of the

energy/mass inputs for the same studied cell. The data was provided by the smelter.

             Figure 6-74: Applied voltage (green) and calculated bath temperature (blue)

       In the model, the bath temperature follows the applied voltage with a certain

delay. This is an expected outcome if the cell is stable; the fluoride additions also affect

the bath temperature, but only in the first hours after the addition. In fact, the total

energy input is largely dependent on cell voltage, and much less dependent on fluoride

additions. These results are in line with the results obtained in the previous analysis, the

145 kA cell.

     One effect that is not represented in the model is the case when ACD is squeezed

and the cell starts to short-circuit. This phenomenon can be manifested in different

forms, according to the CE loss mechanisms described in chapter 2.3.1:

            Metal pad waves driven by the magnetic forces;

            Anode spikes. These are carbon protuberances that are directly touching

               the metal pad, driving a great part of the anode current directly from

             anode to cathode.

            Aluminium droplets that touches the anode and are directly oxidised.

            The net effect is then equivalent to a short-circuit.

       In such situations, the voltage is lowered (by squeezing ACD) aiming to reduce

the cell energy input in an attempt to reduce the bath temperature. But the ACD net

energy generation in the bath increases because part of the energy is not consumed in



                                                                    179

## PDF page 210

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


electrolysis anymore. Instead of making aluminium this energy is being manifested as

ohmic heat generation, heating the bath up even more than desirable.

       This  is probably occurring in this studied  cell. The Figure 6-75 shows the

measured values of bath temperature versus cell voltage.


                                                  Cell 433 - Data from Smelter

     An inverse correlation between measured temperatures and cell voltage was

observed. This fact indicates that the cell might be losing current efficiency by short-

circuiting. Probably the cell is running below its ACD limit. The observed temperature

excursions are large, more than 40°C.

        6.3.2.2  Results: Bath Temperature versus Excess AlF3

       Figure  6-76  shows  the model  results  for  AlF3  concentration  and  bath

temperature. An inverse correlation has been found.  It can be explained by the

following sequence of events: If bath temperature increases, ledge might be melting

down, diluting the %AlF3 and vice versa (in the model, ledge composition is eutectic).

This inverse correlation has been found also in smelter measurements, see Figure 6-77.

        6.3.2.3  AlF3 Addition versus Superheat

      At the smelter, fluoride is added each 32h. Figure 6-78 presents the resulting

superheat during the process calculated using the dynamic model (continuous line) and

the AlF3 addition dumps in [kg]. Peaks in superheat are observed after the fluoride

addition.

      The smelter operation strategy is to calculate the addition mass according to bath

temperature, which seems to be an erroneous approach. This will cause unnecessary


                                                                    181

## PDF page 212

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


variation in the % AlF3 in the cell, since its consumption rate is nearly constant in time

and predictable.

      Another factor influencing superheat is the voltage variation, which explains the

occurrence of negative superheat in two occasions. The negative superheat coincides

with the rapidly dropping in cell voltage, see Figure 6-74. In this situation, formation of

sludge over the cathodes is expected. The alumina feeding needs a positive superheat to

be heated up and dissolved into the electrolyte. In fact, many smelters report sludge

formations when the cell are operating at the energy input limit.

       In order to isolate the effect of the fluoride addition mass, the same case was

rerun assuming constant cell power input (constant voltage and line current). The strong

variations in addition mass and superheat are shown in Figure 6-79 and Figure 6-80.

After the addition, the superheat rapidly increases because T liquidus is decreased. After

some time, the cell readjusts its thermal resistances, by forming/melting ledge, bringing

the superheat back to nearly the same previous value, as shown in the zoom graph

(Figure 6-80). A bigger addition results in a stronger superheat peak.

          It seems that such variation in AlF3 additions (from 19 kg to 113 kg!) may

induce bath chemistry fluctuations during time, affecting T liquidus, superheat and

thermal balance, actually causing thermal excursions instead of improving the cell

transient behaviour.

      The diagram of measured bath temperatures versus  fluoride concentration

presented in Figure 6-81 reveals that the cell was operating systematically outside the

                                                                    183

## PDF page 214

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


optimum  operational window. The diagram confirms  that  the  control system  is

overreacting to the measurements because most of measured points are bouncing from

states of low fluoride/high bath temperature directly to states of high fluoride/low

temperature, probably caused by pot  control  overreaction with  irregular  fluoride

addition mass.

   Figure 6-81: The bath chemistry states measured by the smelter (green region=desired operational

                                   region) in temperature/AlF3 space

      The cell thermal stability could be improved by adopting a cell chemistry control

strategy in line with the 9-Box Control Concept proposed by Iffert (2007) in his thesis.

It requires more measurements and the use of a superheat sensor, but  it is able to

distinguish an energy imbalance from a mass imbalance in the bath.

       This concept was applied with success in industrial cells by Trimet AG (Iffert,

2007). It is a routine based on the understanding of the mass and energy balance, which

obeys fundamental laws of thermodynamics in opposition to, for example, the fuzzy

logic found in the literature (Soares, 2010), which aims to control the cell fluoride

additions based on historical operational decision patterns.



                                                                    184

## PDF page 215

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      The dynamic model  could  help  to improve 9-Box  Control Concept by

forecasting the cell probable state and fine tuning the controller actions.

        6.3.2.4  Ledge Thickness

happens to the ledge dynamics, under such operational practices applied to the cells. In

Figure 6-82, the transient ledge thickness is compared with the applied cell voltage. As

expected, when the voltage rises, the thickness decreases.

       Again, the predicted bath temperatures present a different shape from the

measurements at some time windows. It is possible that the cell lost current efficiency

by metal shorting (MHD or spikes) at some time frames, explaining why at low voltage,

the bath temperature increased and the model was not able to catch the phenomenon.

       Nevertheless, if the calculated ledge behaviour for the informed cell conditions

are compared with measured Fe contamination values shown in Figure 6-88,  it  is

observed that when the cell voltage was high, the ledge almost vanished and, at the

same time, an increase on Fe contamination was measured.

                                                                    188

## PDF page 219

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation





 Figure 6-88: Above: Transient ledge thickness obtained by the dynamic simulation (EM=endwall metal

  level, SM= sidewall metal level). Below: Cell voltage (green) and measured Fe contamination (Black

                                      dots) at the same time frame

       In view of such results, a sequence of events is proposed to explain why the cell

lining failed.

               The high voltage period increased the energy generation inside the

                      cell;

               The thermal imbalance was long enough to melt down the protective

                 ledge at the sidewall lining or endwall lining;

                                                                    189

      The two points inside the negative superheat zone correspond to the same week

          It is difficult to find an explanation for the measured temperature and bath

composition  state of the referred points. One hypothesis could be the sludge re-

dissolution, which would increase the alumina content of the bath and lower the T

liquidus. After some time, bath temperature would have been reduced combined with

high AlF3.

       Perhaps the ledge really disappeared and the cell insulation in the sidewalls and

endwalls were compromised. The  thermal consequences  in  this  case would be

catastrophic and almost unpredictable.

      The dynamic model did not produce an explanation for the low temperature

points, as measured behaviour was not obtained in the model results of the Figure 6-91,

which represents the same period of the measured graph.
