      The model considers the alumina concentration as constant in long timescales;

this could only be achieved by good feeding control combined with low sludge

formation and dissolution rates.

        6.3.3.1  Discussion

          It has been shown that the dynamic model is a very useful tool to study many

aspects of cell behaviour:

                It can be used to study and optimise feeding strategies of alumina and

                   fluorides;

                It can be used to anticipate the consequences of variation of energy

                   inputs, voltage or power modulation;

                It can be applied to investigate cell failure causes.


                                                                    192

## PDF page 223

                  6 - Cell Mass and Energy Balance Software - Dynamic Simulation


      The simulations presented here are useful to study large timescale phenomena

such as ledge movement, because the informed cell data are 24h averaged (voltage,

current) and addition intervals are 32h (AlF3).

      However, the model is capable of handling smaller timescales and it is useful for

studying other dynamic aspects of the cell: alumina concentration, anode effects, etc.





                                                                    193

## PDF page 224

                                                          7 - Conclusions



     7 CONCLUSIONS


      The present thesis demonstrates that the modelling of the cell mass and energy

balance is an important tool for understanding and improving the aluminium reduction

cell technology and industrial operation practices. During the research,  it has been

realised that the industry work practices and control systems are based on many

misconceptions and poor understanding of the electrolysis process, including material

and energy balance, that lead to cell failures and efficiency losses.

    A computational application entirely based on the laws of thermodynamics was

developed and used to study the electrolysis cell energy balance behaviour. With the

help of the computational model, many findings on the cell behaviour were achieved.

Specifically, the findings are divided into averaged steady  state aspects and  cell

dynamic aspects.

      7.1  Averaged Steady State Model


        7.1.1  Complete Mass and Energy Balance versus Traditional Approach

     Many of traditional cell energy balance models underestimate the heat losses due

to material balance because the contributions of many materials are ignored. As a result,

heat dissipation to the potroom may be overestimated. Such a simplified approach can

produce wrong cell operating targets and ledge thickness predictions, also resulting in

poor cell insulation projects. In this thesis, the mass and energy balance model takes

into account all material inputs and outputs of the cell. Each contribution has been

quantified and the error present in traditional models has been evaluated.

        7.1.2  Heat Losses Related to the Gas Collection

       In the context of the aluminium reduction cell, the cell heat lost by the duct

gases represents a very important share of the cell energy consumption. Using the

modelling methodology presented in this thesis, a study of the duct flow rate influence

on cell heat balance was performed. In general, it was found that if the cell duct flow

rate is reduced, the cell heat loss is also reduced, opening the possibility for important

amounts of energy saving. However, as a drawback,  the  cell emissions capture



                                                                    194

## PDF page 225

                                                          7 - Conclusions


efficiency can be worsened. A compromise solution has to be found for the optimal cell

duct flow.

       There are cases when more cell duct heat loss is desirable: when cell current is

increased and more heat has to be extracted from the cell, the increase of the cell duct

flow rate can be applied to re-equilibrate the heat losses and ledge thickness. The

present modelling can be used to predict the proper cell duct flow adjustment.

        7.1.3  Heat Losses Related to Operational Practices

      The operational  practices influence  the  cell energy balance and introduce

materials flow  outside  the  standard  reduction  reaction  process.  In modern high

amperage smelters, this fact has to be accounted for when fine tuning electrolytic cell

efficiency. The anode change duration and dressing cover addition affect the cell heat

losses and should be minimised, as well as the carbon excesses.

       In the studied case of this thesis, cathode surface cleaning is responsible for

0.066 kWh/kg Al of energy loss. The cathode cleaning loss can be reduced by careful

covering procedures, avoiding as much as possible that cover  falls into the bath,

associated with maintaining the bath prone to good alumina dissolution, avoiding

sludge. Good alumina dissolution requires stabilised alumina concentration in space and

time combined with proper superheat. Reducing the bath exposure time is another

action to minimise energy waste.

        7.1.4  Comparisons of Energy Balance of Different Cell Technologies

      Three different  cell technologies were studied using the present modelling

methodology and the energy consumption breakdown for each of them was compared.

It was found that in general, the cell size increase is beneficial in the point of view of

improving the energy efficiency.

     When analysing the modelling results, it was demonstrated that several cell mass

streams are worthy of study and optimisation. In the studied cases, it was shown that

cathode surface cleaning can be responsible for 0.05-0.1 kWh/kg Al of energy losses.

Alumina  impurities  are  responsible  for  0.07-0.1  kWh/kg  Al  additional  energy

consumption. Gross and net carbon excesses can result in more than ~0.1 kWh/kg Al and

other anode assembly parts can cost more ~0.03 kWh/kg Al to be heated up.


                                                                    195

## PDF page 226

                                                          7 - Conclusions


      7.2  Dynamic Model


      The dynamic cell modelling is very useful for increasing the understanding of

the process and proposing actions for  its improvement and control. The industrial

aluminium electrolysis process is essentially transient, with cyclical perturbations. In

this thesis, a dynamic model that combines complete mass and energy balance with

ledge movement and thermal accumulation effects was successfully developed and

applied to theoretical studies and also to evaluate the operation of real industrial cells.

        7.2.1  Cell Thermal Inertia

       Cell thermal perturbations are typically daily based, or turn based. The thermal

delays inherent to the cell sidewall materials combined with ledge movement were

studied. It was demonstrated by the modelling results that a thermal perturbation in the

bath takes more than 24 hours to propagate and stabilise the temperature and the heat

flux at the cell external wall. This finding proves the hypothesis that the aluminium

electrolysis is never under steady state condition.

        7.2.2  Aluminium Fluoride Addition

      The impact of AlF3 additions on the cell energy balance stability was studied. It

was shown that the bath superheat presents significative perturbation after the addition

(one addition each 24h), causing melting of ledge and energy balance readjustment.

Ledge thickness and shell temperature are sensible to fluoride additions, as shown by

the results. The maintenance of constant AlF3 concentration is achievable by adding the

fluoride more frequently, divided into smaller addition dumps.

        7.2.3  Power Modulation

      The dynamic model was applied to study the impact of the power modulation on

the Hall-Héroult cell behaviour. Four different modulation functions were tested in the

model. The results showed that the linear modulation produced lower disturbance

amplitude in the cell energy balance compared with the other tested functions.

      The voltage compensation of power modulation was applied in the dynamic

model. The results identified the optimum extra voltage to be applied in order to achieve

the best compensation, in the point of view of the bath/ledge solidification front

stability. In a subsequent study, different AlF3 addition patterns were associated with the

                                                                    196

## PDF page 227

                                                          7 - Conclusions


voltage compensation of the power modulation. It was demonstrated that the thermal

equilibrium  achieved by  voltage compensation can be  destroyed by poor AlF3

corrections.

      The shell forced cooling effectiveness in helping the power modulation was

investigated. It was found by the dynamic model that the influence of the external shell

cooling on the power modulation window  is negligible. Forced cooling might be

effective in changing the cell energy balance in longer timescales or in averaged steady

state.

        7.2.4  Dynamic Modelling of Real Cells

      The dynamic response to the cell mass and energy inputs was studied using the

presented dynamic modelling methodology. Two  different  cell technologies were

analysed and compared with measured values provided by the smelters. Long periods of

process (90-120 days) were considered.

       In the 145 kA cell case, a reasonable correlation between model results and

measured temperatures  in the bath was found. The relationship between fluoride

concentration and bath temperature obtained by the simulation follows the same logic of

measured values: During high AlF3 concentration periods, bath temperature is lower and
during low AlF3 periods the bath temperature increases. Strong variations in AlF3

addition  quantities  are  observed, and  as  a  consequence,  strong  bath  chemistry

fluctuations during the simulated period are also observed.

       In the 170 kA case, the simulations showed disagreement between the calculated

bath temperatures with the respective measurements. The unexpected opposite direction

of the bath temperature evolution in time may be explained by the cell short-circuiting.

When the cell is shorting, part of energy that is supposed to be used in the reduction

reaction is generating ohmic heat inside the bath. Squeezing of ACD is a traditional

strategy to reduce cell temperature, but in the case of ACD short-circuit, the opposite

occurs. The short-circuit  is very difficult to predict and  its transient effect  is not

included in the model, explaining why the bath temperature behaviour is different from

the predictions. Nevertheless, other results obtained from the simulations are in line

with  expectations.  Fluoride  concentration  presents  inverse  correlation  with  bath

temperature as it is observed in measurements. The bath superheat is very sensitive to

the fluoride additions. In this particular cell, AlF3 additions of more than 100 kg are

                                                                    197

## PDF page 228

                                                          7 - Conclusions


reported, causing a rapid variation of the superheat from 5°C to more than 20°C. On

some occasions, the strong voltage variations produce the superheat peaks. Eventually,

when voltage is decreased, negative superheat was found in simulation results. This

would probably be manifested in the cell as sludge formation over the cathode blocks

because the alumina requires some degrees of bath superheat to dissolve.

        7.2.5  Treatment of Mass and Energy Imbalances

       In both studied technologies and probably among most of the smelters, there is a

tendency of the cell control overreaction on the measurements obtained from the

electrolysis cell. It is very difficult for the potline control staff and the implemented cell

control system to quantify the time delay of internal energy imbalances stabilisation. As

the cell behaviour is never in steady state, every time when a measurement is taken, it

corresponds to a transitory state that is trying to reach a new equilibrium. How far the

cell is from the equilibrium is very often neglected. Only by using a mass and energy

balance dynamic model  that takes  into account the ledge dynamics, the thermal

capacitance of all cell materials and also the history of measurements, it is possible to

predict the transient cell excursion path.

      The described control overreaction behaviour is clearly found in the fluoride

additions of the 170 kA cell. The control system is trying to adjust the bath temperature

by adding big amounts of AlF3, sometimes up to ~4 times the required amount for mass

balance. In a first moment, the superheat is increased and the cell starts to lose more

energy. However, after some hours there might be a new ledge profile and if the reason

for the thermal imbalance  is not found, the cell would continue to overheat, and

additionally presenting high AlF3 concentration. This procedure may induce more cell

thermal excursions instead of stabilising the cell bath chemistry and energy balance.

The correct action would treat a thermal imbalance by reducing or increasing the

thermal generation and treat a chemical imbalance with appropriate mass balance. Only

with very good measurements routine, it is possible to differentiate a thermal imbalance

from a chemical imbalance in an electrolysis cell.

        7.2.6  Using the Dynamic Modelling for Cell Failure Investigation

      The dynamic modelling was used to investigate the cell failure causes. One

particular cell was lost by the smelter due to excessive iron contamination, which is


                                                                    198

## PDF page 229

                                                          7 - Conclusions


indicative of metal penetration through lining/cathode interfaces. The postulated cause

was the total loss of the protective ledge at sidewall. When the model was run using the

referred cell input data (voltage, electrical current, AlF3 additions), total ledge meltdown

occurred some hours after a voltage peak was observed and coinciding with the day of

the increase of measured iron contamination in the metal. The hypothesis of ledge loss

and lining exposure was confirmed by the dynamic model results.

      7.3  Future Research


      The logical follow up for the dynamic modelling of the cell mass and energy

balance is to integrate the model with the reduction cell control system and the potline

control practices. Automatic and human decisions could be supported by modelling

predictions. Starting with a low level integration and, according to the learning of the

new routines and feedback, the level of integration can be elevated.

      The inclusion of some consideration on the bath short-circuiting is one important

aspect that can be improved in the dynamic modelling.  It certainly requires more

information  from  the   cell  measurements  (such  as  individual  anode  current

measurements) that indicate such occurrence, and can be interpreted accordingly by

dynamic model calculations. This feature must be included in the case of the cell control

system to be integrated with the dynamic modelling, in order to produce the correct

actions based on cell measurements interpretation.

     One of the major concerns of the modern aluminium smelting industry is the

anode  effect, a phenomenon  that lowers the  cell energy efficiency and produces

environmental harmful gases (PFC). The analysis of the individual anode effect impact

on the cell energy balance would be possible if the model is expanded in order to

consider anode effect parameters such as AE duration, frequency and voltage including

a predicted decrease in current efficiency. The mass balance would then have to include

the PFC gases as possible products of the process.

       In future power modulation studies, it would be important to search for ways of

compensating the variation in the energy inputs with some kind of modulation in energy

outputs. One suggested alternative is the study of modulation in the cell duct flow. The

model would require knowledge of the relationship between the duct flow rate and the



                                                                    199

## PDF page 230

                                                          7 - Conclusions


gas temperature achieved. This could be determined experimentally or inferred with the

help of a 3D cell top flow sub-model as presented in chapter 4.3.3.

        In the face of the studies presented in this thesis, it has been demonstrated that

the improvement of aluminium reduction cell operation continues to be a big challenge

for the primary aluminium industry. Hopefully, academic and industrial research will

work together for a  better understanding and implementation of new operational

routines strongly based on fundamentals of thermodynamics. This is the path for further

improvements on Hall-Héroult cell energy efficiency.

## PDF page 245

                                                             Annex I


    Annex I: Inputs to the Cell Mass and Energy

   Balance Software (Averaged Steady State)

      The modelling system presented in this thesis requires information regarding the

cell project and operation. The user must provide a set of data regarding: raw materials

(gross and net carbon consumption, alumina impurities), measured cell parameters

(voltage, current, CE) and the heat transfer equivalent resistances.

           Cell averaged measured data:
            o  Cell Voltage and Line Current;
            o  Current Efficiency;
            o  External voltage (busbar);
            o CVD (Cathodic Voltage Drop);
            o  Bath level and metal level targets;
            o  Cell cavity dimensions;
           Target temperature and chemical composition of bath:
            o  Al2O3
            o  AlF3
            o  CaF2
            o  MgF2 and LiF, if used.
           Average relative humidity and temperature of potroom;
           Specify the percentage of CE loss due to short-circuit.
           Composition of primary alumina:
            o % Alpha phase;
            o  %Na2O;
            o  %CaO;
            o  LOI300, LOI1000 or equivalent water content.
           Composition of secondary alumina:
            o % Alpha;
            o  %Na2O;
            o  %CaO;
            o  LOI300, LOI1000 or equivalent water content.
            o  %AlF3.
           Scrubber efficiency and cell hooding efficiency.


                                                                    215

## PDF page 246

                                                    Annex I


  Cell duct gases temperature and gas flow.
  Anode changing information:
     o  Gross Carbon;
     o  Net Carbon;
     o  Geometry and density of anodes;
     o  Geometry and weight of anode assembly parts;
     o % Sulphur of anodes;
     o  Averaged anode lifetime in the cell;
     o  Target cover thickness;
     o  Amount of cover added at each anode changing;
     o % Alumina and % crushed bath in the cover material;
     o  Open crust exposition duration;
     o  Extracted anode butt average temperature;
     o  Cover (extracted with anode butt), average temperature;
     o  Other anode assembly parts (extracted with anode butt), average
          temperature;
 Averaged bath tapping mass.
 Cathode top cleaning (pacmaning):
     o  Average mass and temperature of ―pacman‖ material removed;
  Pacman device information:
     o  Average mass of equipment in contact with bath;
     o  Average temperature before and after the cleaning procedure;
  Specify Cell Thermal Resistances:
     o  Cell bottom;
     o  Sidewall at bath and at metal levels;
     o  Endwall at bath and metal levels;
     o  Anode panel;
  Specify heat transfer coefficients at ledge:
     o  Sidewall at bath and at metal levels;
     o  Endwall at bath and metal levels;
